#6039. Trump pledges to veto 'insane' Dem bills, pinpoints issue partially to blame for Republican losses

President Donald Trump expresses surprise at the results of the 2026 midterm elections after it is projected Democrats will take control of the U.S. House and Senate on ‘FOX News Democracy 26.’

President Donald Trump vowed to veto "insane" legislation from Democrats following the party's major midterm election victories Tuesday, while acknowledging that rising gasoline prices stemming from the conflict with Iran likely contributed to Republican losses.

Trump, who posted an image of a red hat with the word "Veto" to Truth Social late Tuesday night, told Fox News Channel during live Election Night coverage that he was surprised by the GOP's performance but expressed confidence that he could work with Democrats despite their electoral gains.

"They'll send over things that are insane and I'll veto them," Trump said. "And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll go out and they'll say, ‘How do we get this stuff done?’ And we'll work together."

TRUMP CONGRATULATES DEMOCRATS AFTER DOMINANT MIDTERMS, SAYS HE LOOKS FORWARD TO WORKING WITH THEM

In this screengrab taken from President Donald Trump's Truth Social post, the president shared an image of a red "Veto" hat on Truth Social late Tuesday night after Republicans lost control of Congress to the Democrats. (Truth Social / @realDonaldTrump)

Democrats seized control of both chambers of Congress in Tuesday's midterm elections, flipping at least seven Republican-held Senate seats and more than two dozen House districts. The sweeping victories dealt a major blow to Trump and the GOP, leaving Democrats positioned to block the president's legislative agenda, reject his nominees and launch investigations into his administration during his final two years in office.

President Donald Trump stands on stage after speaking at a campaign rally at the Salina Regional Airport, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Salina, Kan. (AP Photo/Jacquelyn Martin)

The president pointed to his veto authority as a negotiating tool and revealed that several Democratic lawmakers had already reached out to him.

"You know, I have a lot of friends that are Democrats, believe it or not. I've already spoken to some of them," Trump said, adding that he had spoken with Democratic members of Congress, with others waiting to call.

The U.S. Capitol on a warm, fall day on Nov. 7, 2023, in Washington, D.C. (Kevin Carter/Getty Images)

Trump also addressed the GOP's disappointing election-night performance, suggesting that Americans were reacting more to higher gasoline prices than to the military campaign against Iran itself.

STATE OF PLAY: BLUE WAVE SWEEPS DEMOCRATS INTO POWER, WITH KEY RACES STILL HANGING IN BALANCE

"I don't think it's the war, Bret, I think it's the gasoline price," Trump told "Special Report" anchor Bret Baier. "People agree with what I'm doing on Iran, but they really probably weren't willing to do the short-term consequence of paying more for gasoline."

Trump pinpointed higher gas prices as a cause for the Republican midterms loss. (iStock)

Trump defended his administration's actions against Iran, arguing that the economic consequences were temporary and necessary to achieve longer-term national security objectives.

"I think a part of it was Iran. But at the same time, people understood the importance of what I've done with Iran," Trump said. "I think historically it's going to be much more important than this evening, because you can't let the number one state sponsor of terror have a nuclear weapon."

The president also blamed some Republican candidates for failing to energize his supporters, arguing that enthusiasm at his campaign rallies did not necessarily translate into votes for those he endorsed.

"I'm a little bit surprised. I thought they'd do better," Trump said. "We had tremendous rallies. We had about 30 of them."

Trump said some candidates had alienated his MAGA base by previously opposing him, while others simply failed to inspire voters to turn out.

'POD SAVE AMERICA' HOSTS CALL DEMOCRATIC PARTY'S MIDTERM WINS 'THE END OF THE TRUMP ERA'

"They didn't like the candidates and some of the candidates that I endorsed fought me, and they didn't like that they fought me," Trump said.

"And I wasn't on the ballot. If I was on the ballot, they would have gotten out," he added.

President Donald Trump speaks at a campaign rally at the Salina Regional Airport, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Salina, Kan. (Jacquelyn Martin/AP Photo)

Despite expressing disappointment over Republican losses, Trump praised Democrats for their performance while rejecting the suggestion that the results amounted to a personal defeat.

"I think the Democrats did a good job, I really do. I think they did a good job. They fought hard," Trump said.

"I don't like to consider it a defeat," he added. "If you're on the ballot, it's different than if you're not on the ballot."

#6036. How to Secure the Straits

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANA STROUL is Director of Research at the Washington Institute for Near East Policy. She served as U.S. Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for the Middle East from February 2021 to December 2023.

Why Iran and the Houthis Keep Winning the Waterways

Over the past month, Iran has made its most intense attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz since the United States and Israel declared war, in February. More than 100 drone and missile launches into the strait have been recorded since the war began; in October alone, a container ship and a bulk vessel sank in the waterway. Meanwhile, in the Red Sea, Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthis have sustained their campaign to throttle seaborne trade as they target Saudi Arabia more directly, seizing new territory along the Bab el Mandeb Strait.

These dual maritime crises form part of the same strategic problem—one that, despite its new intensity, has existed for years. In 2019, Iran attacked shipping across the Gulf in response to the first Trump administration’s maximum-pressure sanctions campaign; in 2023, the Houthis launched assaults in the Red Sea following Hamas’s October 7 massacre in Israel. After each of these campaigns, the United States and its partners established maritime coalitions to protect these vital seaborne trade routes. The efforts helped secure the waterways, but they never fundamentally persuaded Iran and its proxies that asymmetric attacks on shipping were an ineffective way to project power. On the contrary, such tactics have only become more appealing to Tehran.

Even now, some shipping persists through both waterways. On October 15, Lloyd’s List Intelligence reported that transit through the Bab el Mandeb Strait was up modestly from August. U.S. naval patrols—as well as creative logistical adaptation by Gulf states, including ship-to-ship transfers, have allowed some oil to move through Hormuz. But shipping still remains throttled, and international actors have not yet found a way to update their deterrence playbook. In 2019 and 2023–24, U.S., European, and Middle Eastern navies formed separate, overlapping defensive maritime coalitions to escort vessels, collect intelligence on evolving threats, and intercept missile and drone attacks. In the United States’ case in 2024, a separate multinational coalition also conducted offensive strikes in Yemen to undermine Houthi maritime capabilities. Today, governments are falling back on the same instinct—to join forces, but not all their forces. The British and the French have proposed a multinational maritime defense coalition to secure the Strait of Hormuz once the war ends, while Saudi Arabia is planning, but has not yet deployed, its own alliance to protect the Red Sea. Neither project includes the United States, which has deployed nearly one-third of its naval warships to the Middle East in an attempt to reestablish freedom of navigation, enforce a naval blockade against Iran, and support U.S. attacks on Iran.

Multiple short-term, insufficiently coordinated defensive missions organized around voluntary contributions can buy down marginal risk for shippers in the near-term but they cannot deliver long-term deterrence. Separate missions embolden Iranian and Houthi leaders, who will interpret them as half-measures that are easily strained by resource scarcity, an inability to adapt quickly to evolving threats, and the absence of real-time intelligence sharing across the two chokepoints. As crises in the Strait of Hormuz and the Red Sea become more linked amid an expanding regional war, replicating the patchwork maritime strategy that never fully worked would be a serious mistake. What is needed instead is a single, sustained multinational effort to secure both waterways that combines American military capabilities with the imprimatur of a unified coalition of Asian, European, and Middle Eastern countries. Otherwise, Iran and the Houthis will again learn that threatening global commerce in maritime chokepoints is a strategy worth repeating.

The maritime coalitions now taking shape share a troubling precedent. Twice before, international actors have mounted similar efforts, and their mixed results show why even effective defensive operations fail to prevent renewed attacks in Middle Eastern waterways. In mid-2019, after the first Trump administration launched its “maximum pressure” sanctions campaign against Iran, Tehran attacked tankers off the coast of the United Arab Emirates (UAE) and in the Gulf of Oman and seized a British-flagged oil tanker from international waters in the Strait of Hormuz. Insurance premiums spiked tenfold, and freight rates for crude-oil-carrying tankers jumped nearly 40 percent. In response, Washington established the International Maritime Security Construct (IMSC), headquartered in Bahrain; the mission sought to maintain naval vessels across key waterways in the Middle East to deter Iran and collect intelligence on Iranian maritime activities to share with commercial shippers.

By the end of 2019, Albania, Australia, Bahrain, Saudi Arabia, the UAE, and the United Kingdom had joined the IMSC. But despite Washington’s efforts to expand the coalition further, most European countries abstained from joining. They were frustrated with the Trump administration’s withdrawal from the 2015 nuclear deal with Tehran and wary that joining the IMSC—with its focus on confronting Iran—would be seen by Tehran as aggressive or an endorsement of Washington’s maximum pressure campaign. Instead, eight European countries joined a French-led mission called European Maritime Awareness in the Strait of Hormuz (EMASoH), which emphasized de-escalation, surveillance, and encouraging regional dialogue with Iran.

Four years later, when the Houthis began to threaten shipping in the Red Sea—spiking maritime insurance rates and forcing vessels to detour around the Cape of Good Hope—the United States and Europe once again organized separate responses. In December 2023, the United States launched Operational Prosperity Guardian (OPG), a naval coalition soon joined by more than 20 countries. The mission maintained four to eight warships in the Red Sea area at any given time and successfully shot down Houthi drones and missiles; within its first month, its leaders said it had facilitated safe transit for more than a thousand commercial vessels. The European Union formed its own, substantially smaller defensive maritime operation that escorted European commercial vessels through the southern Red Sea and also intercepted Houthi drone and missile attacks.

In both crises, the United States, Europe, and the Gulf states broadly agreed that Iranian and Houthi threats to freedom of navigation required a response. Because freedom of navigation was viewed as a fundamental principle of the global order, assembling large groups committed to upholding this pillar was intended to demonstrate international resolve and isolate the bad actor. But disagreement over how to confront the challenge diluted the missions. Many governments declined to join offensive strikes or coalitions explicitly directed at a state. Political divides further splintered the response, as Europe sought to put distance between itself and the United States’ Iran strategy.The 2019–24 missions achieved important tactical successes by improving maritime awareness, intercepting some attacks, and stopping a complete shutdown of shipping. But the lack of a long-term, coordinated strategy for ending Iran’s aggression weakened the response. The missions never fully persuaded shippers that these waterways were safe—especially the Red Sea—or prevented Iran and the Houthis from eventually returning to their efforts to exploit the chokepoints. According to a 2021 assessment by the Dutch government, the Netherlands assumed that Iran could differentiate between the European maritime mission and the American-led one and would be less aggressive toward European vessels, underscoring Tehran’s ability to exploit the gaps between overlapping operations and the need for a unified front.

The lessons from these missions matter enormously today. After years of shadow warfare, the United States and Israel have now fought Iran directly. In 2025, the United States and Israel also launched separate months-long campaigns against the Houthis in Yemen, following a decadelong Saudi-led effort to dislodge the rebel group. These campaigns badly battered Iran and the Houthis. But they also gave them a chance to hone their asymmetric tactics. Having survived wars intended to collapse their regimes, Iran and the Houthis have emerged more willing to hold global commerce at risk and impose costs on actors not directly involved in the wars against them. Both have learned that they do not need to fully close maritime chokepoints. Simply threatening them can change decision-making in the halls of power.

Nobody wants tolls or unilaterally imposed service payments on these waterways, proposals that Iran has tabled. Instead, to try to deal with the crises, there has been a flowering of disjointed maritime coalition planning efforts. The British-French plan resembles previous European maritime efforts, proposing a defensive multinational mission to escort vessels. It excludes the United States and will not activate until the U.S.-Israeli war with Iran ends for good—a condition that essentially guarantees its uselessness in the near term. Houthi aggression, meanwhile, has sent Saudi Arabia scrambling to secure Red Sea transit, which is increasingly vital given disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz. In late July, Riyadh announced the formation of its own defensive maritime coalition with 14 countries from across Africa and the Middle East, as well as Bangladesh and Pakistan, which will be distinct from both European efforts on Hormuz and the U.S. mission. The United States has put up its own military effort to secure Gulf waterways focused on escorting vessels through Omani waters and actively defending commercial shippers from daily Iranian attacks. Concurrently, the U.S. Navy is enforcing a naval blockade against Iranian ports and disabling tankers that ignore its warnings.

The European and Saudi plans are already behind the curve of events. Leaders across Europe and the Middle East want to differentiate their defensive maritime missions from U.S. and Israeli offensive campaigns against Iran in order to prevent escalation and protect their own forces. But the European and Saudi efforts have not moved beyond planning. The U.S. mission, for its part, is active in the wartime waterways of the Middle East and has for now successfully restored much of the prewar crude oil flows out of the region amid Iranian attacks. But eventually, the U.S. Navy will struggle to maintain its large presence in the Middle East given competing priorities and the wear and tear of continuous deployment. Iranian leaders are gambling that they can outlast the United States in will and capability, while driving wedges between Washington and its long-standing partners in Asia, Europe, and the Middle East, who have yet to meaningfully defend maritime transit. These coalitions will all fail to deter Iran and the Houthis for good if they do not coordinate with each other.

Without American naval capabilities and intelligence, maritime missions to secure these waterways simply cannot succeed given the scope and scale of the threat. (In 2019, the European mission was only able to field a maximum of three warships and a single patrol aircraft, compared with the United States’ dozen ships; the United States’ 2023–24 OPG mission was able to respond thanks to its naval bases nearby in Bahrain.) Yet U.S. naval superiority itself is insufficient. Iran and the Houthis have seen this rush to uncoordinated maritime coalitions before, and they know how to exploit the political and operational gaps. Truly isolating Iran is the best way to win concessions from its leaders. Both it and the Houthis have little reason to abandon their tactics if governments cannot agree on how to confront them.

European and Saudi leaders need to merge their maritime planning efforts and invite the United States to join. Whatever serious disagreements they have with Washington about its military actions in Iran must be set aside when it comes to confronting the throttling of international waterways. An Arab- and European-led command structure, supported by American military capabilities, would demonstrate broad international opposition to maritime aggression while distinguishing the mission from the U.S.-Israeli war on Iranian territory.

For now, the United States should be prepared to contribute significant military capacity and operational support, given the extensive deployments it already has in the Middle East. Its partners across Asia, Europe, and the Middle East should redirect the resources they pledged to the European and Saudi missions to a broader coalition that includes the United States. And Washington must lean into a role as military integrator, smoothing out communication and coordination at sea for navies not used to dynamic multimember missions. In particular, the United States and Israel must share real-time intelligence about the evolving nature of Iranian and Houthi targeting capabilities and weapons, so that maritime coalition members can get ahead of the innovation curve.

In the near term, the United States should commit to operational flexibility, seconding any offensive strikes to a separate chain of command. Leaders in Tehran will take notice of a unified international response to Iranian and Houthi maritime aggression and will think twice before attacking a mission made up of dozens of navies all working together. In return, Washington must accept that its partners would be joining a defensive maritime mission—not enforcing the naval blockade or entering the war against Iran. In 2024, Washington took care to differentiate its role in the OPG defensive Red Sea mission from the separate multinational offensive strikes it undertook against the Houthis. The U.S. government should replicate that model today.

Other militaries, including Israel’s, should contribute intelligence, sea drones and other unmanned capabilities,maintenance and logistical support, and personnel. In return for the United States serving as the mission’s backbone, European, Middle Eastern, and Asian partners should commit to gradually expanding their own naval capabilities and assets so the coalition becomes a long-term investment with equitably shared burdens. Joint messaging—regular coordinated statements and outreach to commercial shippers to counter Iranian and Houthi disinformation—will be vital, as well as collaborative innovation. Iran and the Houthis adapt rapidly; it will be easier for coalition countries’ defense industries to stay ahead of them if they work together.

Military efforts to date have failed to compel either Iran or the Houthis to change course at sea. In fact, Iranian and Houthi leaders believe that they have more to gain from escalation, as evidenced by their statements and continued attacks. Countries everywhere are suffering for it. In October, the World Bank projected that Gulf economies would contract by 4.3 percent in 2026, thanks in no small part to the closure of the Strait of Hormuz and Iranian attacks on energy infrastructure. In June, the International Monetary Fund estimated that the war in Iran could reduce growth in the euro area by 0.5 percentage points in 2026.

A unified Middle East maritime coalition on its own would neither reverse these trends nor fully restore freedom of navigation. But it would be an important first step in denying Tehran opportunities to drive wedges between long-standing allies and partners. If, instead, the current trajectory of political discord, piecemeal missions, and short-term adaptation by commercial shippers continues, Iran and the Houthis will learn that they can exploit divisions among foreign governments, selectively escalating maritime threats to extract concessions. And other U.S. adversaries will learn the same lesson—that economic disruption is an extraordinarily effective weapon when the world lacks the will to defend its commons.

How the Iran War Will Transform America’s Military Role

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

#6032. The Backdoor to American Innovation

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

SAM MULLINS is a Professor at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

MICHAEL MAYO is Special Agent and Division Chief for Pacific Operations with the U.S. Naval Criminal Investigative Service and Fellow at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

NIEL KANESHIRO is a Professor at the Daniel K. Inouye Asia-Pacific Center for Security Studies.

How Military Partnerships Make Washington Vulnerable to Its Adversaries

Sam Mullins, Michael Mayo, and Niel Kaneshiro

In late May, a shipment of F-35 stealth fighter jet parts heading for the United States from Australia was misrouted to Hong Kong and into Chinese hands. Since 2007, Chinese intelligence operatives and their proxies had been stealing data to do with the F-35. Now, some of the jet’s closely guarded components, including a cockpit canopy and weapons-bay door, had landed on Chinese soil—handing Beijing stealth technology that only the United States and its allies have access to. China will surely attempt to reverse-engineer the technologies it was lucky to acquire. U.S. officials do not believe there was foul play here and they think the delivery was likely an accident: the result of a shipping delay and a missed email. But even without active foreign interference, the mere logistics of coordinating F-35 production and delivery among Washington, Canberra, Lockheed Martin, and a third-party shipping company exposed defense secrets to the greatest U.S. rival.

The U.S. defense industrial base—the network of domestic and international companies and organizations responsible for the research, production, delivery, and maintenance that undergird American military operations—is growing at a pace not seen in decades, as Washington seeks to place arms manufacturing back on a “wartime footing,” in the words of U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. The United States is working with allies, notably in the Indo-Pacific, to expand military production and make supply chains resilient to shocks. South Korea has begun performing maintenance and repairs on U.S. naval vessels, Japan is set to quadruple its production of vital antiballistic missiles, and American Precision Strike Missiles will soon be rolling off assembly lines in Australia.

Although this expansion is sensible and necessary, it also carries significant risks. As the U.S. defense base spreads, it becomes more exposed to espionage, theft, and sabotage.

Since the Cold War, U.S. adversaries have targeted the research and infrastructure that give the United States its military advantage. Now, the likes of Russia, China, and North Korea can also learn about American capabilities by mounting espionage campaigns against U.S. allies. Because the U.S. military is more dependent on supply chains that snake around the world, it has become more vulnerable to the disruptive actions of hostile powers.

If the United States fails to safeguard its deepening military partnerships, adversaries will have more opportunities than ever before to at once undermine American defenses and improve their own militaries. Washington must help its partners raise their security standards, share intelligence and best practices more widely, and fortify monitoring programs. If pursued judiciously, this emphasis on security will not hinder cooperation between the United States and its allies but rather strengthen it. But if the United States does not address this threat, one of the country’s great advantages will become a perilous weakness.

In the 1990s, the United States began working more closely with allies to bolster defense production and supply chains amid post–Cold War cuts to military spending. Washington established the National Technology and Industrial Base in 1993, a policy framework that institutionalized cooperation between U.S. and Canadian defense companies. In the following decades, the United Kingdom, Australia, and New Zealand were added to the agreement. But until recently, a number of legal, bureaucratic, and cultural barriers among the five countries hampered this partnership, keeping it from fulfilling its potential.

Over the last five years, however, Washington’s efforts to expand its defense base have begun to speed up. In 2021, the trilateral AUKUS security pact between the United States, the United Kingdom, and Australia—in which the three countries pledged to work together to produce advanced military technologies, including nuclear-powered submarines—was a milestone for defense industrial cooperation. In the 2023 National Defense Industrial Strategy, Washington outlined its commitment to further collaboration with allies, arguing that “the global activity of pacing threats increasingly requires a global approach to defense industrial relationships, concerns, and competition.”

The next year, the United States helped launch the Partnership for Indo-Pacific Industrial Resilience, a forum that brings together some Western and Asian countries to coordinate defense manufacturing and protect important supply chains. Washington has supplemented these multilateral initiatives with bilateral defense production agreements, including pacts with Australia, Japan, South Korea, and Taiwan to partner on critical defense systems, missile production, and shipbuilding. The United States is also planning to accelerate collaboration with India, Indonesia, and the Philippines. Washington has a growing appetite for defense industrial cooperation in the Indo-Pacific, and its partnerships there can thrive—as long as they can be adequately secured.

States have long used espionage to gauge enemy weaknesses and steal cutting-edge technologies. They have also executed sabotage operations to disrupt opponents’ supply chains and hinder their battlefield performance. The Soviet Union, for instance, gained nuclear weapons in part by penetrating the Manhattan Project; more recently, China stole secrets related to the F-22 and F-35 stealth fighter jets and the C-17 cargo plane. Galloping advances in technology invite subterfuge and theft. New breakthroughs in artificial intelligence and quantum computing could have strategic implications on par with the atomic bomb, and U.S. rivals are tenacious in seeking to acquire such technologies themselves.

Today, the American defense base is “under a state of constant, multivector siege” in cyberspace, according to a February report by the Google Threat Intelligence Group. U.S. adversaries are working to steal sensitive information, trade secrets, and intellectual property at every possible level, from recruiting individual employees to hacking manufacturers’ IT networks.

In addition to staging digital attacks, foreign governments such as China and North Korea are cultivating sources inside firms and research institutions focused on defense and dual-use innovation. Governments hostile to the United States, notably China, have sought to establish seemingly legitimate business and academic relationships to gain access to proprietary information. A September 2025 report from the U.S. House Select Committee on China found more than 100 academic partnerships between American universities and Chinese entities that pose a national security risk. State-sponsored actors have also embedded themselves inside American firms and research departments; for instance, North Korean remote IT workers in 2024 infiltrated a U.S. defense contractor and stole data about export-controlled technology. Perhaps most strikingly, U.S. adversaries have used foreign investment, mergers, and even the acquisition of entire companies—sometimes using subsidiaries to mask their involvement—to gain control of critical dual-use technologies. The Times reported that the 2008 buyout of the British-based Dynex Semiconductor by a subsidiary of a Chinese state-owned railway company, for example, gave the Chinese navy access to technology crucial to developing rail guns and electromagnetic launch systems for aircraft carriers.

As the United States has deepened its defense cooperation efforts in the Indo-Pacific, China and North Korea have increasingly targeted U.S. allies in the region. In 2024, South Korea’s National Police Agency revealed that North Korean hackers had mounted all-out cyberattacks against South Korean defense companies for over a year, breaching their networks and stealing technical data. Last January, the agency reported a 40 percent increase in illegal technology transfer cases—that is, of insiders leaking proprietary information on critical technologies to do with semiconductors, batteries, and shipbuilding. Many of those leaks were connected to China.

Japan, which the KGB nicknamed “spy heaven” during the Cold War, remains a hub for espionage. A July New York Times investigation found that members of a Tokyo-based Russian military intelligence unit called the 20th Directorate have spent years posing as diplomats and businesspeople to help Moscow acquire defense technologies that it is banned from importing. This equipment—which includes Japanese components used to guide Russian Kh-101 cruise missiles—has been essential to Russia’s war effort in Ukraine. From 2019 to 2024, the Chinese hacking group MirrorFace conducted over 200 cyberattacks against Japanese defense, national security, and advanced technology targets. And in February 2025, the Japanese military discovered that some of its members had unknowingly circulated for over a year USB sticks that carried malware used by Chinese hacker groups.

In Australia, U.S. adversaries have worked to infiltrate AUKUS in a number of ways. Last year, the Iranian-backed Cyber Toufan hacker group mounted a series of cyberattacks on Israeli defense contractors that gave it access to sensitive material on Australian weapons programs, including the multibillion-dollar Redback infantry fighting vehicle. Mike Burgess, the director general of Australia’s domestic intelligence service, has repeatedly warned that Australia has become a priority espionage mark where “defense personnel are being targeted in person and online.” In 2024, Burgess revealed that a spy ring run by an unspecified foreign government, referred to as “the A-team,” had cultivated and recruited a former Australian politician to assist them. In one scheme, the A-team invited Australian academics and political figures to an all-expenses-paid overseas conference to build intelligence relationships—a tactic often associated with China. The group also offered to pay Australian defense sector employees for information on AUKUS and sensitive technologies such as submarines and missile systems.

American rivals also use sabotage to weaken the broader U.S. defense base. Since 2022, Russian military intelligence has targeted European weapons manufacturers, transportation, and logistics. Moscow even planned to assassinate Armin Papperger, the CEO of the German defense giant Rheinmetall, to undermine the continent’s support for Ukraine. American security services worry adversaries may introduce malicious products into U.S. and allies’ supply chains to steal or alter data, disrupt operations, or interrupt communications. In a wartime scenario, when the defense industrial base is needed most, compromising American production lines or weapons systems could provide opponents with enormous advantages.

The United States and its allies have systems in place to protect against spying and sabotage, but these systems are not always successful. Within the United States, the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency is the lead organization for implementing the National Industrial Security Program, which protects the domestic defense industry by governing security clearances, cybersecurity safeguards, and the detection of espionage. But even domestically, many of the most vulnerable parts of the military supply chain—notably, smaller companies that provide specialized software, parts, or services—operate beyond the government’s field of vision and often cannot afford to install sufficient cybersecurity protections. Outside the United States, these problems are magnified.

Although bilateral security agreements establish standards for safeguarding classified military and industrial information, U.S. partners still have vastly different legal regimes, security cultures, and capabilities. Australia is arguably Washington’s most capable Indo-Pacific ally: it is part of the Five Eyes Alliance, an Anglosphere intelligence-sharing agreement; works closely with the American and British militaries through AUKUS; and has a robust, albeit relatively new program focused on securing the defense industry. Still, Burgess has cautioned that the country faces “unprecedented levels of espionage” that will only increase as AUKUS collaboration grows.

South Korea and Japan, while both technologically advanced and capable U.S. partners, have long-standing legal and counterintelligence vulnerabilities that they are only belatedly addressing. Until South Korea passed an expanded espionage statute last February, a loophole in the country’s law meant its government could generally bring espionage charges only in cases related to North Korea, forcing it to rely on different laws to prosecute spies from other countries. As a result, South Korean security services have long been operating with one hand tied behind their back in dealing with Chinese subterfuge; most Chinese nationals caught filming sensitive installations in recent years, for instance, have faced little or no punishment. For its part, Japanese law still does not explicitly prohibit individuals from serving as clandestine agents for a foreign power or accepting payment from a foreign intelligence service, creating a permissive environment for foreign spies to operate in Japan.

Japan is aware of these problems and is reforming its security architecture: Tokyo recently established two bodies to oversee its intelligence agencies and is pursuing a new “anti-spy” law that will finally give authorities an appropriate set of tools to counter espionage. Still, these measures face domestic opposition and will take time to come into effect. Although American allies and partners in the Indo-Pacific are making meaningful progress, regional security standards still lag behind those of the United States.

The United States needs its Indo-Pacific allies to support its military goals in the coming years. But weaknesses in one country’s security architecture create vulnerabilities for all. Washington must help its allies better protect their defense industries. Although the United States has acknowledged this need before, it has not treated it with sufficient urgency and has focused too narrowly on cybersecurity. To be sure, absolute security is impossible, and too rigidly enforcing U.S. standards on allies would slow technological cooperation. Still, the United States must ensure that allies’ security programs are comparable to its own and that any glaring gaps are filled as swiftly as possible.

To launch these efforts, the United States should develop a comprehensive grading system for defense industrial security—a framework that assesses allies’ ability to safeguard critical technologies and identifies areas for improvement. The State Department already uses such a system to evaluate global efforts to counter narcotics and human trafficking. A grading system for defense industrial security would gauge the strength of the entirety of allies’ security apparatuses—including the efficacy of their legal frameworks, cybersecurity, personnel vetting, protection of classified information, foreign investment screening, and supply-chain security—and their resilience against foreign interference and espionage.

At the operational level, Washington should expand existing programs that oversee international defense industrial security. Counterintelligence special agents from organizations such as the Naval Criminal Investigative Service and the Air Force Office of Special Investigations already enforce security standards at home and abroad by guarding critical infrastructure, strengthening cybersecurity, and neutralizing foreign intelligence threats in collaboration with host-country partners. As the defense industrial base expands internationally, more resources will be needed to sustain these operations at scale and ensure that security standards keep pace with joint manufacturing and innovation.

The United States should also expand its information-sharing efforts to alert allies about potential threats and vulnerabilities. Foreign partners currently have limited access, for instance, to Department of Defense cybersecurity information-sharing programs. The United States should consider allowing international defense organizations to access these programs, or else work with allies to develop similar, multilateral information-sharing platforms focused on securing defense production.

Finally, Washington should use the Partnership for Indo-Pacific Industrial Resilience to develop a work stream dedicated to defense industrial security, which would provide a forum for regional partners to establish common security standards and share intelligence best practices. Although nonbinding, policy recommendations from this collaboration would provide authoritative guidance on how to strengthen defense industrial security.

The F-35 parts debacle is a reminder of the inherent vulnerabilities of globalized supply chains, which U.S. adversaries are increasingly eager to exploit. The United States must rely on international partnerships to revitalize its defense industrial base—but true burden sharing is possible only if U.S. partners can keep critical technologies secure. The United States and its allies must strive to stiffen the security of their military research, production, and supply chains. Only with those protections can they realize the gains that come from working together.

#6027. Only the Gulf Can End the Iran War

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ALLISON MINOR is a Senior Fellow in the Middle East Program at the Center for Strategic and International Studies and served as Deputy U.S. Special Envoy for Yemen from 2021 to 2023.

NATE SWANSON is a Resident Senior Fellow and Director of the Iran Strategy Project at the Atlantic Council. He served as Director for Iran at the National Security Council from 2022 to 2025. In the spring and summer of 2025, he served on the Trump administration’s Iran negotiating team.

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

For everyone involved, the war in Iran has been a strategic failure. The United States and Israel have shown that they can achieve a few military objectives, such as sinking Iran’s navy. But after eight months of fighting and pressure, they have also proved that they cannot forcibly realize their main ambitions, including regime change, destroying Iran’s nuclear program, or even reopening the Strait of Hormuz—a waterway that was not closed when the war began. The Islamic Republic, for its part, has demonstrated that it can survive intense bombings. Yet it cannot break the American military’s economically suffocating blockade and has thus far failed to achieve its goal of putting an end to indefinite threats of war against Iran.

In theory, the war’s descent into a painful stalemate should open the door to diplomatic outcomes. But both sides have proved incapable of making the concessions necessary to strike a deal. Instead, they appear to prefer a mutually destructive status quo that maintains the delusion of achievable victory and which they can justify with the continued pain they inflict on their adversaries. As a result, rather than moving toward a settlement, both sides are threatening renewed attacks.

But there is a face-saving way for Iran and the United States to extricate themselves from this conflict: a cease-fire brokered and managed by the Gulf Arab states. The deal would begin with Gulf countries providing economic relief to Iran in exchange for Iran lifting its restrictions on the Strait of Hormuz. This would, in turn, allow the United States to lift its own blockade. The Gulf states would also need to commit to taking steps to deter Washington from resuming strikes on Iran in the future. But neither the United States nor Iran would need to make direct concessions to the other, and each would avoid the most painful compromises. The United States, for example, would not have to lift its “maximum pressure” campaign, and Iran would not have to agree to limit its nuclear program. Gulf states could then use their leverage to sustain the cease-fire until conditions permit a more comprehensive agreement.

Serving as the guarantor of a cease-fire comes with real costs and risks for the Gulf, but its countries have good reason to accept them. They are part of the battlefield on which the Iran war is being fought, and should the conflict continue indefinitely, it could pose a threat to the very economic and political models on which they are built. The Gulf depends on oil and gas exports to sustain itself, and there is no way it can completely bypass the Strait of Hormuz in shipping its energy resources abroad. Its efforts to diversify away from hydrocarbons, meanwhile, are also vulnerable to renewed attacks from Iran.

Securing a cease-fire will not be easy. It requires that the Gulf countries overcome their own lack of cohesion, that Iran prioritize economic recovery and forgo explicit guarantees about U.S. behavior, and that the United States gives Gulf countries the space necessary to reach a deal. Even if successful, the resulting agreement will not address the underlying issues driving the war. But with scant alternatives on the horizon, it is the best—and perhaps the only—way to ease the pain for everyone.

In theory, a U.S.-Iranian agreement should be straightforward. There is a historical template for deals between Iran and the United States—Tehran agrees to constrain or give up a specific behavior, and Washington agrees to give it money and recognition—that could apply neatly to the current conflict. In the Algiers Accords of 1981, for instance, Iran agreed to release American diplomats it held hostage in exchange for frozen funds and a commitment by the United States not to intervene in Iran’s internal affairs. In the 2015 nuclear deal, Tehran accepted strict limits on its nuclear program in return for sanctions relief and an implicit acceptance of its right to enrich uranium. Today, Iran could stop impeding maritime traffic through the Strait of Hormuz (and perhaps eventually make concessions on its nuclear program), and Washington could grant it some combination of sanctions relief, cash, and acknowledgment of Tehran having a formal role in the waterway.

In practice, however, reaching this understanding will be extremely difficult. One reason is technical: Iran won’t make the nuclear concessions that U.S. President Donald Trump wants, and the United States will not provide the upfront, irreversible financial incentives that Tehran demands. But the bigger problem is reputational. To justify his quixotic war and decision to withdraw from the 2015 nuclear deal, Trump wants sweeping concessions from Tehran. Iran, for its part, is loath to settle for fewer benefits than it received from the United States in their ill-fated June memorandum of understanding, in which Washington pledged significant relief. As a result, both sides feel compelled to sustain the confrontation, even though doing so is mutually detrimental.

The Gulf states cannot resolve the dispute over Iran’s nuclear program. But they can bring about relative peace by helping address other essential issues. The United States may not be willing to pay Iran for merely opening the Strait of Hormuz, but its Gulf partners have the wealth and the motivation to offer the regime immediate economic concessions. Those partners could, for instance, give Iran some form of humanitarian and reconstruction funding. Iran, in turn, would stop attacks on ships transiting the strait and on Gulf states themselves. In response to Iran ceasing its attacks on commerce, the United States would lift its own blockade, and the Gulf states and Iran would establish a temporary deconfliction mechanism for shipping that acknowledges Iran’s role in the waterway without establishing unacceptable precedents for international commerce. This mechanism could require that ships use Iran’s preferred traffic pattern but would not formalize Iranian control over the passage.

This particular arrangement would not resolve Iran’s other core objective—prohibiting the United States and Israel from launching a renewed military campaign. This concern is harder to address. The United States may lack a credible military endgame, and the war might be unpopular domestically, but that has not stopped an erratic Trump from drawing up plans for renewed military action. But the Gulf states can provide Iran with assurances that they would use their leverage with Washington to help deter future attacks. For example, in May, Kuwait and Saudi Arabia temporarily paused U.S. access, basing, and overflight—the approvals that allow the United States military to use Gulf countries’ territory and airspace—because of concerns about the United States’ plans for escorting merchant ships through the Strait of Hormuz. The tactic worked: the United States paused the operation.

The United States might bristle at handing Iran negotiations over to the Gulf countries. But ultimately, U.S. officials should be able to spin this deal as a win. Trump, for instance, could exit the war while telling Americans that he saved the world from high energy prices without making the kind of economic and nuclear concessions to Iran that the Obama administration did under the 2015 agreement. He could likewise boast about the steps he took to weaken Iran, and, in the absence of a nuclear deal, point out that his self-proclaimed “maximum pressure” sanctions remain in place. In fact, in some ways, a Gulf-brokered deal is exactly the kind of “America first” foreign policy Trump promised. It would let the region take on the burden of managing a bad actor, including responsibility for providing most of the necessary concessions. Trump would have stumbled into the outcome. But he would still leave office with an empowered set of allies more capable of managing their own security.

Iran, for its part, would not only get significant economic benefits. It might also advance its goal of reducing the United States’ influence in the region. After all, if Israel or the United States undermines an arrangement down the road, it will only accelerate the decline of Washington’s credibility and influence with its Gulf partners. The United States has already placed its relationship with these countries under stress by going to war with Iran, something they did not ask for and for which all have suffered serious consequences. A Gulf state deal would thus allow Tehran to leverage any future U.S. military campaigns against Iran to further drive a wedge between the United States and its regional partners.

Iran has repeatedly made clear that its top war-related priority is to end the war on terms that reduce the likelihood of a future conflict. To some extent, Iran has already achieved this benchmark with the pain it has inflicted on the global economy. Assuming this war eventually ends, the next time an Israeli prime minister asks an American president to approve an attack on Iran, that president will have to weigh global economic instability against Israeli security interests. The threat posed by Iran, in the absence of a nuclear breakout or massive terrorist attack, may not outweigh the risk to the global economy. Israel might act independently of the United States, but doing so would risk further damaging the U.S.-Israeli relationship and invite Iranian retaliation without American protection.

But this deterrent is effective only once the current iteration of the war ends. If the war continues, Iran will forfeit the future de facto security guarantee that the Strait of Hormuz and geography provide. The longer this conflict goes on, the more it favors those in Israel or the United States advocating for regime change or endless war. Unless there is a solution to high energy prices that allows Trump to disengage from Iran, he will continue grasping, and it could eventually lead him to adopt aggressive kinetic options that have not yet been tried—and for good reason.

Iran and the Gulf countries are already exploring regionally led solutions to the conflict. On October 26, Saudi officials traveled to Tehran to meet with their Iranian counterparts, alongside leaders from Pakistan and Turkey. Gulf countries had also planned to meet with Iran in Salalah, Oman, on September 14 to discuss the Strait of Hormuz. But that gathering never happened, and unless there is a broader regional solution, Gulf countries will instead mostly pursue private, bilateral deals with Iran to help forestall future attacks on their ships or territory. These agreements might help lessen the pain for individual Gulf countries in the short term, but they fail to address the overall risk of Iranian escalation or resolve the global consequences stemming from disputes related to the Strait of Hormuz. Furthermore, they allow Iran to stoke division and distrust between Gulf countries while driving up its own demands.

To get a workable deal, the Gulf states will thus need to improve internal cohesion. This is easier said than done, particularly in light of continued tensions between two of the major Gulf players—Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates. But fixing this issue is wholly within the power of Gulf states, and the Salalah effort demonstrates there is some interest in doing so. Improving cohesion among Gulf states also does not require resolving the larger bilateral tensions and competition among the region’s capitals; it only requires an agreement to isolate the Iran issue from those tensions and address this shared imperative. The Gulf states must therefore collectively agree on redlines for Iranian behavior and coordinate on how to enforce them. If Iran seeks to test the agreement or demand more concessions from Gulf governments, they must collectively push back by pausing economic aid or cooperation—even if it means risking Iranian retaliation.

The Gulf states and Iran will also be tested by the Houthis. Saudi Arabia understandably holds Iran accountable for Houthi attacks on its territory because Iran helped build the group’s capabilities, continues to provide it with significant technical and material support, and helped trigger the re-escalation of the Yemen conflict in July. But Iran does not control the Houthis, so the group is not going to stop attacking Saudi Arabia or call off its campaign inside Yemen simply because Iran says to. Tehran will thus need to extend an olive branch to Riyadh by showing that it is willing to limit its direct involvement in the current Houthi attacks against Saudi Arabia. For instance, Iran could credibly promise Riyadh that Tehran-backed Iraqi militias will stop attacking the kingdom. It was, after all, Iraqi militia attacks that took the kingdom’s valuable East-West oil pipeline offline on September 10, and defending against attacks from both the north (where Saudi Arabia borders Iraq) and south (where it borders Yemen) greatly complicates Riyadh’s already strained air defenses.

These efforts to craft a deal might still come up short. Even if the Gulf states overcome their internal divisions and sideline concerns about Houthi activity, Tehran and Washington could refuse to sign on. Iran could conclude that control of the strait and the ability to throttle energy markets are its best security guarantee, whereas the United States might struggle to even implicitly acknowledge that the war will not achieve the outcome its leaders promised. And even if the cease-fire agreement does work, the result will be far from ideal, for both the United States and its Gulf partners. It could encourage Iran to increase its control over the strait, and it could weaken U.S.–Gulf state partnerships. Worse yet, it fails to address the underlying issues driving the U.S.-Iranian conflict and the tensions between Iran and its neighbors: Tehran’s nuclear program and support for armed groups, respectively.

But a comprehensive Iran deal is not feasible today. A Gulf-brokered outcome on the Strait of Hormuz is. In fact, it may be the only way to stop a mutually hurting stalemate that is poised to last years and cause mounting damage to global markets. The strategies that states have employed to blunt the consequences of global oil and gas shortages will likely start wearing thin in 2027, as governments deplete their strategic reserves, Chinese demand increases, and alternative supply routes out of the Gulf come under Iranian attack. The economic consequences of the conflict could even undermine the domestic stability of the most vulnerable Gulf states or reignite old conflicts, as happened in Yemen this year. A Gulf-brokered deal, then, is the least bad outcome. Such is the irony of this conflict: the bystanders that have already lost the most will need to save the warring parties from themselves.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

#6024. Bonta says he'll counter DOJ election monitors with observers of his own

Acting Deputy Attorney General Trent McCotter discusses the DOJ lawsuit against California over voter rolls. McCotter details efforts to crack down on noncitizens registered to vote and ensure election integrity.

California Attorney General Rob Bonta said Tuesday afternoon that his office will deploy election observers in response to concerns that Justice Department monitors could intimidate voters.

Bonta said California was taking the same approach it used during the previous two elections, when the state dispatched its own observers alongside federal monitors.

The DOJ's Civil Rights Division has sent 1,000 monitors across the country to ensure election integrity and protect Americans' right to vote, a plan that has been public knowledge for months. The monitors will be personnel from across the DOJ, U.S. Attorney's Offices and employees from the Office of Personnel Management.

The department confirmed Tuesday that while federal election monitoring is routine, this year's operation is the largest in its history, with monitors deployed to all 50 states for the first time.

California Attorney General Rob Bonta speaks as Gov. Gavin Newsom listens during a news conference at Gemperle Orchard on April 16, 2025, in Ceres, California. (Justin Sullivan/Getty Images)

DOJ FIRES BACK AT DEM AG OVER ELECTION DAY GUIDANCE TELLING OFFICIALS WHEN TO CALL COPS ON FEDERAL AGENTS

The DOJ also released a list of cities where monitors are being deployed. In California, they are being sent to Los Angeles, Alameda, Sacramento and San Diego.

Bonta and other critics have argued that the unusually large federal presence could spook voters or discourage some from casting ballots.

Assistant Attorney General Harmeet Dhillon, who leads the Civil Rights Division, told Bloomberg in an August interview that the role of DOJ election monitors would be to observe election practices and pursue investigations into any possible instances of fraud after the fact — not to intervene directly while people are voting.

In Tuesday's announcement, the DOJ added that monitors will also be responsible for ensuring that polling places are accessible for those with disabilities.

Harmeet Dhillon, head of the Justice Department's Civil Rights Division, during an interview in Washington, D.C., on Aug. 17, 2026. (Al Drago/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

DOJ OFFICIAL DEFENDS TRUMP'S NONCITIZEN VOTER PROBE ON '60 MINUTES,' ACCUSES SHOW OF EDITING OUT HER ANSWERS

In her Bloomberg interview, Dhillon said the election monitors would respect the laws of whichever states they end up in. Many states restrict monitors from entering polling places and require them to remain behind designated boundaries outside.

"We respect state law," Dhillon told Bloomberg at the time. "We reach out to the states. If their perimeter is 150 yards, we respect that."

Dhillon also pointed out that the DOJ sent more than 80 monitors to observe primary elections across seven states this year, including locations in Minnesota, Michigan, Florida, Wyoming, South Carolina, Massachusetts and New Hampshire.

People vote at a polling location on Election Day at Boyle Heights City Hall in Los Angeles, California, on Nov. 3, 2026. (Frederic J. Brown/AFP via Getty Images)

The nationwide deployment appeared to be proceeding without major incident Tuesday afternoon, with no significant confrontations involving federal monitors publicly reported.

This comes after the Supreme Court on Monday rejected the Trump administration's effort to require California election officials to segregate ballots cast by 20,000 voters the DOJ believes are noncitizens.

#6023. Don’t Go Backward on China Policy

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ALI WYNE is Senior Research and Advocacy Adviser for U.S.-China Relations at the International Crisis Group.

RYAN HASS is Director of the John L. Thornton China Center and Chen-Fu and Cecilia Yen Koo Chair in Taiwan Studies at the Brookings Institution.

Nearly halfway through his second term in office, U.S. President Donald Trump has undercut both the United States’ capacity to protect its interests related to China and its role as a provider of regional security in Asia. His undisciplined approach to dealing with Beijing, in which he has focused on building warm personal ties with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and discounted or ignored long-standing sources of tension between the two countries, has reduced Washington’s leverage to shape the relationship. At the same time, Trump’s sustained attacks on allies and partners have driven them to hedge against the United States and strengthen their ties with China. Beijing, in turn, has successfully cast itself as a steady counterweight to an erratic Washington: in 25 of 36 countries that the Pew Research Center surveyed in the spring of 2026—among them Australia, Canada, France, Germany, and Mexico—more respondents had a favorable view of China than of the United States.

Given the failures of Trump’s second-term strategy, many Democrats and even moderate Republicans may try to strike more hawkish postures on China after the midterm elections and during the 2028 presidential campaign. This temptation is understandable: there is a strong desire to provide a contrast to a flailing approach and correct what Trump has gotten wrong. But succumbing to it would be dangerous. If Congress and future presidential candidates reflexively adopt a more aggressive stance toward China, they could both undercut a future administration’s capacity to preserve vital diplomatic space with Beijing and further disquiet allies and partners that do not want to be trapped in a new twilight struggle. Overcorrecting Trump’s China policy would also be unlikely to help candidates get elected or boost their public profile in the run-up to 2028. Recent polling shows that only a small share of Americans in seven battleground states view China as an enemy or consider it to be one of the top threats to U.S. security.

The goal for the next Congress and, eventually, Trump’s successor, should be to prioritize steady competence over performative toughness in dealing with China. Political point scoring and wistful nostalgia for strategies that discount Beijing’s mounting power, and growing domestic support in the United States for great-power calm, would compound Trump’s mistakes rather than mitigate them. A smarter, more coherent China policy—one rooted in disciplined competition with Beijing, patient efforts to build goodwill with anxious allies and partners, and vigorous efforts to refresh the domestic sources of U.S. strength—can make life safer, healthier, and more affordable for Americans.

Trump’s views on China are iconoclastic in Washington, and even at odds with other members of his own administration and party. Whereas most policymakers agree that China is the United States’ foremost strategic competitor, Trump has embraced a narrow conception of competition focused primarily on the size of China’s trade surplus and its export of chemicals used to make fentanyl. He also sees Xi less as a determined geopolitical foe than as a transactional business rival with whom he can negotiate to stabilize bilateral ties.

This judgment—that dealing with China can and should be nonideological and transactional—was also present in Trump’s first term in office. It even survived the COVID-19 pandemic, during which Trump, believing that Xi had exported the virus, empowered senior advisers to implement a hard-line approach to China that included challenging the legitimacy of Chinese Communist Party rule and openly calling for political change. But Trump’s core views did not dictate U.S. policy as greatly then as they do now because he had less command over his own administration during his first term and was less dominant over the Republican Party as a whole.

Trump has also been chastened into a more realistic appreciation of Beijing’s power. China is the only country in the world that stared down Trump’s April 2025 tariff barrage and forced him to retreat. In 2026, Beijing used Washington’s fear of a fresh economic rupture to deter Trump from targeting major Chinese banks as part of his global efforts to squeeze Iran’s economy. Even as the president claims to be ushering in a new era of U.S. dominance, he has had to acknowledge China as a “near peer”—a designation that his administration codified in its 2025 National Security Strategy. Similarly, he has touted the prospect of a “G-2” of the United States and China, making allies and partners worry that he is privileging Beijing’s sensitivities over their concerns.

In some discrete areas, the Trump administration continues to view China through a competitive lens. For example, it is reducing U.S. dependence on Chinese rare-earth exports through initiatives such as Project Vault, an effort to stockpile critical minerals, and pressuring countries to forswear participation in Chinese-led artificial intelligence initiatives. And even as Trump lambastes allies and partners, alleging that they exploit U.S. beneficence, Washington is deepening its security cooperation with treaty allies in Asia, including Australia and the Philippines, and stepping up its training of troops in Taiwan.

Yet on balance, the president has been more conciliatory than competitive toward China. And with his other foreign policy goals—dominating the Western Hemisphere, pushing for regime change in Iran, and ending the war in Ukraine—vying for priority, Trump has devoted far less attention to the world’s most consequential bilateral relationship than many observers had expected when he retook office. His various retreats on China have left the United States worse off: he has undermined Washington’s leverage over Beijing while causing U.S. allies and partners to question whether they should align with a country that prefers a veneer of stability with China to coordinated efforts to address Chinese assertiveness abroad and repression at home.

Because Trump’s China policy places him at odds with much of the foreign policy bureaucracy and the vast majority of lawmakers, including those within his own party, there will likely be pent-up pressure to resume intense strategic competition with China. Candidates seeking the 2028 presidential nomination, in particular, will be tempted to pledge to take a tougher approach to Beijing, potentially including calls to curtail high-level diplomatic exchanges between U.S. and Chinese officials or adopt more expansive measures to limit China’s technological progress. But attempting to restore or make minor updates to the status quo that existed before Trump’s second term—or to go even further in an effort to project ostensible strength—would be misguided.

Most obvious, the nearly decadelong U.S. effort to slow Chinese innovation through sanctions, tariffs, and export controls is foundering. In AI, for example, Chinese companies are finding workarounds to such efforts and advancing their capabilities alongside those of their U.S. peers. Any sustainable attempt to limit technology access also requires multilateral cooperation; as Washington’s ties with many of its closest allies and partners fray, questions are mounting about whether any coalition can thwart Beijing’s technological strides and would be willing to bear the costs of trying to do so.

At the same time, China is widening its lead in sectors such as electric vehicles, advanced robots, and life sciences. Attempting to construct a technological moat around the United States is unlikely to help it regain its competitiveness. Instead, Washington will need to strike a better balance between mitigating the security risks of Chinese investment and collaboration and securing commercial benefits from Chinese knowledge and industry. The United States could gain from using the same technology transfer practices that China employed against U.S. companies starting in the late 1970s, when Washington led in many cutting-edge technologies. In battery manufacturing, for instance, U.S. firms would benefit from acquiring know-how from their Chinese competitors, and both countries would gain if their laboratories pooled capabilities to accelerate the discovery of cures to diseases such as cancer.

The United States also may not be able to pick up where it left off before Trump retook office because even its well-wishers no longer can treat his foreign policy as an aberration. He won a second term after losing in 2020, and he did so more decisively than in 2016. Although U.S. allies and partners would welcome a more multilateralist administration after Trump, they will find it difficult to overlook the possibility that aggressive unilateralism will periodically shape Washington’s foreign policy. Merely eschewing Trump’s crude bullying will not be enough to rebuild trust; his successor must avoid treating allies and partners as instruments of strategic competition.

Paradoxically, though, Trump’s mishandling of China policy has opened the political space for a new, more durable strategy to emerge. Previously, proponents of sustained high-level diplomacy between the United States and China were often dismissed as shortsighted, Chinese investment in the United States was seen as a Trojan horse, and critiques of zero-sum conceptions of bilateral relations were considered naive. But Trump’s actions have shifted the Overton window of what is possible, creating room for a wider variety of ways to work productively with China.

Both Republican and Democratic members of Congress have gradually resumed bipartisan visits to China. Even Secretary of State Marco Rubio, one of the foremost hawks during his time in the Senate, said in September that it would be “outrageous” for top U.S. and Chinese officials to avoid each other. Respected voices in Washington, including former senior Biden administration officials, are calling for some joint ventures with Chinese companies to help U.S. companies develop. And there is a growing recognition in both Congress and the White House that China’s steady accrual of power necessitates a more focused approach to competition.

There is also public support for a new China policy. Although many policymakers frame relations with Beijing as a competition to be won, most Americans regard it as a reality to be managed. In a July 2025 survey by the Chicago Council on Global Affairs, 53 percent of Americans wanted the United States to pursue “friendly cooperation and engagement with China”; it was the first time since 2019 that a majority of respondents had expressed that view. Young people are driving this shift. A January 2026 Pew poll found that only 32 percent of Republicans and ten percent of Democrats between the ages of 18 and 49 called China an enemy, compared with 55 percent and 18 percent, respectively, of those age 50 and older. A March 2026 survey by the Searchlight Institute, a center-left think tank, found that 62 percent of respondents between 18 and 34 wanted the United States to “cooperate with China and improve U.S.-China relations.”

A better China policy will begin by abandoning hollow admonitions to “win” the strategic competition with Beijing. Although China faces numerous internal challenges, including slowing productivity, an aging population, and mounting government debt, it also has powerful advantages, two of which have become undeniable in Trump’s second term: China’s dominance of supply chains that provide vital commodities to Americans and its stranglehold on rare earths that the U.S. automotive industry and defense industrial base need to function.

The next president should neither understate nor exaggerate Beijing’s trajectory; rather, as we have previously argued, Washington should consider it “an enduring yet constrained competitor.” The inescapable reality is that neither country can deliver any knockout blows against the other without doing grievous damage to itself. Both countries will remain interdependent and have to operate with awareness of their vulnerabilities to each other.

If the world’s two foremost powers are fated to compete over the terms of coexistence, they must redouble efforts to avoid a catastrophic confrontation. Washington will have to build trust with rightfully wary allies and partners if it is to shore up deterrence in Asia. It will also have to continue signaling to Beijing a willingness to take reciprocal steps to reduce the risk of incidents that could spiral out of control, such as accidental air or naval collisions. Such signaling will become ever more essential as AI rapidly advances. Protecting the U.S. homeland against AI misuse and misalignment will require Washington to coordinate with Beijing on guardrails for frontier models and reject the argument that such efforts are inimical to securing a decisive strategic advantage over China. The need to work together on AI safety is already shaping U.S.-Chinese relations: at September’s summit between Trump and Xi, the two countries agreed to establish an incident alert system, and many members of Congress have publicly supported broader bilateral efforts with China to reduce the risks of AI-enabled security crises.

Reindustrialization will also be a major project for Trump’s successor. Progress on that front will be essential if the United States is to reduce its vulnerabilities to China and put itself on firmer footing to pursue a favorable coexistence. The next Congress and potential 2028 candidates could outline realistic plans for reducing dependence on China for active pharmaceutical ingredients, rare earths, and potential future chokepoints. They could also prioritize key industries on which U.S. security depends, such as AI-powered robotics and shipbuilding, and explain how they would deploy funding and talent to those sectors. Elsewhere, with public support for the president’s tariffs sagging, his successor could narrow or place sunset provisions on—or, through executive order, rescind—protectionist policies toward China that lower productivity and increase the costs of inputs for American producers. Such efforts would be broadly politically popular at home and would better position the United States to work with allies and partners to push back on China’s unfair economic practices, including extensive subsidies, undervalued currency, and suppressed domestic demand.

Ultimately, the United States must remember that its external competitiveness will flow from its internal vigor. Leaders in Washington should accordingly embed policy positions on China in a hopeful vision: helping the United States realize its potential as a pluralistic, self-correcting democracy. Framing U.S.-Chinese relations around the United States’ capacity to steer its ties with China in productive directions is more likely to produce a resilient approach than casting Washington as a victim or reasserting a policy framework that no longer meets the moment.

Observers can and should debate specific policy positions vigorously. But another harsh swing in the United States’ approach to China would create more policy instability while also discounting the extent to which Beijing’s rise is transforming the geopolitical balance. Taking advantage of the political space that Trump has generated, however ineptly, the new Congress taking office after the 2026 midterms and presidential aspirants looking to run in 2028 will have an opportunity to present a steadier approach, articulating a clear focus on national renewal and a balanced posture toward Beijing that reflects the mood of the American public. Approaching China’s challenge with this sensibility would not only be more prescriptively sound; it would also be more politically sustainable.
