#6019. North Korea’s Enemies Within

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

How Long Will Elites Stay Loyal to Kim Jong Un?

In 2015, the North Korean diplomat Thae Yong Ho received an unusual assignment. Kim Jong Chol, the older brother of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, was coming to London to attend an Eric Clapton concert, and Thae, who was then stationed in London, was assigned to look after him. Providing direct service to the ruling family confers honor on North Korean officials and their households, so Thae expected his two sons to be impressed.

To Thae’s surprise, his sons were outraged. Ordinary North Koreans could be expelled from university (or worse) for listening to Western music, they told him. And now the Kim family was spending thousands of dollars in a single evening to enjoy entertainment forbidden to all other North Koreans. In the eyes of his children, Thae later wrote in his memoir, Password to the Third Floor, he was a prisoner forced to take care of a prison warden. Their reaction left him ashamed.

The next year, Thae, his wife, and their sons escaped to South Korea. His defection surprised the international community. Only weeks earlier, Thae had been defending the North Korean regime with great fervor. Yet this incongruous behavior is familiar to those who understand life among North Korea’s elite: officials can profess full devotion to the Kim family right until the day they run.

The gap between public conduct and private belief is difficult to observe from the outside. For over a decade, I interviewed dozens of former diplomats, Korean Workers’ Party cadres, military officers, businesspeople, and technicians, all of whom had lived, studied, or worked in Pyongyang. These people ran the party, military, embassies, foreign-currency businesses, and technical programs on which the state depends for its survival. They received the best education, housing, food, and access to influential social networks their country could provide. Some once believed sincerely in the system; others learned early that loyalty was a role they had to pantomime in a nationwide, lifelong performance—now with Kim Jong Un as the maestro. Nearly all described an elite class under growing strain.

Since Kim Jong Un came to power in December 2011, he has tightened his grip on the country’s elites by carrying out sweeping purges of the old guard of senior party officials and military officers and executing others for minor political missteps. Many members of North Korea’s elite fear that they or their families can be killed at the drop of a hat. And their escape routes are narrowing; since Kim sealed off the borders during the COVID-19 pandemic, the number of North Korean defectors reaching South Korea has plummeted.

Those North Koreans who still manage to escape increasingly come from the elite. As of August 2024, South Korea’s National Intelligence Service reported that the number of defections from North Korea’s upper class recorded under Kim Jong Un was two and a half times the combined total observed during the reigns of Kim Il Sung (1948 to 1994) and Kim Jong Il (1994 to 2011). The absolute number remains small—in the hundreds—and it would be a mistake to interpret this trend as a harbinger of imminent regime collapse. North Korea is remarkably effective at preventing private grievances from becoming organized opposition. The regime does not need every official to believe in it. But it needs its doubters to feel isolated and alone in their skepticism and disillusionment.

North Korea has long been referred to as the “Hermit Kingdom,” but that term offers the wrong impression of the country. It is not totally isolated from the world. It is a nuclear-armed state with diplomatic relations with over 160 countries, and it has the world’s fourth-largest standing army and highly skilled cyber-operators. The vast majority of the population may be fed a robust diet of state propaganda, but most elites are exposed to life outside North Korea. They have a reasonable sense of how the world works and their country’s place in it. And yet they have to live with intense cognitive dissonance, at once understanding the real world and having to create, enforce, and trumpet the state’s official narratives to their countrymen and foreigners.

Many high-ranking North Koreans are finding it harder to sustain this balancing act. Access to privilege can purchase compliance—and terror can enforce it—but the state will not give elites what they increasingly want: guaranteed security for them and their families and a viable future for their children. Kim can allay some frustrations by doling out luxury apartments, promotions, and access to extravagant lifestyles. He cannot address the deeper sources of discontent without relaxing the very controls that protect his family’s rule from challenges to its power. The regime remains capable of suppressing any organized resistance in the country, but the growing disaffection of the elite threatens to weaken the state from within. Kim presides over a system increasingly marked by doubt, suspicion, and pervasive anxiety. When the chips are down and North Korea faces a true crisis, its most powerful leaders may not know who they can trust and where they can turn.

Most of the more than 34,000 North Koreans now living in South Korea came from the poor northern provinces along the Chinese border. Many of them fled to the south to escape famine and economic hardship. They had little access to the regime’s inner workings, even if they had tremendous experience of its cruelty and depredations. In contrast, elite escapees come from a wholly different world. They are products of Pyongyang, a capital of roughly three million that is closed to most of the country’s 25 million citizens. They grew up with relatively reliable electricity and running water, and attended schools designed to educate the next generation of regime operators. They benefited from the new construction of luxury residential districts, extravagant resort towns such as Samjiyon and Wonsan-Kalma, and the country’s sustained economic growth in the face of heavy international sanctions. North Korea’s elite serve in a range of occupations beyond just the party cadre ranks, the military, and the security services; they can be artists, software engineers, restaurant managers, and much else. The regime groomed them from birth to serve the party and the Kim family.

Consider the family of Ri Jong Ho and his son Hyunseung Lee. Ri spent three decades in Office 39, which earns and manages foreign currency for the leadership. Kim Jong Il appointed him to senior trade and economic posts, and in 2002 the state awarded him a Hero of Labor medal and a Kim Il Sung watch, prestigious honors and a recognition of his loyalty and patriotism. Ri’s son attended some of the country’s finest educational institutions, including Kumsong Academy and Pyongyang Foreign Language Institute, which prepare the children of senior cadres to enter high positions in the party. Hyunseung and his sister were also among the rare few North Koreans who were permitted to study abroad in China. By his mid-20s, Hyunseung was already a party member, working in China, and was trusted to chair a branch of the Kim Il Sung Youth League while helping facilitate millions of dollars in trade between Chinese companies and North Korea. This family faithfully served their country while living at the highest echelons of their society and enjoying the best creature comforts. But their privileged positions did not make them safe.

The shocking 2013 execution of Jang Song Thaek, Kim Jong Un’s uncle, unnerved Ri. The slain Jang had been married to Kim Il Sung’s daughter and had served all three Kims. If the regime could eliminate such a decorated official—and a member of the ruling family—and seek to erase him from the country’s history and memory, then no one was untouchable. Ri’s family escaped to South Korea from their posts in China in 2014, and Hyunseung and his sister later earned master’s degrees from Columbia University. Despite the many privileges their family received from the regime, they fled because their proximity to power made them feel deeply vulnerable.

North Koreans live under institutions designed to make independent organization impossible. Party committees operate inside every school, embassy, farm, military unit, office, and factory. Security bureaus obsessively police information flows and political conduct, while neighborhood units monitor every household. Every citizen, starting at a young age, belongs to a state-directed organization. For elites, closeness to power invites suffocating scrutiny; it places them more deeply within the grasp of the state rather than freeing them from the overbearing system.

The Organization and Guidance Department, the party’s most powerful agency, oversees appointments, inspections, and discipline everywhere—in Pyongyang as well as rural villages. No escapee is known to have come from the OGD’s central ranks, an indication of the control imposed on the country’s most influential officials. Diplomats and workers sent abroad must surrender their passports and leave relatives in North Korea as collateral to deter defection. When they come home, they must account for what they saw, heard, and did abroad, enumerate every single political sin they committed, and then undergo extensive ideological deprogramming and reprogramming.

Kim Se Yun (a pseudonym), a former senior party cadre who traveled overseas, described the monthlong central review she underwent every time she returned to Pyongyang. Officials have to “cleanse” themselves of what they have encountered and report even small details, including whether they had gone out drinking with colleagues after dinner. A late night out could require a written piece of self-flagellation to account for a lapse unbecoming of “a devoted worker for the state.” The review was meant to minimize the effects of foreign exposure: a person could see how others lived but, upon returning, had to renounce what she had seen. Kim Se Yun described the constant surveillance when abroad and at home as miserable and utterly asphyxiating.

An elite position brings goods, status, and influence denied to ordinary citizens, but it also places a person’s career, relatives, and life within reach of the leader who can destroy them at any point for any reason. As one common saying in Pyongyang has it, “The closer you are to the sun, the more likely you will burn.”

Nothing impressed this lesson upon the elite class more forcefully than the fall of Jang in 2013. His sudden arrest and execution sent lasting fear through Pyongyang. Every elite I spoke with understood the warning implicit in his killing.

Jang had already undergone “revolutionization” three times. This distinctly North Korean punishment, used especially to discipline and defenestrate high officials, strips a person of office and sends him to perform physical labor and political reeducation, often in the countryside. It humiliates an elite official in front of the people he once commanded, even if it does not foreclose the possibility of his future rehabilitation. Jang returned each time, only to be executed in the end by the third leader he served. Heo Kang Il, an elite defector who managed a North Korean restaurant in China and in 2016 brought 12 of his staff with him to South Korea, knew people subjected to revolutionization and was subjected to it once himself. He told me that many never recovered even after being rehabilitated. Some developed psychological illnesses or died within a few years from physical ailments.

When Kim Jong Un became the country’s leader in December 2011, he was in his late 20s (his exact birth year is unknown). He aggressively imposed his writ by carrying out purges of party senior cadres and military officials who had served under his two predecessors. Outside observers described his vicious consolidation of power as an attempt to compensate for his youth, his limited political experience, and his complete lack of a military background. When the COVID-19 pandemic hit, Kim Jong Un reinforced the country’s already hard borders to eliminate any unauthorized cross-border movement and passed draconian laws that further criminalized consumption of any unauthorized foreign media, all with the aim of “repurifying” the country’s socialist Korean culture. Although the pandemic has passed, the information environment is more controlled than ever before and the borders remain sealed.

In North Korea, loyalty is evaluated and documented from childhood. A person’s conduct at school; performance in political organizations; and songbun status (a hereditary classification of loyalty to the regime that breaks up North Koreans into three broad groups: “core,” “wavering,” and “hostile”), determine admission to universities, party membership, and promotion. Adults attend regular self-criticism sessions. At home, portraits of the Kims must hang spotless at the highest point of the most prominent wall, with nothing placed above them. One woman I interviewed recalled habitation inspectors making regular visits in white gloves to check the portraits for dust. She cleaned the portraits meticulously, not because she revered the men in them but because a speck of dust could endanger her family.

Even a weekly public self-criticism session, which requires a person to publicly share a political transgression they have made and then criticize a colleague, often becomes a coordinated performance. Many elite defectors told me that the day before a self-criticism session, colleagues would warn one another about what they planned to criticize at the next day’s session. The advance notice protected friendships while allowing everyone to satisfy the requirements of the political cleansing ritual. A person might confess to lacking sufficient revolutionary zeal during the previous week and vehemently rebuke a friend for a similar offense. The trick was to disclose transgressions that were sufficiently blameworthy but not serious enough to get oneself or a colleague in too much trouble.

Another former elite described the public mourning after Kim Jong Il’s death in 2011. She felt relief at the leader’s death, and no sorrow, but knew that she had to appear to cry, so she dredged up her saddest memories—those of her grandmother’s death and of a cousin who was forcibly repatriated after a failed defection—to force herself to weep. When tears still would not come, she bowed her head, dramatically shook her shoulders, and avoided eye contact with anyone in the hysterically wailing crowd. She believed that many people around her were giving the same performance.

Scholars refer to this gap as preference falsification: people publicly misrepresent their true beliefs because honesty is dangerous. In Pyongyang, preference falsification is an essential part of ordinary daily life. Elites constantly calibrate their speech and compartmentalize their innermost private thoughts. Some still believe in the system or feel genuine national pride. Others resent Kim while valuing the status his regime gives them. Belief and cynicism can exist in the same office and within the same person. This is how a senior diplomat can lecture British bureaucrats about North Korea’s victimhood one day and then flee North Korea with his entire family a few weeks later.

When elites do leave, their departures remind others who harbor private doubts that they are not alone. This is one reason Pyongyang treats prominent defections as security emergencies. When Heo (the restaurant manager) and 12 workers left a North Korean restaurant in China in 2016, the state recalled all other workers in China for interrogation. In his memoir, Thae analogized this group defection to stones falling from a castle wall: small losses can eventually lead to the collapse of the entire castle.

Nobody lightly gives up their lofty place in the pecking order of North Korean society. Kim Jong Un has not neglected his ruling class. His government has built gleaming new housing in Pyongyang for scientists, party cadres, and other favored groups, and it distributes foreign cars, watches, jewelry, food, and apartments. An elite position also provides something less tangible: a strong sense of purpose and relevance. A bureaucrat, as powerless as he may be, matters in North Korean society. People appreciate his official title, defer to his family, and recognize the heft of his social and political connections.

The prospect of losing that status can make the thought of escape unpalatable even for people who detest the regime. Over dinner at a Seoul barbecue restaurant buzzing with customers eating sizzling meat and drinking somaek, a mixture of beer and soju, a former official I call Mr. Kim recalled an incident elsewhere in which he had asked a table of loud college students to quiet down. Decades younger than him, they barely looked at him—they “didn’t even put their drinks down.” One student told him to eat somewhere else. As he recalled the humiliation, Mr. Kim removed his glasses and rubbed his eyes. In North Korea, he told me, a restaurant would have gone silent when he entered. He would have walked with his shoulders straight and head held high, knowing that his family name and job carried weight. In South Korea, where he worked a warehouse job, he was “just a mere North Korean defector with a suspicious accent.”

Mr. Kim’s experience of losing status and purpose after defection helps explain why the regime’s mixture of privilege and coercion still works. Elites know they have a lot to risk and lose, so for most aggrieved elites, the rational option is to remain in the system, perform loyalty for the rest of their lives, and extract all the advantages their positions still offer.

Yet this bargain has an expiration date. After retirement, senior officers and cadres do not receive pensions. The government takes back state-provided cars, homes, and appliances. After serving the state for decades, retired generals and senior cadres are often reduced to working as guards or market vendors. Younger elites watch this happen and accumulate money as much as possible, knowing that the state that demands their whole lives will not care for them in their old age.

Younger and middle-aged elites are also less inclined to put up with the careers and sacrifices the state has historically presented as honorable. Many interviewees told me that elite parents no longer want their sons to spend over a decade conscripted in the army or build careers entirely inside the party. They prefer business, technical work, study abroad, and amassing private wealth. They want access to the outside world and would welcome greater economic liberalization at home, even if they do not expect full democracy or the reunification of the peninsula in the near future. Above all, they want their children to have lives less constrained than their own.

Money has altered the old order in what Kim Jong Un calls a “socialist paradise.” During one interview, an escapee told me that he knew who would succeed Kim Jong Un to become North Korea’s fourth ruler. I expected him to name Kim Jong Un’s daughter or sister. “Money,” he said. He was being cheeky, but only partly. He pulled out his phone and read the biblical warning that no one can serve two masters, God and money; in his analogy, Kim occupied the place of God. North Koreans jostle for food, better education, and influence, and search for ways to make money outside state-sanctioned activities by paying bribes and using black markets. Younger elites increasingly judge a good marriage, job, and future career path in terms of how it can offer them access to cash and useful business connections. A title remains useful, often because it can be converted into income. The collapse of the state’s public distribution system and devastating famine years of the 1990s drove the grassroot marketization of the country, with the development of state-regulated marketplaces, some so large that they’ve hosted over 10,000 vendors. Presently, in this de jure socialist country that officially condemns capitalism, markets and the imperative of earning hard cash shape people’s daily lives.

This change appears in ordinary choices. One former businessman carried a two-dollar bill in his wallet as a good-luck charm to attract prosperity and wealth. Other interviewees remembered a time when families seeking husbands for their daughters preferred sons of party cadres; today, a man with access to foreign currency or a profitable business can be much more desirable. Political rank still opens doors, but money can keep them open.

Kim has accommodated some of these economic changes; once prohibited, markets are now regulated and taxed by the state. Although foreign media and the Internet remain strictly prohibited, the state allows North Koreans to access a highly controlled intranet and use smartphones with local software and applications. It grants apartments to selected families and permits officials to enrich themselves by engaging in business ventures, provided they continue to perform their jobs exquisitely and contribute mandatory “loyalty funds” to the state.

What Kim cannot offer is dependable and lasting safety. Everything remains subordinate to the leader’s needs and whims. An official who follows the law may still be punished for crossing a better-connected family, and a successful cadre may be swept away because his patron was purged.

Growing elite grievances do not mean an uprising is near. A disaffected elite has five options: remain silent and maintain the status quo; flee the country; build parallel networks of money and influence; protest; or push for incremental reform. The last three are extraordinarily dangerous in North Korea. The regime will extinguish dissent, bids for reform, and any networks that strive to operate or even seem to operate beyond the state’s control. For most, the choice then narrows to either carrying one’s grievances to the grave or mustering the courage and resolve to defect.

Most people opt to stay. Through imposing guilt by association and collective punishments, the North Korean state has spent decades attenuating the relationships through which private anger might be converted into collective action. Its surveillance system encourages colleagues, neighbors, and even relatives to watch and report one another. Unapproved associations and even impromptu sports matches at schools that bring together more than a few people are forbidden unless they are specially approved, and the state tightly controls information about purges and acts of protest to prevent the spread of dissension. In some border cities, such as Sinuiju, the party has recently banned any unapproved gatherings of three or more people.

When I discussed the obstacles to collective action with Lee Chul Eun, a former high-ranking official in the Ministry of State Security who defected in 2016 by swimming six kilometers to South Korea, he described the state’s security apparatus as a spiderweb of security agents, surveillance offices, military commands, and party bodies. A person would have to be “absolutely insane” to challenge such a system, he told me. Another former official summarized the social knowledge produced by that web: “We [elites] know everything but just don’t express it.” Elites can recognize propaganda, corruption, and private anger around them while not knowing whom they can fully trust to confide in.

The regime has other sources of durability. Trade with China, weapons sales, cybertheft from foreign banks and companies, and sending North Korean laborers abroad all help fund the regime’s operations, including its nuclear weapons program, security services, national network of political prison camps, and luxuries necessary for managing elites. The regime has survived famine, international sanctions, and leadership successions. Discontent has been building for years without ever transforming into any form of sustained, organized opposition.

This helps explain another surprising finding from my interviews and research. Many elites understand that the official story is false. They know that colleagues complain in private, seek bribes, watch foreign media, and concoct safe ways for their children to remain and work abroad. What they cannot safely determine is who would follow and stand with them if they tried to protest and push for reform. First movers are the most vulnerable to state repression, while everyone else can deny sharing the same views. North Korea’s political system depends on atomization and making doubters feel isolated from one another. The regime understands that the most plausible organized threat to its hold on the state would be Pyongyang powered, not people powered; a challenge or rebellion is more likely to start among elite insiders than as a mass revolt from below. But for now, elites don’t trust one another and lack the shared vision required to move against the regime.

Defection is the one option an individual can choose without first building a coalition. Elite escapees take with them expertise and signal to peers that leaving is possible. Yet China continues to forcibly repatriate North Koreans who cross its border. Officials posted abroad wishing to defect must dexterously evade state security officials stationed alongside them and come to peace with the fact that loved ones back home will surely be punished.

In August 2025, a North Korean interpreter traveling with a delegation from Pyongyang’s Academy of Social Sciences entered the South Korean embassy in Ulaanbaatar in Mongolia and asked to defect. A year later, the request is still unresolved and the interpreter remains in limbo in the embassy. Mongolia, which maintains relations with Pyongyang, has been slow to cooperate. North Korea objected, and Seoul, currently under a left-leaning administration that seeks warmer relations with the North, will not even confirm that the case existed. For officials considering escape, the lesson is stark: even reaching the embassy of an ostensibly democratic country may not deliver them from the tyranny of home.

Several elite defectors I spoke with had reached the United States and applied for asylum. Their claims have languished for years or been rejected; one claimant has waited more than nine years. They cycle through hearings and background checks with no resolution to their cases. A former North Korean official could certainly pose a counterintelligence risk. But keeping them in this purgatory tells others that the United States may be happy to take their valuable intelligence while still not offering them a future. The vagaries of American asylum policy are not responsible for the persistence of the Kim family regime, but Washington’s reluctance to welcome these defectors could suggest to wavering elites in Pyongyang that it might just be safer to stay put.

Kim Jong Un can continue to manage elite disaffection by rotating, purging, rewarding, and surveilling the very people who keep his regime running on a daily basis. But this approach provides only symptomatic relief to the overarching and insoluble crisis produced by his totalitarianism. To gain the confidence of his elites, he would have to stop using purges and executions, end the collective punishment of families for the transgressions of an individual, close political prison camps, permit freer flows of information, and allow for autonomous market activity without the fear of unpredictable shutdowns and confiscations. Each concession, however, reduces the fear and isolation of elites. To Kim’s detriment, that would only embolden elites to turn against him.

He will likely not relax his grip on the ranks of the powerful. But in so doing, he will weaken his own regime. The costs of preference falsification and performative loyalty will accumulate among the highest echelons of the regime’s operators. That will become most dangerous during a succession struggle or severe economic shock. In ordinary times, each official can continue assuming that all his colleagues will strive to maintain the status quo. But a crisis—such as a military coup, another volcanic eruption of Mount Paektu, or an economic meltdown—can change those assumptions swiftly.

In a moment of upheaval and uncertainty, already disaffected officials may conclude that obedient compliance will no longer protect their positions or their families. Officials who appear loyal today might withhold cooperation from a startled regime, engage in a power struggle, or defect en masse. If enough regime operators make these calculations, then Kim will find that the people he depends on to carry out his orders are prioritizing their own survival over that of his regime. And he could lose his hold on the country.

My interviews with erstwhile members of North Korea’s elite do not point to imminent regime collapse. Rather, they suggest a more haunting vulnerability for the Kim family: the loyalty of the elites, which is critical for regime survival, is becoming more performative, less genuine, and motivated by self-preservation, not a wellspring of ideological conviction. The regime’s habitual recourse to maximalist punishments and mass purges will likely drive more elites to defect and maybe, more strikingly, muster unprecedented expressions of collective action. Kim will face his last days when a large enough body of North Korean elites conclude that remaining loyal is no longer their safest and most rational choice.

JIEUN BAEK is the author of Privileged but Powerless: How North Korean Elite Grievances Reveal the Regime’s Greatest Weakness, from which this essay is adapted, and North Korea’s Hidden Revolution: How the Information Underground Is Transforming a Closed Society.

#6014. Can the Nordic Model Save NATO?

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MATTI PESU is Senior Research Fellow in the Finland and Northern European Security Research Program at the Finnish Institute of International Affairs.

In August, at Finland’s request, the Swedish government deployed fighter aircraft and a warship to help patrol Finland’s eastern airspace and the Gulf of Finland against Russian air incursions and drones straying from Ukrainian operations. Critically, the Swedish assets involved were placed under Finnish command and followed Finnish rules of engagement. It was the latest example of how Finland and Sweden, the two newest additions to NATO, have made qualitative leaps in defense coordination.

The Nordic countries have expanded regional collaboration significantly in the past four years. Between them, Denmark, Finland, Iceland, Norway, and Sweden now boast a new long-term defense strategy, a cross-border transit area, frequent joint exercises, and unprecedented levels of command integration. In effect, these governments are forming their own regional deterrence cluster: a like-minded group of states deepening military cooperation to strengthen deterrence and, if required, ensure NATO’s broader collective defense.

The Nordic model offers a glimpse of the adaptive solutions that may sustain NATO through uncertain times. Moscow’s appetite for risk is growing; its military buildup and escalating attacks on European infrastructure suggest that Russia constitutes a long-term threat to the alliance. At the same time, the Trump administration is pushing its European allies to take primary responsibility for the continent’s defense. Europeans must therefore deter a more dangerous Russia with less help.

To strengthen their deterrence and defense capabilities, member states along NATO’s extended frontline—and in the Baltic and Black Sea regions, in particular—should build their own regional clusters and foster a strong latticework of bilateral and “minilateral” partnerships with their neighbors. Regionalization remains a taboo subject among most of the alliance’s political leadership; NATO is, after all, a collective defense alliance founded on indivisible security. But regional efforts at the military level will both make NATO more effective and advance the United States’ vision of a more European NATO.

Nordic-style deterrence clusters are thus a win-win. Yet in its erratic, occasionally aggressive approach to Greenland and the North Atlantic, Washington has risked undercutting the very allies that are delivering what it wants. The United States should use the recently signed Greenland deal as a reset to embrace Nordic defense efforts and create incentives for other allies to follow suit.

Nordic defense collaboration took its current institutional form in 2009, when recession-era budgetary constraints pushed the region’s five countries to rethink their defense commitments. Finland and Sweden joined NATO members Denmark, Iceland, and Norway in establishing the Nordic Defense Cooperation, primarily in hopes of cutting procurement costs. NORDEFCO boasted some early wins, including cross-border air force training in the uncongested airspace of northern Finland, Norway, and Sweden. But because NORDEFCO spanned NATO members and nonallied states, bureaucratic hurdles and a lenient threat environment quickly sapped momentum from joint procurement.

Only after Russia’s 2014 invasion of Ukraine did some Nordic countries begin to see cooperation as a security imperative, not just a way to save money. Finland and Sweden launched a no-limits military partnership that included joint wartime planning. And Norway, despite maintaining its NATO-first approach, gradually embraced operational cooperation with its two non-NATO neighbors in times of crisis. By 2020, when the countries signed a trilateral statement of intent, they were conducting extensive joint exercises, sharing intelligence on common threats, and discussing their national defense plans with an eye toward coordinated crisis response.

Any remaining limits on Nordic military cooperation evaporated once Finland and Sweden joined NATO following Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, in 2022. The region’s five countries agreed that Moscow posed an urgent security threat to themselves and to Europe as a whole. Even Denmark, which was previously reticent about regional collaboration, warmed to collective defense efforts. In 2023, it joined Finland, Norway, and Sweden in developing the Nordic Airpower Concept, which will give them the ability to fight as one force with their combined 250 advanced fighter aircraft. Denmark has also pledged to contribute personnel to the Swedish-led Forward Land Forces Finland, the NATO battle group that rotates between northern Finland and Sweden. When Denmark served as chair of NORDEFCO in 2024, the five governments also signed NORDEFCO Vision 2030, which outlines their ambition for joint operations that are aligned with both national and NATO defense interests. To support the logistical underpinnings of land force cooperation, the initiative established a seamless, borderless Nordic military mobility corridor.

A division of labor is now emerging: Finland commands regional land operations, Norway hosts the regional headquarters for air operations, and Sweden is becoming the logistics hub and transit area for joint military operations in northern Europe. Denmark, for its part, hosts a regional headquarters for special operations. The Nordic deterrence cluster also functions smoothly within NATO’s collective defense framework. Larger NATO allies—including France, Germany, the United Kingdom, and the United States—routinely operate and exercise in the Nordic space alongside their regional partners and fill capability gaps in areas such as intelligence, reconnaissance, and surveillance. The result is an increase in defense capabilities in NATO’s Joint Operations Area Northwest, which covers the North Atlantic and includes vital sea-lanes, critical undersea infrastructure, and the Arctic and Nordic regions bordering Russian strategic hot spots.

Deterrence clusters could well be the future of NATO’s frontline posture. In addition to NATO’s Joint Operations Area Northwest, NATO has two more regional plans—Center, covering the Baltic states and central Europe, and Southeast, covering Turkey and the Balkans—each with its own operations area and command center network. Governments in these two regions should follow the Nordic example and take the initiative to strengthen their own minilateral ties within the NATO framework.

An obvious starting point is the southeastern Baltic Sea, which sits at the heart of NATO’s Joint Operations Area Center. Although the Baltic states have a shared vulnerability to Russian encroachment, defense cooperation has been modest since they joined NATO, with Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania preferring to rely on extraregional patrons such as the United States. Recent moves, however, suggest that regional collaboration is gathering pace: Estonia and Latvia have jointly procured the IRIS-T air defense system, and all three countries are coordinating their HIMARS rocket systems and building the Baltic Defense Line, a fortification project designed to channel, delay, and block a Russian ground invasion.

But Baltic cooperation would benefit from a more comprehensive minilateral layer resembling NORDEFCO. In practice, this could mean coordinating national defense plans, conducting joint exercises, and sharing missile stocks for air defense systems. It could also entail merging HIMARS launchers for joint fires capability, better integrating national contributions to the Baltic Defense Line, and jointly operating uncrewed vessels to monitor the sea and underwater infrastructure. The aim would be to enable three militaries to fight together as seamlessly as possible, generating a combined power greater than the sum of its parts.

Such a format, if realized, should also involve Poland—an emerging European military powerhouse that currently plays a limited role in Baltic defense. Although Warsaw maintains a relatively robust defense partnership with Lithuania, involving joint exercises and working groups, the two neighbors have previously failed to establish a joint exercise area in the Suwalki Gap, a key strategic corridor and vulnerable supply line connecting the Baltic states to continental Europe. A stronger minilateral framework would create a standing process for joint planning and consultation that could resolve the local objections and divergent strategic priorities that have dogged previous negotiations over the gap.

Within such a framework, Warsaw could reinforce regional defense by connecting its East Shield—Poland’s own program of fortifications, barriers, and surveillance systems along its borders with Russia and Belarus—with the Baltic Defense Line and by contributing ground-based air defenses to NATO’s Baltic Air Policing mission. The fundamental aim should be to strengthen Poland’s role as a leading security provider in the Baltic region alongside Germany, which is permanently stationing a brigade in Lithuania.

The other frontline arena that would most benefit from a deterrence cluster is the Black Sea region, which sits within NATO’s Joint Operations Area Southeast. Bulgaria, Romania, and Turkey have struggled to cooperate because of divergent assessments of the threat posed by Russia. Building minilateral latticework here is clearly harder than in the Nordic or Baltic Sea areas. But in a more European NATO, the rest of the alliance would expect Black Sea allies to contribute more to collective defense, and the conditions for doing so are improving.

The most promising domain for expanded minilateral cooperation is maritime. In July 2024, Bulgaria, Romania, and Turkey launched a joint mine-clearing operation, which led to the establishment of a joint mission to protect energy infrastructure, telecommunications networks, and undersea pipelines owned and operated by the three allies. This embryonic naval force could be developed further to cover more ambitious contingencies in the Black Sea, from sea surveillance to the protection of territorial integrity, as the Finnish-Swedish naval task force does in the Baltic Sea.

Air forces are another potentially fruitful area for Nordic-style collaboration, especially as regional militaries grow their capabilities. Bulgaria awaits its F-16s, Romania is set to acquire F-35s, and Turkey, already a potent air power, is seeking readmission to the F-35 program. In 2009, the Nordic air forces began weekly cross-border training, paving the way for today’s cooperation. More frequent exercises among the Black Sea allies would be a logical first step toward enhancing interoperability.

For the Nordics, reaching today’s level of collaboration took more than 15 years. Other frontline regions cannot afford to wait that long. Russia may emerge from the war in Ukraine more capable and less risk averse. Tighter subregional cooperation would not only benefit the security of exposed allies on the frontline by enhancing their capacity to respond together. It would also make NATO’s frontline posture more effective, deterring Russian encroachment deeper into Europe—an aim that allies farther from the frontline should share. Moreover, by strengthening the minilateral latticework, countries close to Russia would enhance their capacity to act together before NATO acted collectively. In extreme circumstances, if the alliance was paralyzed, subregional clusters would offer a hedge, enabling frontline allies to respond together.

The United States, too, has a vested interest in the development of deterrence clusters across NATO’s eastern borderlands. U.S. officials have articulated a vision for “NATO 3.0,” a rebalanced alliance in which Europeans lead their own conventional defense and the United States plays a supporting role, providing extended nuclear deterrence and a few other capabilities. If the Europeans are to assume primary responsibility for their conventional defense, subregional cooperation arrangements—with high interoperability, joint procurement, planning and command capacity, cross-border mobility, and even certain divisions of labor—will be paramount.

Nordic defense cooperation represents the most mature version of such efforts, and the Nordic states have already demonstrated their readiness to work with the United States on Russian-related security threats. Finland and Norway, for instance, have directed their intelligence capabilities toward monitoring what happens on the Russian side of their borders, and Finland lets U.S. signals-intelligence aircraft use its airspace to monitor the Kola Peninsula, where the majority of Russia’s nuclear second-strike capability is concentrated. By preventing Russian dominance in the broader North Atlantic, Nordic cooperation has also advanced the 2025 U.S. National Security Strategy objective of maintaining—in cooperation with allies and partners—favorable balances of power in the world’s key regions.

There is every reason for Washington to treat the Nordic states as exemplary allies and endorse their advancements in regional cooperation. And yet the Trump administration’s approach to the region has been at best incoherent. On the one hand, U.S. officials have commended Nordic efforts to bolster defense spending—Denmark, Finland, Norway, and Sweden are all above or on track to reach three percent of GDP by 2029 and are committed to reaching the Hague target of five percent of GDP by 2035. On the other, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly mused about taking over Greenland, a nightmare scenario for the Nordics that would pit their mutual solidarity against their respective relationships with the United States.

The Greenland agreement that Washington, Copenhagen, and Nuuk signed in September should encourage the Trump administration to play a larger, more constructive role in Arctic security. So far, Washington has treated defense spending as the key enabler of NATO 3.0. But a European-led alliance also needs allies that can use their growing capabilities as efficiently and seamlessly as possible. If the United States were to encourage and back Nordic-style minilateral latticework in other critical subregions, it would empower a more competent, capable, and commanding NATO 3.0.

The Greenland Deal Proves Self-Reliance Is Not an Option

What the World Can Learn From Switzerland, Taiwan, and Vietnam

What Sweden and Finland’s Membership Will Mean for the Alliance

Thibault Muzergues and Kenneth M. Pollack

Will Russia Seek to Disrupt Helsinki’s NATO Bid?

#6010. How America Can Counter Chinese Coercion

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

THEODORE BUNZEL is Managing Director and head of Lazard Geopolitical Advisory. He has worked in the political section of the U.S. Embassy in Moscow and at the U.S. Treasury Department.

EDWARD FISHMAN is Senior Fellow and Director of the Maurice R. Greenberg Center for Geoeconomics at the Council on Foreign Relations. He is the author of Chokepoints: American Power in the Age of Economic Warfare.

ADDAR LEVI is Deputy General Counsel and Global Head of Regulatory Affairs at Lazard. She was previously the Acting General Counsel of the U.S. Department of the Treasury.

The Case for an Economic Security Alliance

Theodore Bunzel, Edward Fishman, and Addar Levi

Over the past year and a half, China has shown the world the power of its economic arsenal—starting with the United States. In April 2025, Beijing retaliated forcefully against U.S. tariffs, imposing matching across-the-board levies and export controls on rare-earth elements. This counterpunch rattled bond markets and disrupted auto supply chains, forcing Washington to seek a truce that persists to this day.

Next, China targeted Japan. After Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi described a potential Chinese naval blockade of Taiwan as a “survival-threatening situation” that could justify deploying Japan’s Self-Defense Forces, in November 2025, Beijing imposed suffocating bans on rare-earth exports to the country. Reluctant to reinflame tensions with Beijing, Washington offered nothing more than token support. Tokyo has held firm, refusing to walk back Takaichi’s statement; the restrictions remain in force, and Japan’s mineral stockpiles are dwindling.

Then Europe came under fire. Fed up with a trade deficit with China that had reached $1.1 billion per day, the European Union began exploring new tariffs to counter Chinese overcapacity in May. As China threatened “resolute countermeasures,” French President Emmanuel Macron urged U.S. President Donald Trump at the G-7 summit in June to join forces in addressing global imbalances. But the United States elected to sit on the sidelines again, and Europe blinked, opting for talks with Beijing, with an October deadline set by Europe to take concrete steps toward reducing China’s trade surplus. The EU has since been in tense negotiations with Beijing over reducing exports of hybrid vehicles, chemicals, and machinery, taking on the threat of Chinese retaliation alone.

China, in other words, is exploiting the disunity between the United States and its partners. In a divide-and-conquer strategy that mirrors the Trump administration’s own globe-spanning tariff campaign, Beijing has broken the Western alliance into more manageable pieces, allowing it to fight actors one on one. Its market heft and control over critical minerals have been decisive, and as its fear of retaliation has diminished, it has rolled out more aggressive export restrictions and sanctions. In 2023 and 2024, Beijing added only five Western companies to its Unreliable Entity List, exposing them to financial and legal penalties; in 2025 alone, it added 76.

There is still hope for the United States and its allies—if they can relearn to act in concert. These countries should build a formal economic security alliance that establishes collective defense against economic coercion. The foundation of this alliance would be a pledge modeled on NATO’s Article 5, which considers an attack on one as an attack on all, in this case promising a joint response to economic coercion against any member of the alliance. Over time, this alliance could grow to address other economic challenges shared by members, just as NATO began as a security guarantee before evolving into a more integrated and expansive military alliance. It could adopt joint tariffs against Chinese overcapacity and currency manipulation, coordinate investment restrictions and export controls on advanced technology, and cooperate on critical minerals and AI development.

Amid transatlantic squabbles and European calls for “strategic autonomy,” a Western economic security alliance may seem more out of reach today than ever before. But enduring interests can outweigh temporary political friction. With the right policy shifts and trust-building measures—starting with the removal of Trump’s tariffs on allies and legislation that limits the U.S. president’s ability to reimpose them in the future—Washington can forge such a partnership and use it to check Chinese coercion.

The United States, Europe, and Japan are all confronting a major shared challenge: Beijing is intent on making the rest of the world more dependent on China and China less dependent on the rest of the world. Beijing subsidizes domestic production far beyond local demand, undervalues its currency by 20 to 30 percent, and enacts coercive economic measures—such as export controls and sanctions—to gain leverage over Western governments and companies and undermine policies it deems against its interest. The country’s resulting industrial overcapacity and surging exports, epitomized by a record $1.2 trillion trade surplus last year, threatens U.S., European, and Japanese industrial competitiveness and jobs. Beijing’s record of weaponizing its strengths also means that Chinese advancements in frontier technologies, such as artificial intelligence and connected vehicles, which continuously share data with external devices, pose security threats to Washington and all its partners.

Individually, the United States and its allies may struggle to match Beijing’s economic leverage, particularly its chokehold on rare-earth elements. But collectively, they are far stronger. The United States, Europe, and Japan alone produce around three times the amount of economic output that China does. They are home to the vast majority of the world’s most sophisticated companies. They also control critical inputs that China depends on, including lithography machines, specialized machinery for industrial production, chemicals, and aircraft engines. Despite efforts to reduce its dependence on the dollar, Beijing remains exposed to U.S. financial sanctions; only about 30 percent of China’s trade is settled in renminbi, and most of the rest is settled in dollars. The scholars Stephen Brooks and Ben Vagle have estimated that if the United States and its allies were to fully decouple from China, the harm to China’s GDP in the short run would be five to 11 times larger than that to U.S. GDP.

China is also more vulnerable than it appears. Despite an official GDP growth rate of five percent last year, the economic research firm Rhodium Group estimated that the country’s actual growth rate was 2.5 to 3.0 percent. China is undergoing a prolonged real estate crisis, state-driven overinvestment, and anemic domestic demand. With Beijing unwilling to address weak consumption, exports now fuel more than half of the country’s growth, according to Rhodium’s numbers, making access to Western markets China’s economic Achilles’ heel.

If history is any guide, these Western states should be able to coordinate to use their collective power. During the Cold War, they came together to form the Coordinating Committee for Multilateral Export Controls to restrict sales of military and dual-use technology to the Soviet Union. After the 1973 Arab oil embargo, Canada, Japan, the United States, and a large group of European countries established the International Energy Agency, which required members to share reserves with any member affected by a supply cut—effectively a mutual defense pact for energy. And during the 2008 financial crisis, the U.S. Federal Reserve and other allied central banks coordinated rate cuts and established emergency swap lines to avert another Great Depression.

Yet in recent years, collective economic initiatives among Western allies have been underwhelming. In 2021, the U.S.-EU Trade and Technology Council was created to coordinate industrial policy and export controls toward China, but it largely devolved into a talk shop. In 2023, the EU established its Anti-Coercion Instrument after China boycotted Lithuanian goods in response to the opening of a Taiwanese Representative Office in Vilnius, but it has yet to use it. That same year, the G-7 announced a new Coordination Platform on Economic Coercion, which pledged members to warn each other and cooperate against shared economic threats, such as China’s nonmarket policies and economic coercion on critical minerals. But the platform contained no binding commitments and has largely been ignored.

These initiatives foundered from a lack of urgency and a reluctance among European countries to antagonize China, which is one of their largest trading partners. They were also constrained by disputes between European countries and the Biden administration over steel and aluminum tariffs and the large domestic subsidies in the U.S. Inflation Reduction Act. But Trump’s second-term policies have been the nail in the coffin. U.S. tariffs and threats to annex Greenland have rattled trust among American allies. Washington’s trade truce with Beijing has dispelled illusions that the transatlantic partners form a united front and made European partners doubt that the United States will come to their aid. Calls are indeed growing within Europe for de-risking and “digital sovereignty”—but from the United States, not China.

These calls will not be easy to quiet, even if Trump is followed by a president focused less on tariffs and more on building alliances. During the Biden administration, allies told U.S. officials that they would not commit to larger-scale economic coordination on shared challenges regarding China, Ukraine, or the Middle East because they were concerned that a future U.S. president could erase the progress. Europe’s concerns are well founded. As American politics has become increasingly polarized, more power has amassed in the executive branch. Congress, which historically provided ballast in U.S. foreign policy, has been absent. When Trump announced sweeping duties under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act against U.S. allies in April 2025, Congress did effectively nothing. When the Supreme Court ruled these tariffs as unconstitutional, the administration pivoted to other tariff authorities, and again, Congress stood by. Rebuilding trust will therefore require an institutional remedy that goes beyond communiqués and professions that “America is back.”

There is reason to think that the United States can repair its ties. The country still has a strong economic relationship with Europe. The value of cross-border mergers and acquisitions and investments across the Atlantic last year remained mostly normal, and total trade in goods and services between the United States and the EU increased by nearly six percent over the course of 2025. There is also little evidence a major economic rupture is coming. Despite frustrations with Washington in European capitals, European businesses want to maintain ties with the United States.

More important, Europe has finally awoken to the economic threat from China, because the surge of auto and industrial exports from China to Europe poses an existential challenge for the continent’s economic model. Chinese auto exports to Europe increased by 29 percent in 2025 and by over 60 percent year-over-year in 2026 as European auto exports to China have flatlined. Germany’s mighty auto industry alone lost 50,000 jobs in 2025. Even Volkswagen, which lobbied against the EU’s tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles in 2024, has changed its tune. In July, its CEO all but urged the EU to extend tariffs to Chinese plug-in hybrids. Germany, which shed 74,000 industrial workers in non-auto sectors in 2025, has begun quietly identifying chokepoints it can use as leverage over China. Despite a provisional agreement with Beijing to curb Chinese hybrid vehicle exports in October, Brussels appears poised to unveil new measures against China on chemicals and machinery in the coming months as it finally grapples with the threat of Chinese industrial overcapacity.

These challenges will only get worse in the coming years and make Europe more likely to seek the United States’ help. And in Washington, the notion of a united Western front against Beijing has bipartisan resonance. As Kurt Campbell and Rush Doshi have recently argued in Foreign Affairs, even if the Trump administration continues to alienate some European partners, shared interests will keep them open to allied cooperation when the next administration comes.

When it does, the most important step will be terminating every tariff—issued pursuant to Section 232, Section 301, Section 338, or Section 201—and pending investigation against any ally that was imposed during the second Trump administration. This would present both a unilateral demonstration of good faith and an opening gambit for negotiations on an economic security alliance. It would be a decisive break from Trump’s tariff policy and a rejection of Biden’s halfway model, which mostly turned tariffs into quotas and suspensions. It would be both politically popular, considering Americans’ dismal view of tariffs, and good for the economy, as the removal of duties could lower prices and accelerate growth. And because Trump’s tariffs have all been enacted via executive action, they can also be terminated that way.

As part of this first phase, the White House should also pledge support to any ally facing Chinese coercion, by providing targeted economic assistance or access to relevant stockpiles, such as the rare earths the Trump administration has begun amassing in its U.S. Strategic Critical Minerals Reserve. If the president made good on such pledges, it would demonstrate concrete benefits to potential members and build confidence in the alliance’s viability.

These unilateral measures would set the table for a formal economic security treaty. That treaty’s foundation would include an economic mutual defense commitment, mimicking NATO’s Article 5, which would go a long way toward restoring Western escalation dominance over China and therefore defanging Beijing’s threats. Such a pledge would have both defensive and offensive components. For defense, members would commit to providing targeted allies with economic assistance, preferential market access, and access to stockpiles of critical goods. Offensively, members would respond to coercion against an ally by applying joint sanctions, tariffs, or export controls. The United States, the EU, and Japan should form the core of this alliance given their individual economic and strategic heft, but other like-minded partners, such as Australia, Canada, and the United Kingdom, should be welcome to join as well.

Ensuring the alliance’s longevity will require more than goodwill gestures carried out by executive action. The most durable approach would be a congressional-executive agreement backed by legislation, which would embed the alliance firmly in U.S. law and make it harder to unwind. Critically, this legislation should protect allies from the whims of future presidents. Over the years, Congress has delegated broad powers to the executive to impose tariffs for national security and other reasons—powers that recent administrations have used with reckless abandon. Congressional treaty legislation could codify the removal of these excessive tariffs and restrict any additional duties on alliance members unless approved by Congress. This self-limiting measure would give allies confidence that the bargain would survive changes of U.S. administrations. To address the possibility that novel legal theories could somehow be used by the executive branch in the future, the treaty legislation should also enact an automatic sunset after 60 days on any new duties on alliance members unless Congress affirmatively acts to retain them. Finally, the bill would pre-authorize rapid assistance to any ally facing foreign economic coercion, giving the executive the authority to honor the pact’s collective defense commitment.

These measures would be important not only to improve trust between governments but also between governments and the private sector. Firms make investment decisions based partly on whether they expect relations between countries to remain stable and predictable—often on a five- or ten-year time horizon, beyond any one U.S. presidential term. Today, in light of the transatlantic friction over trade and technology, many companies are debating whether to create separate operational and supply chain ecosystems for Europe and the United States. By constraining a future U.S. president’s ability to impose new tariffs on alliance members, an economic security pact would give companies confidence to build lasting economic links across the alliance system, even in the absence of a formal free-trade agreement.

Enacting ambitious legislation is always difficult, but there is reason to believe that U.S. lawmakers would be supportive of such an alliance. Chinese economic coercion is a bipartisan concern. Although some members of Congress may be wary of coming out against tariffs, the tool has become significantly less popular during Trump’s second term, and reclaiming congressional authority over tariff policy would serve Congress’s institutional interests. In a February survey by the Harris Poll, 72 percent of Americans agreed that Trump’s tariffs have had more of a negative than a positive impact, with 60 percent of Republicans agreeing that they have negatively impacted consumers. Tariffs on allies are especially unpopular: in an Ipsos poll conducted in August, Americans opposed placing additional tariffs on Canada by nearly three to one. Tariffs have also become closely associated with inflation. In an August poll conducted by the Council on Foreign Relations and Morning Consult, 75 percent of Americans reported being concerned about the price of food and groceries, and 83 percent of that group said that tariffs had increased those prices. With affordability now Americans’ number one concern, lawmakers have strong incentives to vote for legislation limiting tariffs.

The United States would have to lead the way for an economic security alliance, but just as important would be negotiations among potential members over the rules governing the pact itself. The agreement should spell out how collective defense would work in practice, specifying how allies can facilitate sharing commercial information and economic intelligence; who determines when economic coercion has occurred; and what kind of assistance, offensive and defensive, members can expect in such an event. If the alliance were successful in expanding its membership and scope, it could establish an independent secretariat to help it function, but the goal should be to make commitments credible without creating another sprawling international bureaucracy.

Collective economic defense would be just the starting point. Over time, the alliance could adapt to address a broader set of economic security challenges. On Chinese overcapacity, members could coordinate tariffs on the sectors most distorted by nonmarket practices such as subsidies and preferential financing, preventing Beijing from simply redirecting surplus exports from one allied market to another, or leverage a common baseline tariff on Chinese goods to compel Beijing to strengthen its undervalued currency. The same logic applies to export controls, which Western countries vary widely in applying. To break reliance on Chinese critical minerals, members could use the alliance to simplify the confusing set of international critical mineral initiatives underway by streamlining financing, making long-term purchase commitments, investing in processing, and building up stockpiles.

In this way, collective economic defense could follow a path similar to NATO. The North Atlantic alliance began with a relatively simple security guarantee that evolved into a cohesive military bloc: common institutions, shared planning, interoperable capabilities, and habits of cooperation that would have been difficult to create at the outset. An economic security alliance could evolve in much the same way. A credible promise of mutual defense would create the confidence needed for governments and firms to integrate more deeply over time.

To be sure, an economic zone for the free world would be an ambitious undertaking, especially at a moment of unusually low trust between the United States and its allies. But the United States, Europe, and Japan do not need to agree on all matters of economic statecraft to build such a space, nor do they need to trust that future governments will always share the same priorities. What they need are institutions that allow them to cooperate despite those disagreements and that make their most important commitments durable enough to survive political change.

Moments of geopolitical rupture have provided fertile ground for institution building in the past. NATO emerged amid doubts about whether U.S. support would continue after World War II. In 1956, the Suez crisis badly strained U.S.-British relations, but in the years following, Washington and London rebuilt their partnership and established the 1958 Mutual Defense Agreement, which solidified nuclear cooperation and further bound the two countries together. The economic turmoil of the early 1970s, including the United States’ controversial exit from the Bretton Woods system and the Arab oil embargo, ended up pushing the major industrial democracies toward regular economic coordination that evolved into the G-7. In each case, crisis created opportunity.

Today, the hardest part for Washington will be accepting constraints on its freedom to act unilaterally. Presidents of both parties have long been wary of legislative checks on executive power out of fear that binding their hands would signal weakness. But if American power is constrained by predictable rules, allies will be more comfortable depending on it. If allies fear American economic power, they will hedge against it. Washington, in reserving the right to turn its economic weapons against its allies, will struggle to mobilize the collective weight of its alliances in markets, technology, and capital.

China’s divide-and-conquer strategy has capitalized on distrust within the U.S. alliance system. Restoring that trust will require Washington to make commitments that persist no matter who occupies the White House. The central wager of an economic security alliance is that the United States can gain leverage over China by surrendering some leverage over its allies. An economic zone for the free world can turn the tide against China, but only if the United States ties itself to the mast.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

How Military Partnerships Make Washington Vulnerable to Its Adversaries

The Case for an Economic Security Alliance

#6008. America saw the blue wave coming — but drastically underestimated the extent of the Dem tsunami

Historian Tom Del Beccaro joins 'Fox & Friends First' to analyze the midterm election results and discuss President Donald Trump's vow to work with Democrats.

Polling and election analysts pointed to Democratic gains in the 2026 midterms, but the results brought wider margins and bigger upsets that even some of the most ambitious predictions didn't see coming.

This included a historic Democratic breakthrough in Texas and other deep red strongholds.

Democrats won control of the Senate and House in Tuesday's election in what could be their largest House majority in nearly two decades.

The results marked a stunning rebuke to President Donald Trump, who ran in 2024 on fixing the economy and ending all wars. He launched a campaign blitz in the final weeks of the campaign cycle to rally Republican voters — but it wasn't enough to save the party's floundering message.

James Talarico’s victory over Ken Paxton helped make Texas one of the biggest upsets of the 2026 midterms, as Democrats flipped a Senate seat in a state Trump carried by double digits in 2024. (Brandon Bell/Getty Images)

DEEP IN TRUMP COUNTRY, A NEW GENERATION REVEALS THE DIVIDE BEHIND TEXAS’ TIGHT SENATE RACE

That rejection was especially striking in Texas, where Democrats pulled off twin upsets, with James Talarico defeating Attorney General Ken Paxton and Gina Hinojosa unseating Gov. Greg Abbott. The victories defied expectations in a state Trump carried by double digits just two years earlier.

A Fox News poll in October showed Talarico and Paxton nearly tied, with Abbott leading Hinojosa. Both Democrats ultimately won by wide margins.

The Texas Senate race was marked as a "toss-up" by the bipartisan Cook Political Report that gave Republicans an advantage with an R+6 ranking. But as of the early morning hours of Wednesday, Talarico held 53.9% of the vote to Paxton's 44.2% — a 9.7% gap in a race where polls were showing a clean match-up.

Abbott's race was the biggest miss when it came to ratings and polling versus real results. Hinojosa flew mostly under the radar, with Cook ranking the race a "lean Republican" — but the Democrat candidate ran away with a nearly 7% victory gap as of 3 a.m. on Wednesday.

And the Texas results came amid a broader Democratic tsunami.

Trump made a late campaign blitz to boost Republicans, but the party still suffered major losses in states he carried by double digits in 2024. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Texas’ Democratic upsets came as voters nationwide voiced dissatisfaction with Trump and the economy. A Fox News poll found Trump’s approval at 36%, while 74% of voters rated the economy negatively.

POLL FLASHES WARNING SIGNS FOR REPUBLICANS IN 7 BATTLEGROUND SENATE RACES

Democrats’ sweeping gains extended beyond Texas, with Senate upsets in Kansas and South Carolina, two states Trump carried by double digits in 2024.

In Kansas, Adam Hamilton scored a historic defeat over Sen. Roger Marshall, ending Republicans’ 94-year winning streak in Senate races in the state. Kansas will send to the U.S. Senate their first Democrat elected senator in nearly a century.

While the race was ranked a "toss-up," it still marks a historic departure for the ruby-red state.

And in deep red South Carolina, Annie Andrews defeated Sen. Darline Graham, who had been appointed to succeed her late brother, Lindsey Graham, but couldn't secure a victory at the ballot box.

The race was ranked "lean Republican," but the divide was 4.2 points as of Wednesday morning, marking a significant upset and an end to the more than two-decade reign of Grahams' holding onto the South Carolina Senate seat after Lindsey was first elected in 2002.

Democrat Adam Hamilton defeated Sen. Roger Marshall in Kansas, ending the state’s 94-year streak without a Democratic Senate win. (Jim Vondruska/Getty Images)

While Maine has long been considered a purple state, its 30-year-tenured Republican Senator, Susan Collins, was ousted in Tuesday's midterm by Democrat Troy Jackson. Collins was widely viewed as a career politician and served five terms in the U.S. Senate.

Cook rated the race a "toss-up," but the 6-point divide was still viewed as a major political upset.

Taken together, the results showed that the Democratic wave reached well beyond the usual battlegrounds, sweeping into states and seats that Republicans had long counted on carrying.

Amanda Macias covers the intersection of business, economics and politics, with a focus on how policy decisions shape markets, businesses and American workers.
