#6004. The Limits of China’s Self-Sufficiency

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOEL WUTHNOW is a Senior Research Fellow at the Institute for National Strategic Studies at the National Defense University and a co-author of China’s Quest for Military Supremacy. The views expressed here are his own.

Why the Country’s Food Insecurity Could Prevent a War Over Taiwan

When assessing the probability of a conflict in the Taiwan Strait, analysts tend to focus on a few obvious risks that China would face—namely, military defeat, economic slowdown, and restricted oil and technology imports. All of these are important, but an equally significant vulnerability is often left off the list: China’s reliance on soybean imports.

Beijing recognizes this liability. At the 20th Party Congress, in 2022, Chinese leader Xi Jinping identified “ensuring the security of food, energy resources, and critical industrial supply chains” as issues essential to national security and social stability. Food security, conspicuously listed first, is an especially sensitive issue for China’s 1.4 billion inhabitants: roughly 15 percent of the country’s population today was alive during the 1959–61 famine, which resulted in an estimated 30 million deaths.

As with technology and oil, Beijing has tried to limit its dependence on soybeans by building up stockpiles and diversifying its sources. But because soybeans are a fragile foodstuff, there is only so much China can do. Beijing can support a war effort, therefore, for only as long as the Chinese public could put the People’s Liberation Army’s success above its nutritional needs. And the key for deterring Chinese leaders from starting a conflict is to convince them that a domestic crisis would arrive sooner than any battlefield victory.

Disruption to critical imports is one of the most powerful deterrents to China’s use of force, and it’s a threat that the Chinese government has spent decades working to counter. The specter of a foreign blockade in the Strait of Malacca, through which most of Chinese oil imports pass, has worried Chinese leaders since at least 2003, when Chinese leader Hu Jintao warned about the risk of major powers’ interference creating a “Malacca dilemma” for China’s energy security. Beijing, however, has stockpiled massive reserves of oil, which it could complement in a crisis by increasing imports of Russian oil and, as Moscow has done, building up a sanctions-evading shadow fleet to transport oil from the Middle East.

Access to foreign technology is a newer but just as important concern, and China is similarly stockpiling critical components, such as chips. It is also having success in developing local alternatives. Beijing has already pulled ahead in some sectors, such as solar and battery manufacturing, or it is working to close the gap in others, as in semiconductors.

But Beijing has fewer options when it comes to self-sufficiency in food. Domestic production of staple grains—such as wheat, rice, and corn—accounts for more than 90 percent of consumption in these categories, but China remains dependent on foreign producers for other important foodstuffs, including soybeans, beef, and barley. Between 2000 and 2020, the share of China’s food supply provided by imports grew from six percent to 34 percent, and analysts predict it to increase to 41 percent by 2030. Part of this growth comes from the country’s economic development; as people become wealthier, their diets diversify, and they are no longer satisfied eating only grains. Between 1962 and 2024, meat as a percentage of the typical Chinese diet increased by a factor of 18.

This is where the central role of soybeans comes in. The legume provides a complex array of proteins in animal feed that other food sources and synthetic amino acids cannot replicate. Without it, livestock herds in China would shrink, thereby threatening domestic meat and dairy supplies. Soybean oil is also the most used cooking oil in China, making up nearly half the market. Alternatives, such as palm and rapeseed oils, are also mostly imported and face the same risks of maritime disruption, offering little relief.

China has invested a great deal in securing and, when possible, diversifying its access to foreign food sources. In 2013, a Chinese company bought Smithfield Foods, the United States’ largest pork producer. And state-owned enterprises such as COFCO International, a branch of China’s largest food and agriculture company, are buying farms and building logistical infrastructure in major agriculture-producing countries, such as Argentina and Brazil, which allows them to buy directly from farmers. Such direct investments provide a buffer against price volatility, giving Beijing more control over the volume and cost of supplies at their source. China has also steadily increased its own domestic meat production, which accounts for more than 90 percent of China’s demand. But such efforts do little to mitigate the effects of disruptions in maritime shipping—especially of soybeans, which is a supply chain that is fragile even in peacetime.

The central rub is that China has no choice but to import its soybeans. There is not enough arable land in China to both expand soybean cultivation and sustain self-reliance in staple grains. (Soybeans are also a much less efficient crop than corn or wheat in China’s arid north, making this tradeoff the economical choice.) But the countries that grow and export soybeans are almost all in the Western Hemisphere, which presents even more logistical challenges. When the trade war between the United States and China broke out in 2018, for instance, Beijing limited its U.S. soybean imports from 40 percent to about 20 percent of its supply, making up the difference with increased imports from Brazil. But Brazil’s poor infrastructure and frequent natural disasters make its harvest more susceptible to failure than the U.S. harvest. In 2024, one Brazilian state lost a quarter to a third of its soybean harvest because of flooding.

U.S. and Brazilian ports are also about as far from China as they could be. Brazil’s Santos port, for example, is more than 10,000 miles from Guangzhou, which takes 45 days to ship. Because of soybeans’ sensitivity to humidity, this distance means that ships often arrive in China with rotten cargo. For similar reasons, the legume is also difficult to stockpile. Unlike rice and wheat reserves, which Beijing claims could last for years, soybean reserves are smaller and must be replaced more frequently. Chinese stockpiles might be able to last only a few months if its imports were cut off. Although China has been experimenting with bioengineered supplements in an effort to reduce the amount of soybeans in animal feed, rising food demand is offsetting any gains. In other words, its dependence on soybean imports is only continuing to grow.

The outbreak of war between China and the United States would pose existential threats to this supply chain. In the most extreme scenario, the U.S. military could block shipments from the Western Hemisphere to China, which would take away most of the country’s soybean imports. China has no naval bases or forward forces in Latin America that could counter these efforts.

But China’s food supply chains would suffer from disruption even without a U.S. embargo or blockade. Any war in the Pacific would bring with it massive risk premiums for maritime shipment insurance that might cause shipping firms to balk. Such an effect has already been on display for shipping in the Red Sea, where Houthi missile threats have caused insurance costs to skyrocket since 2024. A war in the Pacific could be far more intense and costly because it would involve major navies, and ships could not route around the danger zone, making the long-haul journey from Brazil financially ruinous for many companies.

Beijing could, in theory, offer sovereign guarantees to offset firms’ insurance premiums. Even then, the firms transporting soybeans, most of which are owned by U.S. and allied country parties, might cease transporting products to China to avoid abetting a hostile regime, to steer clear of dangers that would put their employees at risk, and to head off possible future exposure to sanctions for doing business with the country.

Chinese analysts believe that China could circumvent some of these risks by using its own commercial fleet. Although this fleet has impressive capacity during peacetime—the Chinese shipping giant COSCO owns 468 dry bulk carriers—in wartime it would be stretched thin, facing demands to help move construction materials, industrial supplies, and foodstuffs for the People’s Liberation Army and to transport critical military materials such as coal and iron ore. Even available ships would likely lack personnel with the training needed to keep soybeans fresh during the long journey. And if ships tried to avoid the most dangerous sea-lanes, trips could take even longer, surpassing the 40 days that soybeans can be safely stored. Cargo might arrive rotten, if it arrived at all.

A war in the Pacific would not trigger an immediate food crisis in China. In the shorter term, the government could manage reduced imports by releasing supplies of soybeans, frozen pork, and other staples from state reserves. It could also reduce the minimum soybean inclusion rates for animal feed, which would slow livestock growth and cut meat output but which would be workable for a time. A few months of a managed crunch, however, is a best-case scenario. More likely is that panic buying and shipping disruptions would drain reserves faster than planned, turning a manageable shortfall into rising prices and visible shortages within months.

There is reason to believe, for instance, that Chinese stockpiles might not provide the short-term security they purport to. Over the last decade, local government warehouses meant to store emergency food supplies have been found empty or filled with rotten supplies, largely because of corruption. This not only calls into question how much extra food China truly has but also undermines public confidence in the government. In the case of a conflict, citizens conscious of the long-term disruption to come would begin panic buying and hoarding supplies. Whatever reserves do exist would eventually run out, with members of the elite likely being prioritized.

If the conflict dragged on, the situation would become increasingly dire. Agricultural producers, especially small farms, would be forced to cull their herds. Poultry, which requires especially high soybean content in its feed, would be the first casualty, which would then threaten the egg supply. Pork would be next. Cooking oils would grow scarce and expensive, making it difficult for restaurants and households to function. Eventually, public patience would wear thin: hoarding, protests over prices and shortages, and local officials diverting supplies could put mounting pressure on Beijing to end the war.

Unlike China’s vulnerabilities in energy or technology, the fragilities of the food system are endemic. If a conflict were to break out in the Pacific, the United States would not need to institute a blockade against China’s agricultural supplies or gather an international coalition to sanction China’s soybean imports. Soaring insurance costs and ship owners’ reluctance to sail into a war zone would be enough to squeeze the flow, and the Chinese government would have little recourse to stop it.

If Chinese leaders believed that they could achieve victory quickly and with few shots fired, they might take the bet. Deterrence, then, will require convincing them that they cannot. The United States, Taiwan, and other governments should invest in drones, rebuild munitions stockpiles, and ramp up messaging to China that a long, drawn-out conflict is likely. As long as China’s leaders believe that a war would be indecisive in its early stages, the threat of domestic crisis would be a powerful reason not to start one.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

#5999. America’s AI Ambitions Depend on Taiwan

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LIN CHIA-LUNG is Minister of Foreign Affairs of Taiwan.

Washington Cannot Achieve Tech Dominance on Its Own

The United States and China are locked in a global competition to determine who will dominate artificial intelligence and whether it will be democracies or autocracies that set the standards and values of the new digital age. The United States leads the world in the sophistication and power of its artificial intelligence models, and its innovation base and investment in research and development remain unparalleled.

But AI competition is much broader than modeling alone. It encompasses a technology ecosystem that includes power generation, servers, and deployment in real-world applications. Jensen Huang, co-founder and CEO of Nvidia, has described the AI industry as a five-layer cake with energy at its base and chips, infrastructure, models, and applications above it. To win the AI race with China, the United States needs to maintain or establish an advantage in each of these layers. And to do that, the United States needs Taiwan.

Taiwan produces more than 90 percent of the world’s most cutting-edge semiconductors, making it indispensable to the United States’ AI industry. Taiwanese companies also assemble roughly 90 percent of the servers that power the global AI economy. With complementary strengths in manufacturing and design, respectively, Taiwan and the United States have built a mutually reinforcing partnership in AI that has underpinned U.S. leadership in the sector. Alongside democratic countries around the world that don’t want to rely on China for their technology needs, the U.S.-Taiwanese partnership has also made a secure AI supply chain possible.

Some policymakers in the United States have blamed Taiwan for the offshoring of U.S. semiconductor manufacturing. Although it is true that the United States no longer manufactures as large a share of the world’s chips as it did in the mid-twentieth century, this narrative obscures the larger story. Taiwan did not weaken the U.S. semiconductor industry. Instead, Taiwan has been crucial to maintaining the United States’ technological superiority. By carving out a niche in advanced manufacturing and supply chain management, Taiwan can help the United States maintain its advantage in innovation and R & D. As the stakes of the AI race grow, Taipei and Washington—and the rest of the democratic world—will need to work together to prevail.

First and foremost, AI relies on access to advanced semiconductors. For much of the 1950s through the 1970s, the United States dominated the entire chip industry, from design to manufacturing. As the Center for Strategic and International Studies notes, “The United States created the modern-day semiconductor industry and has historically led in the technology’s development.” But as chips became increasingly complex and expensive to produce, the industry became more specialized. Although Silicon Valley held on to its strong lead in chip design, it began to outsource manufacturing capacity to East Asia. Today, as Washington tries to revive manufacturing, understanding the sources of this shift are essential for determining how to most effectively rebuild U.S. capacity.

In fact, Taiwan’s semiconductor industry was born out of U.S.-Taiwanese cooperation. With the onset of globalization and expansion of free trade starting in the 1970s, U.S. companies accelerated their efforts to outsource chip manufacturing to overseas partners. Taiwan’s government, seeking to move up the global value chain, established the Industrial Technology Research Institute, an organization for nurturing new technology ventures and channeling R & D to industries; bought technology licenses from the United States; and built Hsinchu Science Park in northwestern Taiwan. Taiwan also invested heavily in education and training for engineers. As the semiconductor industry developed, large Taiwanese technology firms such as TSMC expanded their operations and countless small and medium-size enterprises contributed to supply chains, creating a virtuous cycle that encouraged entrepreneurship, strengthened regulations and standards, and promoted international cooperation.

The result was an industrial ecosystem that was specialized, collaborative, and difficult to replicate. This ecosystem freed up the United States to do what it does best: design incredibly complex chips. Taiwan’s investment in manufacturing not only lowered the cost of U.S. technology; it also meant that whatever Silicon Valley dreamed up, Taiwan was soon capable of building.

Taiwan’s success as a semiconductor powerhouse has therefore never been at the expense of the United States. Rather, it has been based on decades of collaboration, a division of labor, and mutual trust between Washington and Taipei. Today, the United States continues to lead the world in semiconductor innovation, design, and research and development, whereas Taiwan is at the forefront of advanced manufacturing, packaging, and supply chain integration. U.S. tech firms such as Nvidia, Apple, and Qualcomm need Taiwan’s manufacturing sector to turn their creative ideas into usable products. Rather than replace the United States, Taiwan enables American innovation to reach global markets.

Still, Taiwan understands Washington’s determination to strengthen domestic semiconductor production and is willing to support that effort. TSMC has committed to investing $265 billion in semiconductor fabrication plants in Arizona. More than 20 other Taiwanese companies have also invested billions of dollars in the U.S. semiconductor ecosystem, creating jobs and expanding manufacturing capacity in the United States.

For Taiwan, these investments strengthen the existing supply chain partnership with the United States. They will become even more important as the U.S. semiconductor ecosystem grows, because the United States will need to rely on Taiwan’s knowledge and network of suppliers and engineers, built up over decades, to sustainably develop its own industry. There is no contradiction between what’s good for the United States and what’s good for Taiwan: with demand for chips continuing to rise, both sides benefit from deeper partnership and the sharing of expertise and supply chain security.

The U.S.-Taiwanese partnership extends beyond chips. The United States boasts world-leading AI platforms and cloud service providers; Taiwan is the world’s leading manufacturer of the AI servers on which they run. Taiwanese companies such as Foxconn, which accounts for approximately 40 percent of the world’s AI server market, and Quanta Computer, which builds high-density AI servers and infrastructure for cloud service providers, manufacture the essential products and components underpinning the operations of major U.S. technology companies, including Alphabet, Amazon, and Meta.

The United States can expand its AI-related manufacturing capacity, but it would take many years for it to match the scale, supplier networks, engineering expertise, and manufacturing capabilities of Taiwan’s existing industry. It is far easier and cheaper for Taiwan to manufacture the infrastructure that enables U.S.-designed AI chips and platforms to be deployed rapidly and at scale. Even if the United States were to drastically expand its domestic manufacturing capacity, it would only be able to produce a fraction of what is needed to keep pace with AI demand.

The next phase of AI competition will be over how to apply the technology in the real world. The United States and China are going to be in a race to fuse AI with robots, drones, and other defense systems, as well as to integrate it into manufacturing, health care, and transportation. Indeed, this process is already well underway: robotic taxis, for instance, are now operating in cities such as San Francisco and Shenzhen, and companies in China and the United States are jockeying for leadership in the sector.

In the application of AI technology, Taiwan’s strengths also complement those of the United States. The island’s semiconductor, communications equipment, and precision machinery industries are precisely those that will be needed to build the physical systems that can use American AI models. Taiwanese companies such as Thunder Tiger are already developing AI-driven defense drones, demonstrating the growing potential for integrating artificial intelligence, precision engineering, and advanced electronics into civilian and military applications. As AI evolves from a purely digital technology to an essential component of factories, robots, and vehicles, Taiwan’s advanced hardware production base will be even more valuable to the United States.

As the AI race intensifies, it will also become a contest over values, data management, and trust. If the United States and its democratic allies and partners want to stay ahead in technology, they must work together to create secure and trustworthy AI ecosystems that reflect their principles.

Supply chain collaboration will be crucial because no single place can dominate every aspect of the AI ecosystem. The United States excels in innovation and is home to major platforms; Japan is a leader in specialized refining, recycling, and research into deep-sea resources, as well as in precision semiconductor manufacturing equipment; the Netherlands supplies advanced lithography machines needed to produce the most advanced chips; and Taiwan specializes in wafer fabrication and packaging. Only through coordination can these countries build efficient and secure AI supply chains while reducing their reliance on China for essential components.

International disruptions have reinforced the necessity of supply chain cooperation among democratic countries. The COVID-19 pandemic, for instance, exposed the weaknesses of the world’s global supply chain networks, a lesson reinforced by disruptions to trade flows in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and rising energy prices amid instability in the Middle East. In 2025, Taiwan proposed a global semiconductor democratic supply chain partnership initiative to jointly construct reliable systems that can withstand international disruptions. In this initiative, Taiwan seeks to harness cooperation among democratic countries to usher in a new era of growth in industries connected to high-end chips.

Taiwan and the United States have already made strides in technology cooperation. The U.S.-Taiwan Economic Prosperity Partnership Dialogue, a bilateral platform inaugurated in November 2020, has hosted high-level discussions on AI supply chain security, digital infrastructure, critical minerals, drone supply chains, and high-tech talent. These exchanges have helped U.S.-Taiwanese cooperation evolve beyond traditional trade and economic relations and paved the way for establishing a technological ecosystem that can bring together democratic partners and allies.

Taiwan also stands ready to ensure the safe and ethical usage of AI. Safety depends not only on the makeup of the AI model itself but also on the security and reliability of the hardware on which it operates—an area in which Taiwan can bring its expertise to bear. Taiwan and the United States can work together to increase cybersecurity, establish testing standards, and build reliable manufacturing systems to ensure that AI develops safely and can be trusted by the United States and its allies.

When it comes to the technology underpinning AI, Taiwan is not an adversary of the United States but its most trustworthy partner. In every layer of the AI “cake,” Taiwan and the United States have developed—and can continue to expand—a productive and mutually beneficial partnership. Taiwan is a force multiplier for the United States in artificial intelligence: it has the capability, reliability, and values necessary to support the United States’ technological ambitions. If Washington wants to compete effectively in AI, Taiwan is indispensable.

#5996. What Sanctions Get Right

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

S. C. M. PAINE is William S. Sims University Professor of History and Grand Strategy Emerita at the U.S. Naval War College.

ANAND TOPRANI is a Jeane Kirkpatrick Fellow at the American Enterprise Institute.

They are the co-editors of The Strategy of Sanctions: From Antiquity to the Twenty-First Century.

The United States frequently uses sanctions or embargoes to rein in malign actors. When policymakers become dissatisfied with the results, they often embrace military action next. Washington, for example, applied sanctions on Iraq for more than a decade before invading the country in 2003. It did the same with Iran, using sanctions to pressure the government into nuclear negotiations in the 2010s and then, after withdrawing from the resulting agreement in 2018, going to war with Tehran in February 2026.

Washington follows this pattern of behavior because sanctions alone rarely—if ever—make a problem disappear, such as by toppling the government responsible. This frustrates Americans, who tend to seek simple, quick, and permanent fixes to foreign policy problems. Many interpret the persistence of these problems as evidence that sanctions are futile. But the effectiveness of sanctions is measured in crises that do not occur—the nuclear test that never happens, for instance, or the shipping lane that remains undisturbed. These successes are invisible, but an adversary’s continuing provocations are not. As a result, American officials often conclude that it is necessary to turn to war.

This logic reflects a misunderstanding about how sanctions work. Sanctions may not be able to achieve maximalist aims, yet when they are deployed in concert with other policy tools, they can help manage problems. They do this by reliably depressing the target country’s economic growth over time. Although this hurts innocent civilians in the targeted countries as their leaders shift the costs from regime supporters to the politically disenfranchised, no government can escape the inexorable math of slowing economic growth. If an economy starts out with a three percent growth rate—roughly the global average—it will double in size in around 23 years. But if sanctions reduce annual growth to one percent, doubling in size will take 70 years, three times as long. Just compare the North Korean and South Korean economies, or look at nighttime satellite imagery of the Korean Peninsula, to see the compounding effects of sanctions over generations. Although sanctions may not change the targeted regime’s aspirations, they reduce its financial wherewithal and may deter other governments from emulating the behavior that precipitated the sanctions.

Wars can overthrow adversarial regimes, but usually only after years of fighting, and fixing the damage afterward takes considerable time and money. Too often the United States misidentifies its foreign policy problems as solvable on the cheap. It took an Allied occupation force of over 1.5 million soldiers to garrison Germany, a country of 80 million, after World War II. Fast forward 60 years, and Washington was not interested in raising the numbers of troops necessary to subdue Iraq, a country of nearly 30 million. Americans today are likewise unwilling to make sacrifices on the scale necessary to vanquish Iran, with its 90 million people—much less China, with its 1.4 billion. The challenges these countries might pose are too large to solve at an acceptable cost. In such cases, sanctions offer an economically sustainable strategy to manage troublesome situations in the long term.

According to the conventional wisdom about sanctions, these measures discourage unsavory behavior by raising its economic costs. If force becomes necessary, they also weaken the adversary in advance of war. This logic has endured since 1935, when the League of Nations sanctioned Italy for invading Ethiopia, in the hope that doing so would induce Italy to call off its invasion or at least temper its ambitions. Yet because the countries imposing sanctions were unwilling to include vital commodities such as oil and coal on the list of sanctioned items, the effort was ineffective.

Sanctions have evolved since 1935. Today, unlike broad sanctions that inflict significant collateral damage, so-called smart sanctions target specific institutions or individuals and are enforced through the banking system, blocking financial transactions linked to sanctioned activities. UN sanctions against Iraq starting in 1990 were, in a sense, the last gasp of the old sanctions regime. Their lifting was contingent on Iraq’s dismantling its weapons of mass destruction program and agreeing to respect Kuwaiti sovereignty. Because verification of the former proved difficult, many of the sanctions remained in place after a 1991 armistice, and they continued through the 2003 Iraq war. After U.S.-led forces invaded Iraq in March 2003 and overthrew Saddam Hussein, it became apparent that the sanctions had been effective. Isolation from the global economy had prevented Iraq from restoring its economy, rebuilding its military, and resuming its development of weapons of mass destruction.

Between the two wars, however, many observers believed that international sanctions were unable to deter Iraqi aggression and neutralize its weapons programs. Worried that Iraq’s Sunni government still posed a threat to Kurds in the north and Shiites in the south, the United States, the United Kingdom, and, for a short time, France imposed two no-fly zones over those regions, without UN consent. In 1998, after Baghdad expelled UN weapons inspectors, the United States and the United Kingdom bombed Iraq. The same year, a group of hawkish scholars and former government officials sent an open letter to U.S. President Bill Clinton describing the sanctions-based Iraq policy as “dangerously inadequate.” Many of these signatories would return to government in the George W. Bush administration to implement their recommendations.

To a certain extent, the critics had a point. Political divisions within the United States and between Washington and its partners had constrained international efforts to contain Saddam. Arab governments may have been wary of hurting their relationships with the United States, but Arab publics were sick of Western imperialism. The growing U.S. military presence in the Middle East further antagonized both the anti-imperialist left and radical Islamists, and many Europeans resented American unilateralism.

Saddam also pushed doctored stories about civilian hardship arising from sanctions, which Western journalists readily covered. Iraqi suffering was very real, but it was largely a result of Saddam’s own policies, which channeled resources to his regime rather than to the civilian population. Starting in 1995, the UN’s oil-for-food program allowed Iraq to sell some oil to pay for civilian necessities, but Saddam used the profits to pay kickbacks instead. He signed lucrative oil deals with China, France, and Russia, enticing these permanent UN Security Council members to weaken their support for sanctions. It was around this time that the UN and civil society groups started calling for smart sanctions, to narrow the scope of economic punishment and avoid causing wide-scale economic pain, although it would take several more years for the United States to refine this tool.

In the meantime, in Iraq, sanctions and no-fly-zone enforcement became increasingly frustrating, costly, and unpopular. Because their main purpose was to prevent Iraq from developing weapons of mass destruction and funding malign activities such as international terrorism, they required constant vigilance. Then came the 9/11 attacks and the war in Afghanistan, where a U.S.-led coalition toppled the Taliban regime within months, suggesting that wars—at least those conducted by the highly trained, technologically advanced U.S. military—could solve problems, unlike the sanctions that left dictators in place.

When U.S. President George W. Bush announced the Iraq war on March 19, 2003, he described its goals as “to disarm Iraq, to free its people, and to defend the world from grave danger.” This danger was Iraq’s alleged stockpiles of weapons of mass destruction and its links to al-Qaeda, the group behind 9/11. At first, Washington intended to leave the task of reconstructing Iraq to the UN, but it had to abandon that strategy five months after the invasion when foreign jihadists bombed the UN’s local headquarters, killing the head of the mission and 22 others. Within weeks, the UN withdrew most of its 600 staff members, leaving the United States fully in charge.

Rather than ending the trouble Iraq caused, occupation created more problems. The war lent credence to al-Qaeda’s accusations that Washington oppressed Muslims, attracting would-be jihadists around the world to join the cause. In 2006, al-Qaeda’s bombing of the Golden Mosque in Samarra, a holy site for Shiites, triggered brutal Sunni–Shiite fighting. More than 4,000 Americans lost their lives over the course of the war, while more than 200,000 Iraqis lost theirs. After the U.S. withdrawal in 2011, much of the Middle East and North Africa went up in flames. Foreign jihadists and remnants of Saddam’s Baath Party formed the Islamic State, also known as ISIS, and overran much of Iraq and Syria in 2014 and 2015. U.S.- and Iranian-backed forces gradually and at great cost pushed the group out, but many of its fighters simply took up arms elsewhere. Some went to Sahelian Africa, exacerbating violence there. Others joined the opposition to Bashar al-Assad’s regime in Syria. And the refugee crisis sparked in part by the Islamic State’s initial expansion continues to roil Europe.

Whereas war brought spiraling instability to the Middle East, North Africa, and Europe, international sanctions were more effective than U.S. policymakers thought. While they were in place, Iraq was unable to build weapons of mass destruction, and it dismantled many of its existing programs to secure sanctions relief. The 2003 war was not nearly as successful and cost far more than sanctions enforcement. Washington went in with the aim of building a free and stable Iraq allied to the United States and Israel, but it quickly became clear that goal was out of reach.

Another unintended consequence of the war was the unleashing of Iran, which for years Saddam’s Iraq had kept in check. Iran and the United States had been at loggerheads since the Iranian revolution of 1978–79. In November 1979, Washington sanctioned Iran for taking the U.S. embassy’s staff members hostage, lifting them upon the staff’s release in 1981. The Reagan administration reimposed sanctions in 1984 in retaliation for Iran’s support for Hezbollah in Lebanon, only to violate them by selling arms to Iran (and using the funds to arm anticommunist Nicaraguan rebels, in defiance of Congress) in what became the Iran-contra scandal. The Clinton administration, concerned about Tehran’s nuclear ambitions, added sanctions between 1994 and 1996. After the International Atomic Energy Agency found Iran to be in noncompliance with the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty in 2005, the Bush administration imposed additional sanctions, but they targeted specific entities, such as the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, rather than Iran as a whole to minimize the humanitarian costs. Then, in 2007, the world got behind a UN Security Council resolution sanctioning Iran to prevent it from acquiring nuclear weapons. Finally, in 2010 and 2011, the Obama administration, with congressional backing, limited Iran’s ability to sell oil and gas abroad, “virtually grinding the Iranian economy to halt,” as the president bragged.

The growing strength and scope of sanctions contributed to Iran’s election of a moderate president, Hassan Rouhani, in 2013 and empowered the Iranian factions advocating a negotiated settlement to the nuclear standoff. In 2015, Tehran agreed to a nuclear deal with the United States, China, France, Germany, Russia, the United Kingdom, and the EU. The agreement capped Iran’s uranium enrichment levels, reduced its operational centrifuge numbers, required Iran to repurpose its nuclear weapons development facilities, and established more rigorous international inspections to verify compliance. Sanctions relief would occur in tandem with verification over a 15-year period. Thereafter, either the signatories could negotiate a new agreement, or the sanctions would snap back into place. Critics complained, justifiably, that the 2015 deal did not prevent Iran from supporting proxy groups or limit its ballistic missile program. But the deal did offer a sustainable way to delay Iran’s nuclear weapons development and thereby manage the most important problem posed by a hostile Iranian regime. A more comprehensive solution, however desirable, was not feasible at the time.

The United States is discovering, yet again, that military action has delivered a far worse outcome than sanctions. After the first Trump administration withdrew from the nuclear deal in 2018, Iran restarted its weapons program. The second Trump administration and Israel went to war early this year in an ostensible effort to end Iran’s nuclear ambitions but proved unable to destroy all its nuclear sites. The United States and Israel assassinated Iran’s leadership, including the supreme leader and much of his family, yet the regime remains intact. Tehran has promised retribution for these killings, and there is no reason to think it will not follow through on the threat. Meanwhile, the war has wreaked global havoc with no end in sight. Iran responded to U.S. and Israeli attacks by effectively closing the Strait of Hormuz and striking oil, gas, and military infrastructure across the Middle East. The disruption of exports of petroleum, fertilizer, and helium has contributed to inflation, portends crop shortfalls, and even threatens computer chip shortages in countries around the world.

Although U.S. military casualties remain low, U.S. losses in destroyed infrastructure and depleted munitions stocks amount to billions of dollars, and some of it may be irreplaceable. The American military depends on satellite communications and radar to detect and target incoming enemy missiles and aircraft. Iran has heavily damaged or perhaps destroyed air defense systems the United States and its partners deployed at bases in the United Arab Emirates and Jordan, as well as two satellite communications terminals at a U.S. naval base in Bahrain. Iranian strikes also destroyed radomes, the outer shells that protect radar antennae, in Bahrain, Kuwait, and Saudi Arabia. The United States has lost at least 43 warplanes, including specialized refueling and surveillance aircraft and a surveillance plane worth $700 million. Many U.S. military bases are now inoperable, including the main regional naval base, in Bahrain. Because U.S. aircraft cannot operate safely from nearby bases, the war effort has relied on distant aircraft carriers positioned in the eastern Mediterranean and Arabian Seas, where they can avoid the dangers of Iranian missiles, drones, and mines.

Far from removing the Iranian regime, the war has demonstrated that it has the power to hold the global economy hostage. By comparison, the prewar sanctions that devastated Iran’s economy, hobbled its military, and discouraged it from seeking nuclear weapons were a bargain.

Sanctions have a far better track record than war in managing unsolvable problems at an acceptable price for a protracted period. With sanctions, the target country—including its civilian population, unfortunately—pays the costs. In war, by contrast, the costs are shared by both sides, and the collateral damage can be global, drawing the ire of countries distant from the battlefield.

Managing a problem through sanctions can contain it and contribute, along with other measures, to victory in the long run. During the Cold War, U.S. leaders understood that any military attack on the Soviet Union would trigger a ruinous nuclear war. Instead, they managed the Soviet threat for generations with a strategy of containment, which relied heavily on sanctions that blocked exports—the Soviet economy’s means of earning hard currency—and starved the Communist bloc of high-tech imports. The United States established the Coordinating Committee for Multilateral Export Controls for this purpose in 1949, together with Belgium, France, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, and the United Kingdom. Australia, Canada, Denmark, Germany, Greece, Japan, Norway, Portugal, Spain, and Turkey would eventually join. To enforce the controls, the 1951 Mutual Assistance Control Act authorized the cutoff of U.S. economic and military aid to violators. East–West trade plummeted. After the Korean War, Washington reluctantly bowed to European pressure to relax some restrictions. But after détente failed, the Carter and Reagan administrations again limited high-tech exports and tried to block Soviet sales of gas.

The Soviet Union could never overcome the economic damage from these measures. Sanctions greatly exacerbated the bloc’s preexisting economic weaknesses, such as its prioritization of heavy industry over consumer goods, its isolation from the free exchange of ideas, and its inefficient central planning based on mountains of inaccurate statistics. Soviet residents were left queuing up for subpar consumer goods. By the 1980s, new Soviet leadership concluded that their system could never close the growing economic, technological, and military gap between East and West, and they called off the Cold War.

Economic pressure by no means assures victory. But the alternative, war, rarely works as intended. In Iran today, U.S. leaders are learning that big ships and fast planes can close but not open narrow seas. A war ends when both sides agree it is over, so Iran can prolong the conflict even if the United States wants out. And even if the fighting ends soon, none of the prospective deals the Trump administration has announced to eliminate Iran’s nuclear program improve on the terms of the 2015 nuclear agreement. The now defunct memorandum of understanding that Washington and Tehran signed in June promised to lift sanctions and funnel billions to Iran in return for Iran’s reopening the Strait of Hormuz, possibly with Iranian tollbooths. Before the war, freedom of navigation through the strait was not in question.

There are two ways to end conflicts: either remove the capacity to resist or remove the will. Both sanctions and military action diminish an adversary’s ability to resist, but the bloodshed of war makes it more likely than sanctions to harden will. War, therefore, can make the underlying problem even more difficult to resolve. The cases of Iraq in 2003 and Iran in 2026 show that there was a better way. Sanctions alone would not have ended either threat, but even at their least effective, they proved useful at managing both situations, and they did so at a lower cost. Rather than a silver bullet, sanctions should be understood as a form of diplomatic chemotherapy to shrink the tumor and thereby diminish the threat. Sometimes that is the best option.

#5992. The Fight for the Future of Democratic Foreign Policy

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

THOMAS WRIGHT is a Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution. He served as the Senior Director for Strategic Planning at the National Security Council during the Biden administration.

The midterm elections mark the beginning of the Democratic Party’s 2028 presidential campaign. After two years of debate over the lessons of Kamala Harris’s defeat in the 2024 election, it promises to be one of the most open contests about the future of Democratic policies, including foreign policy, in generations. It also promises to be one of the most consequential. The strategic environment in 2029 is on track to be the most difficult and unforgiving since the height of the Cold War.

Democrats must therefore lay out their ideas early and see how they fare both with voters and against the trajectory of world events. The party is generally split into two camps: centrists who, when it comes to foreign policy, are focused on strategic competition, restoring the United States’ national power, and deepening alliances; and progressives, who propose a more affirmative agenda emphasizing shared challenges and global cooperation. In many ways, this divide has become familiar. But as the second Trump administration continues its project of overturning the post–World War II global order, the debates within each camp are at least as important as the disagreements between them.

If the party’s major foreign policy disagreements—on the future of the international order, on Israel, on artificial intelligence, on China, and on the global economy—are not litigated by prospective candidates, former officials, and prominent thinkers now, and voters are not given the chance to make an informed choice between coherent options, the first year of a Democratic administration could be consumed by indecision and infighting. Democrats must therefore understand where they stand on each of those questions, where centrists and progressives converge, and where the sharpest divisions now lie.

The three Democratic presidents since the end of the Cold War have all made some version of a U.S.-led international order a cornerstone of their foreign policy. But although former President Joe Biden’s rhetoric, with its emblematic “America Is Back” catchphrase, gestured at restoring that order after Trump, Biden did not govern like a restorationist. For the Biden administration, in which I served, the intensifying strategic competition with China and Russia and the onset of shared challenges such as the pandemic marked an external unraveling of the old era that necessitated major changes in U.S. strategy. Biden shifted foreign economic policy away from free trade and globalization toward industrial policy, resilience, and technological leadership, and he pushed back more assertively against Beijing and Moscow than did his Democratic predecessors.

As they reflect on the Biden administration, centrists are actively debating whether a future Democratic administration can restore and reform the U.S.-led international order or whether the United States must adapt to a prolonged period of geopolitical upheaval. For now, the differences are more pronounced in diagnosis than in policy prescriptions. All favor deepening alliances and partnerships, modernizing the military, fixing the defense industrial base, strengthening deterrence, tackling transnational challenges such as global public health, and pursuing hardheaded diplomacy with rivals.

Centrists also agree that Trump has severely weakened U.S. power and institutions, although they remain relatively optimistic about the country’s capacity to recover. But most recognize that the world the next Democratic president would inherit in 2029 is not the world that Biden inherited in 2021. Winning an election will not mean the end of Trumpism. The “America first” movement will likely remain a dominant force in the Republican Party, and the United States’ allies and partners will have to hedge against dramatic shifts in U.S. foreign policy from one election to the next. Even if a Democratic president can rebuild American power and repair damaged institutions, restoring international confidence in the durability of U.S. commitments will be much harder.

The party’s progressives argue that the U.S. foreign policy establishment failed over the past 25 years by pursuing U.S. primacy at all costs. They see the U.S.-led order as deeply flawed in its design and track record, arguing that Washington’s selective application of the “rules-based order” undermined the order itself. As Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, of New York, put it at the Munich Security Conference in 2026, “In a rules-based order, hypocrisy is vulnerability.” They favor a rules-based order that applies its principles consistently, even if doing so constrains the United States.

Many progressives, led by Bernie Sanders, the independent senator from Vermont, also believe that a global oligarchy poses a threat to democracy and see big business in defense, technology, and finance as part of a corrupt system. They believe that corporate interests have driven excessive military spending, misguided interventions, and a global economy that favors multinational companies over workers.

Progressives see the turmoil of the past two years as accelerating the emergence of a multipolar order, in which the United States has an important, but more limited, role to play. According to the progressive thinker Ben Rhodes, a former deputy national security adviser to President Barack Obama, Washington should “re-engage the world as something other than a hegemon.” Americans are accustomed to seeing the country as an overall force for good, even when it uses military power, but progressives point to U.S. support for Israel’s war in Gaza, and the Trump administration’s actions in Iran and Venezuela as evidence of the enormous damage that interventionism can cause. Many argue that the United States should go to war only in self-defense—and with congressional authorization, as the Constitution requires. They also favor cuts to what they see as a bloated defense budget that encourages presidents to default to military action instead of diplomacy.

Most progressives believe that the greatest threats to American security and prosperity are increasingly transnational rather than geopolitical: runaway artificial intelligence, climate change, and widening inequality, as enumerated by Sanders earlier this year. They argue that the threat from China is often overblown and that deterrence can be achieved and U.S. interests protected with existing capabilities and without a ratcheting up of tensions. As such, most progressives would prioritize cooperation with other powers, especially Beijing, on existential challenges over competition.

A distinct group of thinkers, usually aligned with the progressive camp, identifies with the restraint school of foreign policy. They tend to agree with the centrists about the centrality of geopolitics but want to reduce the United States’ exposure to it. They argue that Washington’s pursuit of military primacy has overstretched the country, unnecessarily embroiling it in conflicts and crises, and favor a more selective approach that focuses on a smaller number of vital interests, reduces overseas commitments, and emphasizes diplomacy, even if it leads to outcomes abroad that conflict with their values.

Despite these differing worldviews, there is much agreement between centrists and progressives. Both camps support NATO, the EU, and Washington’s alliances in the Indo-Pacific. Both want to stand up to Russian President Vladimir Putin and help Ukraine. Both oppose the Iran war and both want to root out corruption. But new debates, both between and within the various camps of the Democratic Party, are steadily emerging alongside those that have historically divided the party.

One of the most important debates currently unfolding in the party is the United States’ relationship with Israel and Washington’s role in the Middle East more generally. This is likely to be the most pronounced break from the Biden administration. Many Democrats blame Biden for not doing enough to restrain Israel in the Gaza war and some former senior Biden administration officials even acknowledge that they should have done more.

Many centrists identify Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu as the driving force behind a series of Israeli actions that have hurt U.S. interests, such as advocating all-out war against Iran, using excessive force in Gaza, and undermining the Palestinian Authority, and they recommend adjusting U.S. policy accordingly. They believe that Netanyahu’s departure could create an opening for a broader regional settlement, but that Washington would still need to exert pressure on Israel to make progress toward Palestinian statehood. The potential presidential candidate Rahm Emanuel, for instance, has proposed a regional initiative in which the United States would support a Palestinian state in exchange for full Arab recognition of Israel and closer strategic and economic ties across the region. Such an initiative would include forceful U.S. opposition to effective Israeli annexation of the West Bank, backed by sanctions on violent settlers and the entities that build or finance illegal settlements. Emanuel would also end U.S. defense subsidies for Israel, making it purchase weapons on the same terms as other allies.

Other centrists go further. Jon Finer, the former principal deputy national security adviser to Biden, has argued for more sustained pressure on Israel regardless of whether Netanyahu is replaced. The United States must make Israel choose “between the abundant and irreplaceable benefits of the U.S. relationship and the indefensible policies that are driving a wedge between our societies,” Finer wrote recently in The New York Times. This would include treating the occupied territories as different from Israel proper, imposing sanctions on individuals and entities involved in settlement expansion, and conditioning arms sales on Israeli compliance with international humanitarian law.

Few elected Democrats still argue for unconditional support, but a group that emphasizes the value of the U.S.-Israeli alliance and favors lower levels of pressure on Israel will likely remain by 2028. For instance, these centrists would sanction violent settlers, as the Biden administration did, but may not target the banks that finance the construction of new settlements. They would give a new prime minister the benefit of the doubt, should one successfully form a coalition. But if Netanyahu remains in power during the U.S. primary campaign, their position would be hard to sustain.

Many centrists aim to reduce the U.S. military commitment to the Middle East, but they reject the idea of simply walking away from the region. As Philip Gordon, the former national security adviser to Vice President Harris, put it in Foreign Affairs, the United States should “defend a narrower set of core interests with a smaller military footprint, more diplomacy, more alternatives, and more partners.” Centrists are divided over how exactly to do this; some would be open to a defense treaty with Saudi Arabia if it were part of a broader move toward a Palestinian state, whereas others want to avoid new defense commitments. A small group of centrist dissenters, especially those who are primarily concerned about China, see the Middle East as quicksand that stymies U.S. interests in Asia and are more willing to countenance a significant reduction of forces.

Progressives have largely welcomed centrists’ changes on Israel, even if many are skeptical that they would actually follow through on their rhetoric once in power. They tend to see Finer’s support for more sustained pressure on Israel and increased engagement with Palestinians as a good starting point. But many go further. Intraprogressive arguments focus on whether to support Israel’s access to defensive weaponry, such as the Iron Dome missile defense system and whether a Palestinian state should be recognized on day one of a Democratic administration or should be the ultimate goal of a diplomatic initiative. Others favor more radical change. The progressive commentator Peter Beinart, for example, has argued that a two-state solution is no longer viable and progressives should support a single state with equal rights for Palestinians and Jewish Israelis. Some progressives question whether Israel is an ally at all and believe it has little to offer the United States.

More generally, progressives increasingly favor a complete withdrawal of U.S. forces from the Middle East and distancing the United States from its Gulf allies. As Michael Fuchs, Harris’s former deputy chief of staff, wrote in Foreign Affairs, it is “long past time for the United States to withdraw its forces from the Middle East, stop arming governments that violate U.S. and international law, and end its role as guarantor of regional order.”

Israel may be the source of the most contentious division among Democrats, but it is not the only one. AI has also emerged as a source of disagreement, although the battle lines are not so neatly drawn. One group of influential centrists, which includes many Biden administration officials, sees AI as a technology whose benefits are as world-changing as its risks. They believe that the United States must win the race with China—often citing the cyber-capabilities of the Mythos model as an example—but also favor slowing down AI development to ensure that the models are safe and the economic effects, particularly on jobs, can be managed. To do this, they advocate strengthening U.S. export controls over China to provide the breathing room to “pace the frontier” and regulate leading labs. They want to negotiate a common position on AI with allies and partners (including by distributing AI infrastructure to the United States’ closest allies, such as Australia and Canada) and to open a dialogue with China to manage safety risks.

There were, however, significant disagreements within the Biden administration over how to compete technologically with China, which are likely to reemerge in the 2028 campaign. Biden’s Treasury Department questioned whether export controls could prevent China from catching up, arguing that maintaining Chinese dependence on American technology would preserve U.S. leverage and discourage Beijing from developing alternatives. Commerce Secretary Gina Raimondo shared this skepticism, telling The Wall Street Journal in December 2024 that “trying to hold China back is a fool’s errand,” and that investing in industrial capacity in the United States mattered more than export controls. Other centrist Democrats, such as former National Security Adviser Susan Rice, have argued that the United States and China should negotiate an agreement to freeze AI models in place.

Progressives, meanwhile, are generally unpersuaded that the United States can win, or even needs to win, the frontier race against China. A minority are skeptical of the technology, dismissing the claims of frontier labs as hype, and believe that it can largely be managed with existing legal frameworks. Others, however, believe that the United States should halt AI development, impose a moratorium on new data centers, take an aggressive stance toward the technology companies, and negotiate a grand bargain with China resembling a comprehensive arms control agreement. Sanders and Representative Ro Khanna, of California, have both called for a binding agreement with Beijing to restrain the development of potentially dangerous AI systems, explicitly drawing parallels with Cold War nuclear arms control. Progressive members of Congress have mostly voted in favor of export controls on China, although it is unclear whether they would renew and strengthen them if China were to threaten retaliatory escalation early in a new administration.

AI safety could become one of the most consequential issues in the primary, creating new coalitions that cut across the traditional centrist-progressive divide. The pace of AI development and the likelihood of future AI-driven cyberattacks or loss-of-control incidents mean that the issue will remain particularly fluid in the lead-up to 2028.

Several long-standing debates between and within Democratic factions, including on some of the most important issues that the next Democratic president will face, remain unresolved, too. Chief among them is China.

Most centrists see China as the most significant strategic and economic challenge facing the United States, believing that it has a technological and manufacturing lead in many critical areas. They worry about a war over Taiwan but believe it can be avoided if the United States restores its national power, deepens its alliances, strengthens Taiwan’s self-defense and resilience, selectively pushes back against China when necessary, and injects some stability into the relationship through high-level diplomacy—an approach that the Biden administration called “managed competition.” They are actively debating how to expand Biden-era efforts, particularly with respect to alliances and export controls, and how much the United States should dial up its competitive efforts without undermining diplomacy and risking a spiral into confrontation. Although most centrists favor some variant of Biden’s managed competition strategy, some associated with the camp, such as Ryan Hass, who served on Obama’s National Security Council, have cautioned that “attempting to restore or make minor updates to the status quo that existed before Trump’s second term—or to go even further in an effort to project ostensible strength—would be misguided.”

Progressives see centrists’ strategic competition frame as excessively hawkish and as a self-fulfilling prophecy. They believe that Washington and Beijing have a shared interest in tackling shared challenges, particularly AI safety and climate change and that détente is an achievable goal. Some, including Jessica Chen Weiss, a professor at Johns Hopkins University’s School for Advanced International Studies who served on the State Department’s Policy Planning staff during the Biden administration, think that Washington should pursue that goal by deepening economic ties with China, ceasing discussion of the strategic competition, and nurturing moderates in the United States and China. Most progressives do not recommend accommodating China’s geopolitical ambitions, but they believe that Washington can reduce the risk of Chinese aggression by exercising restraint and engaging in high-level diplomacy. For progressives, deterrence can be more readily accomplished than centrists believe, both because the United States’ existing military power is sufficient and because China is not an aggressive revisionist power like Russia.

The debate over China is closely linked to another debate over foreign economic policy and trade. Biden’s shift toward industrial policy and supply chain resilience was partly driven by a belief that China’s unfair trading practices merited a fundamental change in U.S. policy. Many centrists now argue for an expansion of industrial policy to cover a wider variety of critical goods, such as robotics. They want to make common cause with Europe to respond to Chinese overcapacity, including by coordinating tariffs and import bans on emerging technologies and by integrating their manufacturing efforts to compete with Beijing. Some, such as the former deputy national security adviser Daleep Singh, have advocated for a sovereign wealth fund to kick-start investment in new technologies. They are perturbed by Trump’s use of coercive economic power against allies and are developing their own doctrine of economic statecraft with appropriate safeguards. Some centrists want to grant allies and partners more access to the U.S. market, not through the expansive multilateral agreements that defined the 1990s and early 2000s but through smaller, more targeted agreements.

There are significant divisions among progressives over the economic threat from China. Elected officials on the left tend to see trade policy toward China very similarly to centrists. Lawmakers such as Khanna, Sanders, and Senator Elizabeth Warren, of Massachusetts, are also concerned about deindustrialization and China’s dominance of advanced manufacturing and favor stronger measures against Beijing’s unfair trade practices. But progressive foreign policy thinkers are often more skeptical of economic confrontation and favor closer ties with China. Some support industrial policy but are uneasy about framing it principally around competition with China. They recommend pursuing industrial policy for other reasons, such as tackling inequality or climate change, and believe that the United States and China can work together on global economic issues, including Sanders’s proposal for a global minimum wage.

Democrats are likely to disagree on what to do with Trump’s tariffs toward allies and partners, although few talk about it openly. Some say that the next president should describe Trump’s tariffs as an unfair tax on American consumers and lift them immediately and unilaterally. Others may favor keeping some of the tariffs to protect certain industries or as a revenue stream to fund their own domestic priorities. And a third group says that the next administration should lift them as part of a quick negotiation with allies on a common position on economic and technological issues relating to China, including dealing with the overcapacity problem and restoring export controls on China’s compute power.

Different perceptions of China also inform the party’s internecine debates on climate policy. Progressives see climate as one of the top priorities for a new administration, not subordinated to national security goals, and thus prioritize cooperating with China, including on electric vehicles and clean technology. Centrists, however, are skeptical that China will be a reliable climate partner, oppose Chinese electric vehicles on national security grounds, and favor cooperation with smaller groups of like-minded nations as a more dependable way to advance clean technologies.

This disagreement is just one dimension of a deeper split on how best to address climate change. Centrists tend to argue that Democrats should connect climate to affordability, jobs, and energy security rather than making it the organizing principle of the governance agenda. They favor investing in clean technology, although some are more open to natural gas and permitting changes that they say will speed construction. Progressives believe that supporting clean technology is necessary but insufficient and call for policies that reduce the supply of oil and gas.

The centrist-progressive debate runs deeper than a disagreement over policy. It is a question of how the United States can achieve peace and prosperity: by managing geopolitical rivalries and the instabilities stemming from the unraveling of the international order or by overcoming geopolitics through cooperation.

If the party does not have these debates openly and consistently over the next two years, a Democratic president risks entering office in 2029 much as Jimmy Carter did in 1977. Carter inherited an international environment in which the Nixon-Kissinger framework of détente was under increasing strain. His administration was divided between the competing worldviews of National Security Adviser Zbigniew Brzezinski, who favored a more confrontational approach to the Soviet Union, and Secretary of State Cyrus Vance, who emphasized diplomacy and arms control. Heated disagreements between the camps repeatedly complicated Carter’s efforts to develop and implement a coherent strategy toward Moscow.

A repeat of the situation in 2029 could be even more damaging. The next president will inherit frayed alliances, mounting Chinese pressure on Taiwan, deepening integration and cooperation among Washington’s adversaries, and potential wars in Iran and Ukraine, not to mention new crises that may emerge over the next two years. Failure to settle on a clear agenda could paralyze U.S. decision-making at precisely the time when a new administration will want to provide strong leadership and direction.

Fortunately, centrists and progressives have considerable common ground. They both are committed to rebuilding the foundations of American power, supporting alliances, backing a just peace in Ukraine, taking transnational challenges seriously, and eschewing unnecessary military interventions. They may not reach a partywide consensus on every issue. But a spirited, productive primary debate will allow voters to make an informed choice and ensure that the first year of the next administration is spent acting on a vision, not arguing over one.
