#5914. Rethinking the outage map for a multi-hazard world

For many, fall means sweater weather and all things pumpkin spice. For utilities, these months have other, less festive sides. Hurricane season, wildfire season and the start of the heating season all converge in the same 8-12-week window.

Hurricane season may be quieter this year, thanks to El Niño, but data shows climate change has intensified Atlantic hurricanes. Meanwhile, fall is peak wildfire season, particularly in California, when Santa Ana winds kick up. And the northern half of the country is switching furnaces back on after months of inactivity. Individually, these are known risks utilities plan for. Layered together, they're a bigger threat.

Because of this, utilities are starting to ask more of their outage maps, moving beyond "when will my power be back on?" to "what's happening around me?" Maps are becoming less of a status tracker and more of a situational awareness tool.

For decades, utility outage maps told customers when their power would be back on, along with repair status and outage cause. That’s still valuable, but outages aren’t the only thing causing sleepless nights. Hazards now stack and planned maintenance often lands in the same week and region as storms, wildfires and seasonal gas-related risk.

Historically, customers pieced this information together from disconnected sources like text alerts, county web pages, weather apps and wildfire trackers. Now, hazard layers bring it together in one place, drawing on GIS, OMS and weather feeds.

This shift isn’t just about the growing frequency and overlap of hazards. It also reflects rising customer expectations for self-service, with the map as their first stop before the call center.

During a crisis, utility customers want access to whichever channel responds fastest and most clearly. If the utility’s map can’t deliver, their next stop is usually the already strained call center.

Utilities say they want the same thing. Chartwell's 2024 benchmark survey found that 77% named reducing call center and crew impacts as a strategic objective and 93% named satisfying customers as a strategic objective. The stakes are real. Annual call center cost is $20-40 million for a typical two-million-customer utility fielding two to three million calls a year.

What does this look like in practice? During a major winter storm, Rappahannock Electric Cooperative added richer hazard context to its outage map, adding outage polygons to outline affected areas and damage photos to explain long restoration times. The result: 500,000+ views in two days and an average session time of approximately 12 minutes, well above the global benchmark of 2.5 minutes. This shows customers used the map as a primary source, not a quick check-in.

It wasn’t an outlier. During a major multi-state storm, outage maps across affected utilities drew 9.78 million views in three days. Mobile traffic climbed from 75% to 86%, as customers checked and rechecked maps from their phones. Every customer who self-serves on the map is one less person tying up phone lines.

Visibility upgrades only matter if everyone can benefit. Language, disability and access barriers mean some customers still get left behind in a crisis. Addressing these challenges means making map information clear and in actionable language across all channels.

Text, email and voice reach different populations in different ways. During Hurricane Idalia, opt-in data to alerts showed just that: text and email led at 45.3% and 40.3%, respectively, with voice trailing at 14.4%, underscoring the value of offering multiple channels, delivered in language customers can actually act on.

Channel reach is only half the equation; plain language is the other. Most utilities are still catching up. Very few have fully simplified their messaging or removed jargon. The message needs to be clear to work.

Uptime and map refresh rates still matter, but next-gen outage maps will be judged on more than restoration times. As storms, wildfires and gas risks pile up alongside planned maintenance, utilities need to keep every customer informed, not just report on uptime. That means investing in multiple communication channels. As the Idalia data shows, each channel reaches a different slice of the customer base and no single channel is enough alone.

Treating hazard visibility, communication equity and channel diversity as one problem, not three, is what builds trust.

#5911. Exelon eyes $8B in energy storage, in part to address PJM supply crunch

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

President and CEO Calvin Butler said the grid operator isn’t adding generation fast enough, but the utility’s plan to invest in energy storage and transmission can help address the issue.

Exelon utilities could spend $3 billion to $7 billion building 1.5 GW to 3.5 GW of regulated energy storage, not including a $1 billion, 500-MW project already proposed for New Jersey, company officials said Friday during an earnings call.

“With regulated storage, we have certainty that projects get built, and control over when and how they're deployed,” Robert Kleczynski, Exelon’s CFO, said during the call. “We view utility-owned storage as one option alongside competitive development, not a replacement for it.”

Customers would benefit from the projects through lower capacity prices and the flow-through of investment tax credits, which would ease the assets’ effect on electricity rates, he said.

Three states where Exelon utilities operate — New Jersey, Maryland and Delaware — allow them to own energy storage facilities to bolster grid resiliency, Kleczynski noted.

Atlantic City Electric’s 500-MW project, developed with Invenergy, would be the largest battery installation in the PJM Interconnection and is expected to provide more than $700 million in net benefits to customers, he said. Exelon expects the New Jersey Board of Public Utilities to make a decision on the proposal late in the first quarter next year, according to Kleczynski.

PJM isn’t moving fast enough to add generation onto its system, which is the biggest issue facing consumers in the grid operator’s Mid-Atlantic and Midwest footprint, according to Calvin Butler, Exelon president and CEO.

Two key PJM initiatives — its backstop capacity procurement plan and its Interim Resource Adequacy Service proposal — have been delayed by only partial approvals by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.

“Neither proposal would have solved the reliability and affordability challenges facing our customers, but both represented important steps forward,” Butler said.

However, adding energy storage and transmission can help address the issue, according to Butler. Exelon’s current capital expenditure plan calls for investing $16.3 billion on transmission through 2029, and the Chicago-based company sees opportunity for $12 billion to $17 billion in transmission spending beyond that plan, including on infrastructure for large loads.

Exelon’s “high-probability” large load additions remained flat at 11 GW — with 9 GW in Commonwealth Edison’s service area in northern Illinois and 2 GW in the Mid-Atlantic region, the company said.

Its future pipeline slipped to 23 GW — 14 GW with ComEd and 9 GW in the Mid-Atlantic — from 25 GW last quarter.

On the financial front, Exelon’s third-quarter income fell to $729 million, down from $875 million in the same period last year, partly due to higher writeoffs related to a rate case, and higher storm and interest expenses. Third-quarter revenue jumped to $7.3 billion from $6.7 billion in the year-ago period.

Not including one-time factors, third-quarter adjusted income increased to 87 cents/share from 86 cents/share in the third quarter in 2025.

In a note, Jefferies equity analyst on Friday said the earnings results and the potential spending on storage and transmission, in addition to Exelon’s $41.7 billion capital expenditure plan, were “favorable.”

However, the analysts warned that regulatory uncertainty in 2027 dampens the outlook and may offset any incremental earnings from new capital expenditures.

Exelon has close to 11 million customers through its six utility subsidiaries —  Atlantic City Electric,  Baltimore Gas and Electric,  ComEd,  Delmarva Power &  Light,  PECO Energy Co.  and Potomac Electric Power Co.

#5908. CPP Wind Engineering develops wind testing certification for utility-scale solar

CPP Wind Engineering, a global wind engineering consultancy for solar, is launching a wind certification program for utility-scale solar projects, which the organization believes is the first of its kind in the industry. The new program aims to improve the performance and durability of utility-scale solar over a range of wind conditions.

The program offers two services. Site Certification confirms that each project has properly assessed climate and site conditions, implemented an effective stow strategy, suitably positioned anemometers and used a qualified tracker or fixed-tilt system. System Qualification is a one-time review of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line’s wind engineering design data and methodology.

CPP’s new program is built from the organization’s 45 years of experience in wind engineering, including wind tunnel testing of solar projects, wind stow and siting studies and forensic investigation of wind damage to solar projects. Tornado risk falls outside the certification program and is assessed separately using a probabilistic risk approach.

“Wind governs how a solar power plant is built, yet there has been no consistent way to show that the wind loads behind a design were derived and applied correctly,” said David Banks, president of CPP Wind Engineering. “Most of the wind damage we investigate occurs at wind speeds sites see every year, and most of it can be engineered out. This program gives owners, manufacturers and their advisors an independent basis for confirming the reliability of wind engineering. Tornadoes, on the other hand, can’t be engineered out. We must predict the probability of loss for those.”

For project owners and developers, CPP’s Site Certification provides comparisions of mounting design options and a documented record of wind engineering diligence for financing, insurance and project sales. For manufacturers, CPP’s System Qualification evaluates the wind engineering basis of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line, providing a foundation for determining its suitability for site-specific wind conditions. For independent engineers, CPP serves as a partner, contributing wind engineering expertise to the due diligence they lead.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5905. Dominion sees $137M net income boost from rate increase

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Chairman, President and CEO Bob Blue said during a third-quarter earnings call that the company “benefited from better-than-budgeted sales” and “better-than-normal weather.”

Dominion Energy saw a 15% year-over-year rise in net income, including a $137 million increase associated with the rate increase its subsidiary Virginia utility received in 2025, the company announced during its Friday third-quarter earnings call.

Dominion Energy Virginia also saw a $55 million increase associated with weather, and a $112 million boost from rider equity returns.

Bob Blue, the company’s Chairman, President and CEO, said during the call that Dominion’s year-to-date operating earnings per share “benefited from better-than-budgeted sales, better-than-normal weather, and higher-than-expected 45Z credit income, combined with strong overall financial and operational execution.” 45Z is the Inflation Reduction Act’s technology-neutral clean fuel production tax credit.

Dominion installed 44 of 176 turbines at its Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project, representing more than 640 MW of generating capacity out of CVOW’s total anticipated 2.6 GW of nameplate capacity, Blue said during the call. He noted that installation of the 45th turbine was happening “now,” and that turbine installation “continues to average approximately two days.”

CVOW is around 85% complete, according to Dominion’s presentation slides, and construction is still on track to conclude by the end of 2027. To date, project investment totals $10.1 billion with $1.5 billion remaining in project costs – $800 million of which will be funded by Dominion.

As state and federal regulators weigh NextEra Energy’s proposal to acquire Dominion, Blue said during the call that he is “looking forward” to the Virginia State Corporation Commission’s first evidentiary hearing on the merger, scheduled for Nov. 17th.

In response to an analyst’s question about the 57 conditions that SCC staff proposed Dominion and NextEra commit to before the commission approve the merger, Blue said, “a chunk of them are conditions that we had already voluntarily proposed, so I think it's important to keep that in mind.”

“But fundamentally, as you heard from [CEO John Ketchum] on NextEra's call a week or so ago, we're still focused on the statutory standard, which is ‘no harm’,” he added. “That's what the judges are going to follow.” Dominion’s rebuttal to staff and respondent testimony is due Monday.

In response to another analyst question about the proposed merger conditions, Blue declined to go into detail, but said, “We're going to continue to present what we think is a very strong package through that regulatory process.”

Blue said that the company expects energy storage to “play an increasingly important role as use cases continue to broaden and the technology continues to advance,” and Dominion is “actively evaluating both short-duration battery storage and emerging long-duration technologies.”

“Looking longer term, the proposed combination with NextEra Energy would bring together significant capabilities and experience in energy storage,” he said.

The company’s third-quarter net income rose 15% from $1 billion in 2025 to $1.16 billion this year, according to an earnings release.  Dominion also took a $719 million impairment charge associated with its nonregulated renewable natural gas facilities.

Of the 54.9 GW of data center capacity that Dominion has in “various stages of contracting,” over 12 GW of capacity [is] contracted under electric service agreements,” Dominion Chief Financial Officer and Executive Vice President Steven Ridge said during the call. “To put that in context, we have added over 6 GW of contracts, or 13%, since the end of last year. In conclusion, I am highly confident in our ability to deliver on our financial plan.”

In its earnings announcement, Dominion noted that risks and uncertainties are associated with “increased energy demand or significant accelerated growth in demand due to new data centers, including the concentration of data centers primarily in Loudoun County, Va., and the ability to obtain regulatory approvals, environmental and other permits to construct new facilities in a timely manner.”

During the call, though, Ridge said the company continues to see “robust and durable demand from our differentiated, high-quality, low-risk data center customers across multiple localities in Virginia.”

#5903. Op-ed: Plug-in solar expands access to renewables, but needs a strong safety framework

By Michael Becker, Global Business Director, Energy Storage/Energy & Power, CSA | October 29, 2026

Access to plug-in solar is becoming increasingly available across North America. As more consumers explore technologies such as balcony solar and plug-in photovoltaic (PIPV) systems, these emerging solutions have the potential to make renewable energy more accessible and support broader participation in the energy transition. Solar remains one of the most accessible and promising renewable energy tools being added to the grid, and plug-in solar products may help expand that access to consumers who have traditionally faced economic barriers to participation.

As adoption grows, however, products designed to be convenient and accessible must still be treated as electrical products that interact with a building’s infrastructure. Product design, connectors, wiring, system integration and installation practices all play an important role in safety and performance. Expanding access and maintaining confidence in emerging technologies are complementary goals, and achieving both will require continued attention to the frameworks that support responsible deployment.

Clear safety standards, code-compliant installation practices and appropriate regulatory requirements will be essential to helping ensure that innovation can reach more consumers safely and responsibly. Solar-related issues are often associated not with the panels themselves, but with connectors, wiring and improper installation practices. A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet. Responsible deployment of PIPV systems depends on several complementary elements working together: products evaluated to appropriate safety standards, compliance with the National Electrical Code (NEC), qualified installation practices, and applicable state and local requirements.

No single element alone can provide the framework needed to support broad and responsible adoption.

An important emerging component of that framework is UL 3700, which is helping establish a product-safety benchmark specifically for plug-in photovoltaic systems. UL 3700 can provide manufacturers with a clearer benchmark for product development and evaluation, while giving regulators greater confidence as they consider how plug-in PV should be addressed within electrical codes and regulatory frameworks. Continued industry involvement in the development of UL 3700 will also be important as the standard evolves toward broader consensus and adoption. At the same time, UL 3700 is only one part of the overall approach. It does not replace NEC compliance, qualified installation practices or applicable state and local requirements that govern the deployment of electrical systems.

Emerging safety benchmarks are already moving from concept to practice. CSA has certified a microinverter to UL 3700, providing an early example of how the standard can be applied to products entering the market. The certification demonstrates how a consistent benchmark can be applied as new technologies enter the market, while helping support confidence among regulators, manufacturers and consumers as plug-in solar continues to develop.

The regulatory landscape for PIPV continues to evolve, with proposed changes to the NEC, state laws and local codes and ordinances aimed at facilitating the safe installation of these systems. As the industry works to establish appropriate requirements, keeping standards, codes and regulatory frameworks aligned with technological advancement will be critical to supporting the growth of PIPV products. Well-aligned requirements can help create a pathway for innovation while maintaining consistency in how emerging technologies are evaluated and deployed.

Technology itself continues to advance rapidly. Improvements in inverter technology are helping make solar more affordable while enabling greater integration of renewable energy into the grid. As inverters become more energy-dense and cost-effective, they can help lower the overall cost of PV systems and support broader adoption. New inverter capabilities can also provide valuable grid-support functions that help integrate more renewable resources and contribute to a more flexible and resilient electric grid. As these technologies evolve, standards, codes and regulatory requirements must continue evolving alongside them so that innovation and deployment can advance together.

Ultimately, greater accessibility brings greater responsibility. As plug-in solar technologies become easier for consumers to adopt, the safety frameworks that support them must keep pace with innovation. By developing appropriate product standards, maintaining NEC-compliant installation practices, utilizing qualified installers and advancing state and local requirements alongside technological progress, the industry can help expand access to renewable energy while maintaining the confidence of consumers, manufacturers, regulators installers and other stakeholders.

The statement “A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet.” is, in and of itself a misconception. If the plugin systems are safely designed, that is exactly what they are meant to prov ide the consumer–a solar panel with a plug they can connect to a household outlet. The fact that several millions of such systems have already been plugged in around the world without incident amply demonstrates their safety. All the hand-wringing about safety seems to be originating with existing vested interests that stand to loose significant revenues built on the existing over-complicated and over-regulated systems.

Helpful safety framing. One practical reader check is to separate product evaluation from deployment permission: UL 3700 does not replace NEC compliance, installation instructions, or state/local and utility rules, and effective dates vary by state. PluginForSolar.com maintains a U.S. state-by-state guide plus exact-system readiness checks so readers can verify those layers before buying.

#5901. Mitsubishi Electric seeks to expand North American grid foothold

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

In August, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions, whose platform manages about 60% of U.S. power generation.

On Friday, Mitsubishi Electric reported a 20% year-over-year revenue increase in its utility-facing business segment as it prepares to launch an electrical components joint venture with fellow industrial firm ABB and acquire an energy management software provider to boost its exposure to North American wholesale power markets.

On Aug. 20, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions. PCI supports utilities, independent power producers and other wholesale market participants in bulk power trading and settlement, generation and transmission operations, supply-demand planning and risk management, Mitsubishi Electric said.

About 60% of U.S. power generation is managed by PCI’s platform, according to Mitsubishi Electric.

The transaction, which is expected to close by the end of 2026, will help the combined company manage a North American bulk power system transformed by rising renewable energy generation, expanded use of distributed energy resources, greater power market volatility and a broad increase in electricity demand, Mitsubishi Electric said.

“These developments are increasing demand for advanced energy management and optimization solutions capable of supporting integrated forecasting, scheduling, trading, risk management, settlement and operational decision-making,” the company said.

Mitsubishi Electric’s Infrastructure segment houses most of the firm’s work for utilities and heavy power consumers. It took in 379.7 billion yen in the second quarter of the 2027 fiscal year, up from ¥316.4 billion in the second quarter of fiscal 2026, according to financial figures released Friday.

The company’s fiscal year runs from April 1 to March 31.

Both of the Infrastructure units serving utilities and large power consumers increased their revenue by double-digit percentages, with Public Utility Systems rising by 13% and Energy Systems by 18%. Together, they comprise about 70% of Infrastructure revenues, with the non-utility Defense and Space Systems unit accounting for the remainder.

Public Utility Systems manufactures and markets water treatment controls, communications systems and power supply equipment for large utility users. Energy Systems makes and sells power plant controls, substation equipment, electric transmission and distribution components, power electronics and power supply equipment.

The Public Utility Systems unit drew strength from “large-scale projects in the uninterruptible power supply business outside Japan,” while Energy Systems “saw robust demand …. from the expanded use of renewable energy and increased investments in data centers,” Mitsubishi Electric said.

Transmission and distribution equipment orders were especially robust, the company added.

Mitsubishi Electric’s just-announced partnership with ABB would create a jointly owned subsidiary to manufacture and distribute circuit breakers, electromagnetic switches, controls and uninterrupted power supply systems rated for 5 kilovolt-amperes or less, and to sell “certain high-voltage power distribution control equipment” and smart meters, the Japanese conglomerate said on Oct. 20.

“ABB holds a leading position in the power distribution and control field, and develops power distribution and control solutions by leveraging its technologies and global sales network developed over many years,” Mitsubishi Electric said, adding that rising electricity demand outside Japan necessitates a partner with deeper exposure to international markets.

Mitsubishi Electric expects the ABB transaction to close by October 2027.

In the first half of its 2027 fiscal year, Mitsubishi Electric earned 45% of its revenue in its home market of Japan, according to its second-quarter earnings presentation. Most of the remainder came from China, other parts of Asia, Europe and North America, with roughly equal shares for each, according to a chart provided by the company.

North America, home of Mitsubishi Electric’s Pennsylvania-based electric power products subsidiary, is the conglomerate’s slowest-growing major overseas market so far this fiscal year, according to company figures. North American revenue is pacing about 5% ahead of the same period in fiscal 2026, compared with about 19% for Europe and 34% for China. Japanese revenue is up about 10% so far in fiscal 2027.

#5898. PJM’s revised data center backstop procurement plan reduces utility risks: ICF

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The planned procurement could be for as little as 630 MW, down from an initial 6,800-MW target, after utilities in PJM’s footprint lowered their large-load forecasts, Jefferies analysts said.

The PJM Interconnection on Thursday filed a revised reliability backstop procurement, or RBP, proposal with federal regulators, with a goal of beginning the process on March 1 — about seven months later than originally planned.

The revisions come after the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission in late September rejected most of PJM’s initial proposal, which was designed to fill a capacity shortfall driven by data center development. FERC said in its decision that the original plan’s provisions for cost allocation, transmission owner exit rules and load-serving entity, or LSE, collateral requirements may have been unjust and unreasonable.

The proposed revisions were “carefully tailored” to address the issues identified by FERC while keeping the RBP’s fundamental structure and policy objectives intact, PJM said in its recent filing.

Under the new proposal, “PJM is using more current information on large-load growth to improve cost causation, revising the collateral methodology, and, importantly, recognizing that an RBP obligation and its associated collateral can move with the load-serving obligation when a large customer changes LSEs,” Maria Scheller, vice president of energy and planning at the consulting firm ICF International, said in an email to Utility Dive.

The proposal sharply reduces risks for providers of last resort and default service providers — utilities that provide power to customers that don’t buy electricity from power marketers, according to Scheller.

“If a large load initially relies on default service, the [provider of last resort] may temporarily carry significant RBP collateral and liquidity requirements,” she said. “The key credit question is whether state rules provide timely, certain recovery and adequate customer security, so the utility remains an administrative intermediary rather than taking meaningful balance-sheet or stranded-cost risk.”

As a result, the state role is especially important in the process’ outcome, and some state utility regulators may need to craft new rules, according to Scheller.

“Regulators may need to determine customer-specific allocation, establish appropriate security and cost-recovery rules, track changes in suppliers, and decide what happens if the underlying large load shrinks, defaults or disappears,” she said.

The proposal reduces the direct risk to residential and other existing customers, but it does not eliminate it, according to Scheller.

“Administrative costs, collateral and financing costs, regulatory lag, customer default and stranded-cost risk can still create indirect exposure if the state-level mechanisms do not keep the obligation tightly attached to the large load that caused it,” she said.

The planned procurement aims to fill a 6.8-GW capacity shortfall from PJM’s 2028/29 base residual auction.

However, the procurement may shrink significantly from the initial target after PJM recalculates how much capacity is needed, according to equity analysts with Jefferies.

PJM utilities, such as PPL Electric, FirstEnergy and AES Ohio, recently lowered their overall, near-term large load forecasts by 6.5 GW, the analysts said in an Oct. 28 note, citing recent filings to PJM’s Load Analysis Subcommittee. That reduction could reduce PJM’s 2027 load forecast, which would affect the grid operator’s backstop procurement target, they said. With that reduction, the procurement could be for as little as roughly 630 MW, according to the note.

The procurement could also shrink if data center developers enter into bilateral contracts with power suppliers.

Even so, analysis by Charles River Associates discussed Monday by the subcommittee found that PJM could face a roughly 11.5-GW shortfall by 2030. PJM hired CRA to provide an independent data center demand forecast.

Under the proposal, PJM will close the RBP bid window on March 28, with the final procurement target and supply amounts determined on April 28, the grid operator said Thursday. PJM would post the results on May 10.

#5896. What Is a Great Google Quality Real Estate Score: A Comprehensive Guide

Navigating the world of Google Ads can be challenging, especially in an industry as competitive as real estate. One term you've probably heard in relation to Google Ads is "Quality Score." This score is crucial to how your ads perform and how much they cost. But what does it mean for realtors, and what constitutes a great Google Quality Real Estate Score? Let's dive in.

Google Ads Quality Score is essentially a measurement of the quality and relevance of both your keywords and PPC ads. It is a dynamic metric, assigned on a scale of 1 to 10, with 10 being the best possible score. It is used by Google to determine your cost per click (CPC) and your ad rank in the ad auction process.

Quality Score is calculated based on three primary components: click-through rate (CTR), ad relevance, and landing page experience.

For any industry, including real estate, a good Quality Score is generally around 7 or above. However, in the competitive world of real estate, striving for an even higher Quality Score could give you an advantage. So, for realtors, a great Quality Score might be considered 8 or above.

That said, keep in mind that a high-Quality Score is not an end in itself. It's a sign that your ads are relevant, and your landing pages provide a good user experience, but it's still just one factor in the overall success of your Google Ads campaigns.

A high-Quality Score is beneficial for several reasons:

Improving your Quality Score is all about relevance and user experience. Here are some strategies:

A high Google Ads Quality Score can be a strong asset for realtors. It not only helps you rank higher and pay less for clicks but also ensures your ads are shown to the most relevant audiences. However, remember that Quality Score is just one piece of the puzzle. You should also consider other factors like your bidding strategy and overall account performance to create a successful Google Ads campaign.

With continuous effort and strategic planning, you can optimize your Quality Score and ultimately generate more leads for your real estate business.

#5894. PSEG Power plans 200-MW nuclear uprate as company eyes data center deals

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

But one of the units at the Salem plant in New Jersey, which is jointly owned with Constellation Energy, faces an extended outage to deal with degraded equipment, according to federal filings.

PSEG Power and Constellation Energy are planning a 200-MW uprate at their jointly owned Salem nuclear power plant in southern New Jersey, according to an earnings presentation by Public Service Enterprise Group, PSEG Power’s parent company.

Salem’s current installed capacity is 2,280 MW. PSEG Power would have a 112-MW share of the uprate and Constellation would own the remainder, which would support a recently announced nuclear-focused power purchase agreement with Google.

PSEG Power’s roughly 3,760-MW nuclear fleet ran at a 95.5% capacity factor in the third quarter, up from 92.4% in the same period last year, according to the presentation.

However, there has been a snag at the Salem plant, PSEG said Monday in its quarterly filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission.

During a scheduled refueling outage at Salem 1, a visual inspection last month revealed that the thermal shield inside the reactor vessel is degraded, according to the filing.

“We have initiated further testing, which is ongoing and is expected to extend the refueling outage,” PSEG said. “The impact of the outage on Salem 1 will depend on the timing and results of the inspection and necessary repairs.”

Meanwhile, PSEG continues to explore potential deals with data center developers centered around PSEG Power’s roughly 3.8-GW nuclear fleet, according to Ralph LaRossa, PSEG chair, president and CEO.

“PSEG Power is continuing to develop terms with large load entities for contracting arrangements involving existing nuclear production, future nuclear upgrades and other generation opportunities, similar to recently announced power purchase agreements in the PJM region and specifically in New Jersey,” he said during the earnings call.

FERC’s recent decision to reject key portions of the PJM Interconnection proposed plan for interconnection data centers and other large loads “reinforces that the focus has shifted to the states to address retail curtailment and cost issues associated with large load customers,” LaRossa said.

Public Service Electric and Gas Co., a PSEG utility, is working with the administration of New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill, D, and the state’s utility regulators to limit electric cost increases for consumers and consider new utility regulatory models, according to LaRossa.

PSE&G, for example, plans to ask the New Jersey Board of Public Utilities to approve a plan for recovering the costs for investments made since its last distribution rate case, LaRossa said.

“Our filing … is all about staying aligned with the governor's goals to keep rates flat for as long as possible,” he said. The utility expects to file the proposal by the end of the year, PSEG said in its SEC filing.

Meanwhile, the BPU’s study on modernizing New Jersey's electric utility business model is now in its second phase, LaRossa noted.

“This initiative is focusing on cost discipline, financing modernization, targeted incentives and shared savings and staged performance-based ratemaking,” he said.

Those and other options mentioned in the Phase 1 report, such as multi-year rate plans, performance-based rates, performance incentive metrics, earnings sharing mechanisms, decoupling and shared saving mechanisms, provide “constructive paths to strengthen the connection between customer value, cost accountability and timely recovery of prudent investment,” LaRossa said.

PSE&G may have opportunities to invest in solar and grid readiness for distributed resources, but the extent of it could hinge on the state allowing utilities to own generating assets, according to LaRossa.

“I think the policy is not yet certain here in New Jersey as to whether or not it will move in a regulated direction or stay solely competitive,” he said.

On the financial front, PSEG’s third-quarter income fell to $376 million, or 76 cents/share, down from $622 million, or $1.25/share a year ago, mainly on mark-to-market losses at PSEG Power, the Newark, New Jersey-based company said in its SEC filing.

Third-quarter revenue slipped to $3 billion from $3.2 billion in the same period last year.

#5892. DTE utility program funded a 132-MW Michigan solar project

DTE Energy’s 132-MW Fish Creek Solar Park near Sheridan, Michigan, is now operational. The $268 million development was fully funded by customers who voluntarily participate in DTE’s MIGreenPower program. The solar park can generate enough energy to power approximately 30,000 homes.

“Our customers expect us to keep the lights on and keep bills as affordable as possible, and bringing new generation like Fish Creek online is part of how we deliver on that commitment,” said Matt Paul, president and COO of DTE Electric. “Fish Creek adds more Michigan-made energy to our system while supporting local jobs, generating new tax revenue and delivering lasting benefits for the communities that helped make this project possible.”

Fish Creek will generate tax revenue, supporting regional infrastructure, such as schools, first responders, road maintenance and veteran and senior citizen services. The solar park also created hundreds of local jobs during construction and will provide participating landowners with supplemental income.

In addition, Bushnell and Evergreen townships each received $330,000 through Michigan’s Renewables Ready Communities Award, a grant available to local governments that host large-scale renewable energy projects. The townships plan to invest the funding in town hall and fire station renovations, along with other community improvements.

Fish Creek is part of DTE’s broader plan to meet Michigan’s growing energy needs with a mix of resources. DTE currently has 20 wind parks and 37 solar parks across Michigan, which together generate enough clean energy to power nearly 1 million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan includes an additional 15 GW of renewable energy by 2042.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5891. Energy affordability tops voter concerns in election set to shake up Congress

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, told Utility Dive.

The outcome of Tuesday’s midterm elections could have serious implications for utilities, as candidates in both parties seek to capitalize on widespread concerns about energy affordability.

Policy areas that could see the biggest impact include data centers, energy tax credits and permitting reform,  experts told Utility Dive.

Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, a finance technology company that connects tax credit buyers and sellers, said he believes energy affordability will be “determinative” in this election.

Polls have given Democrats a strong chance of taking control of the House and possibly also flipping the Senate, but even if they do, Nazar noted that there would still be a split government with a Republican administration in place. He hopes broad support for reform will lead to bipartisan cooperation on energy policy.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Nazar said.

All U.S. House seats are up for grabs, along with one-third of the Senate and 36 gubernatorial seats.

An Oct. 8 poll from Pew Research Center found that 74% of U.S. adults surveyed were concerned about the price of gasoline and energy, the largest share of concern among all surveyed issues.

As candidates campaigned this year, energy issues were central in many races.

Dylan Reed, Advanced Energy United’s managing director overseeing federal and political affairs, said Pennsylvania’s race is also significant given the role that Gov. Shapiro has played in the ongoing debate over the PJM Interconnection’s surging prices.

In 2025, Shapiro was one of the PJM state governors who threatened to pull out of the grid operator’s markets.

“Affordability is the message that we see out here very clearly,” Reed said. “I think when you are looking at high prices when it comes to your utility bill, the number one office that can impact that right now is the governor.”

Nazar said he thinks that following the election, in Congress “there’s probably going to be at least a discussion about new kinds of tax credit incentives that could be deployed.”

In 2022, then-Vice President Kamala Harris cast the tie-breaking vote in the Senate to pass the Inflation Reduction Act, which received no Republican votes and established long-horizon tax credits for clean energy along with new tax credit mechanisms like transferability.

In 2025, a Republican-controlled Congress passed the OBBBA, which shortened the horizon for some of the IRA’s clean energy benefits. Now, if Democrats take back control, they could seek to revise the OBBBA’s impacts, observers say.

“A flipped Congress where both houses are with the Democrats could have a dramatic turn on the energy policy landscape, and particularly with federal energy tax credits,” said Heather Cooper, a partner at the law firm McDermott Will & Schulte. “We have seen pretty dramatic flip-flopping in the last five years as Congress has gone back and forth.”

However, Cooper — who advises clients on federal income tax issues related to the energy transition — said she thinks the clean energy industry is prepared for the possibility of future flip-flopping.

“Most of the players who are in this market … The reality is all of their business models are kind of used to a world where these tax credits have 1, 2, 3, 4-year horizons,” she said.

The IRA, which created a longer runway for the first time, resulted in a “phenomenal amount of money poured into this industry,” she added.

Carl Fleming, global co-head of McDermott Will & Schulte’s energy and project finance group, said, “A number of folks have already worked out scenarios where some of these projects pencil out regardless of tax credits.”

“I do think you'll probably see an extension of the solar credit,” he said. “Solar took a good hit under OB3, but with rising energy costs, I think all that stuff's on the table because if I'm running for office or trying to get back into office, and I can find a technology that can help bring those energy costs down … whatever it takes to kind of get these energy costs down, that’s going to be on the table.”

Some utility executives have suggested that the furor over data centers and affordability could die down after the elections.

Fleming said he thinks there may be a more fundamental shift happening. He pointed to Texas’ pause on data center grid interconnections, aimed at weeding out speculative requests, as a potential policy model.

“You could question whether [the pause was] driven by the midterms or just normal processes, but I think that's going to be the way folks will look at this at the state level going forward — taking a kind of investment-type approach to these projects,” he said.

“It's no longer a race to power; it's a race to bankable power,” he continued. “Data centers are coming along so fast, much faster than people had anticipated. It's tough for legislation to keep up with.”

Reed said that the question of how to achieve speed to power, while preserving grid reliability and affordability, is what “every policymaker should be asking.”

Nazar said that post-election, he sees the chance for “durable” bipartisan legislation.

“I think it is actually quite an interesting opportunity next year to have a necessarily bipartisan approach to these policies, where energy is top of mind for voters,” he said. “I think the [Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act] is a good signal of potentially a return to cross-party substantive, serious and impactful energy policymaking that I could foresee carrying into next year.”

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act aims to speed federal permitting for energy and infrastructure projects, protect permits for approved projects and expand the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s power to eliminate obstacles to new transmission. It was announced in September, the result of careful negotiation between Republicans and Democrats.

But bill co-sponsor Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse, D-R.I., warned in October that the deal could be “[blown] up” by the Trump administration’s moves to revoke federal permits for offshore wind farms.

The bill has also been criticized by some consumer and clean energy advocates who say it would favor oil and gas projects, weaken environmental laws, undermine state and Tribal authority and limit the judiciary’s ability to intervene to prevent harm.

Nazar said he thinks the potential for BAAJA to be amended “seems like that's an ongoing conversation that appears to be in play.”

“That said, I think we will see how the dynamics shift after tomorrow, if they shift at all,” he added.

According to Crux’s conversations with developers, Nazar said, “Predictability is the most important thing they could ask for from the federal government.”

#5888. Can ‘BattMan’ secure the US battery manufacturing supply chain?

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Energy Department’s Battery Manufacturing Accelerator aims to plug gaps in domestic material and component supplies over the next few years as U.S. battery demand looks set to quadruple.

A secure, scaled-up domestic battery supply chain is critical for U.S. economic growth and security in the years ahead, DOE said in the white paper, “A National Strategy for American Leadership in Batteries.”

For utilities, batteries are increasingly indispensable for improving grid reliability and shifting load, particularly in regions where data center loads threaten to disrupt power flows, DOE said in the paper. Data centers themselves could account for one-quarter of total U.S. battery demand by 2030, according to a Benchmark Mineral Intelligence forecast cited in the paper.

The U.S. today is heavily dependent on external trade partners — particularly China — for battery materials and components, which “poses substantial supply chain risks,” DOE said in the paper.

Recent investments in U.S. battery cell and pack manufacturing have not been matched by investments in U.S. production of precursor components, creating gaps in the supply chain, DOE said.

“We have substantial work to do to secure the materials and manufacturing capabilities [lithium-ion batteries] require,” Robertson said in an email. “China currently controls between 60% and 100% of global production capacity across several critical stages of battery materials and component manufacturing … [which] represents a serious vulnerability for American industry and national security.”

China and other Asian countries host nearly all global lithium-ion battery cathode, anode and separator manufacturing and nearly 93% of electrolyte manufacturing, DOE said in 2024.

Robertson said the BattMan consortium, which has support from DOE’s Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation, would expand the Trump administration’s efforts to boost domestic sources of battery materials and components.

The Pacific Northwest National Laboratory leads the consortium, with participation from Argonne National Laboratory, Oak Ridge National Laboratory and the National Laboratory of the Rockies, formerly known as the National Renewable Energy Laboratory. BattMan’s research university partners include the University of Alabama, Arizona State University, Binghamton University, the University of Michigan–Dearborn and the University of Washington.

DOE says the consortium will advance three strategic objectives: establish a “mine-to-market” domestic supply chain by 2030; develop new battery technologies that are “more affordable, longer lasting, higher performing, and less reliant on critical minerals;” and scale deployment of advanced battery technologies in sectors critical to national security.

“We’re excited about technologies that use more abundant domestic materials, reduce dependence on foreign supply chains, and can be manufactured competitively here in the United States,” Robertson said in an email.

In August, DOE announced $500 million in funding for seven projects to expand U.S. critical minerals and materials processing, battery manufacturing and recycling projects. Award recipients include a California-based company expanding lithium-ion battery anode manufacturing with U.S.-sourced materials and another firm piloting battery production using ultra‑thin lithium‑metal films and “prelithiated” material.

The latter “could enable higher-energy, faster-charging batteries,” Robertson said.

According to PNNL, BattMan will work to overcome barriers that have kept promising, U.S.-developed energy storage technologies like these from reaching commercial scale.

The consortium will use a “cost-focused” research and development strategy that “[takes] performance, manufacturability and scalability into account up front;” broaden innovation beyond lithium-ion technologies using “platform technologies that are applicable to all energy storage chemistries;” modernize energy storage manufacturing using digital twins and other AI-enabled tools; and develop reliable, data-driven frameworks for validating emerging energy storage technologies, PNNL said.

#5887. Caterpillar expands production of engines and turbines to meet data center demand

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The heavy machinery and power equipment maker saw its order backlog reach $78 billion during the third quarter, almost double what it was a year ago.

Caterpillar delivered one of its strongest third quarters on record, powered by data center buildouts, as orders across power generation, construction and mining continued to grow, the company said last week.

“Our third quarter results reflect the durability of our end markets, with our highest sales quarter ever, strong order rates and a record backlog,” Caterpillar CEO Joseph Creed said in a Q3 earnings call Thursday morning.

The heavy machinery maker saw its backlog reach $78 billion, nearly double what it was a year ago. Caterpillar has taken steps to expand production of its large reciprocating engines and turbines, while increasing capacity, to meet surging demand for data center customers, Creed said.

“The team is effectively managing the operational challenges that come with this rapid growth,” he said.

Caterpillar’s power and energy division, which manufactures gas-powered engines and generators, led the way for the company during Q3. Sales totaled $8.8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year.

Most of the growth came from power generation sales, which increased 30% year over year and were fueled by data center customers, according to the report. Oil and gas sales also saw double-digit growth, driven by reciprocating engine aftermarket parts and engines used in gas compression applications.

The vast majority of external sales and revenue, approximately 82%, came from North America and the Europe-Middle East-Africa region. Sales declined in the Latin America and Asia-Pacific regions.

The segment turned a profit of $2.1 billion, up 31% from a year ago.

Caterpillar’s construction industries division, which manufactures bulldozers, excavators and other heavy machinery, saw higher sales across all of its geographic market regions during Q3.

Sales totaled nearly $8 billion, up 18% from the same period last year. North America led the way, comprising more than half of the company’s external sales, followed by the EMEA, Asia-Pacific and Latin America regions.

Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing costs, including the impact of higher freight and capacity expansion. Caterpillar last month said it will invest $1 billion to build a facility in Sanford, North Carolina, focused on compact construction equipment for small contractors and growing businesses.

The segment posted a profit of $1.8 billion, up 33% over last year.

Caterpillar’s resource industries division came in below expectations during Q3, in part due to mining shipment and commissioning delays, as well as lower locomotive deliveries, Creed said on the call.

Sales totaled $4.6 billion, up 15% from a year ago. However, Creed said sales to end users or retail customers increased just 4%. The vast majority of Caterpillar’s sales come from dealers and original equipment manufacturers.

The segment turned a profit of $622 million, up 14% over last year. Higher volumes and prices offset unfavorable manufacturing and material costs, including tariffs.

Caterpillar recognized $600 million in tariff costs during Q3, excluding International Emergency Economic Powers Act refunds that it claimed.

This was in line with last year but $200 million lower than what Caterpillar recorded in Q4 2025, CFO Kyle Epley said on the earnings call. Construction industries comprise about 50% of the tariffs total, while power and energy and resource industries each account for 25%.

Caterpillar expects “moderate sales growth” in Q4 over last year, as well as higher operating profit margins, according to Caterpillar’s earnings presentation. For the full year, the company forecasted “mid-to-high teens growth” versus 2025. It also expects full-year tariff costs to be about $2.2 billion, excluding IEEPA refunds, Epley said.

“We delivered another very strong quarter, with year-to-date financial results tracking toward another record year for Caterpillar,” he said. “Our end markets remain healthy with strong order rates and a growing backlog.”

#5885. The Senate permitting bill holds promise but needs amendments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Targeted changes can minimize the most negative consequences of the permitting bill’s overreaching provisions, writes former FERC Chairman Mark Christie.

Mark Christie is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and former chairman of the Virginia State Corporation Commission, and is the director of the William & Mary Law School Center for Energy Law and Policy.

Every true compromise in a polarized legislature contains candy for each side, but also requires each side to swallow some hairballs. In the Senate permitting reform bill (SB 5653), the true permitting reforms hold a welcome potential to get needed infrastructure built faster. The transmission provisions, however — which are not permitting reforms at all — contain some provisions that are indigestible if you care about rising consumer costs and respect for historic state authority over utilities.

The bill’s best transmission provisions seek to ensure that large-load customers pay the full costs they impose on the grid. The worst transmission provisions, however, can be fixed with the amendments described below. Before considering these amendments, it is important to understand that the Federal Power Act for nearly a century has recognized a clear and logical division between state and federal authority over the power grid: The states have authority over the siting and permitting of all physical assets within their borders, including generation and transmission. The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission has authority over the rates for interstate transmission services and wholesale power sales.

The current Senate bill blows up this century-old balance of authority by giving FERC the sweeping power to order a transmission line built in any state, without regard to the state’s own decision on the project or the opposition of the state’s residents who will be directly affected. Congress has enacted so-called “backstop siting authority” before, most recently in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act in 2021, but Congress strictly limited the geographic reach of FERC’s backstop authority to narrow geographic corridors established by the U.S. Department of Energy and only after DOE made a finding that the national interest required the establishment of each corridor.

The Senate bill contains no geographic limits on FERC’s permitting power. It expands FERC’s authority to override the states to every square inch of the country, a breathtaking expansion of federal power at the expense of a core state function: the permitting and construction of utility assets within their own borders, projects that could have huge impacts on the lives of their residents.

Fortunately, targeted amendments can minimize the most negative consequences of the bill’s overreaching provisions. Here are three recommended amendments:

The bill allows FERC to order the construction of transmission lines based on a nebulous “public interest” standard. Such a standard is a blank check for FERC to approve trillions of dollars’ worth of transmission lines for whatever reason a presidential administration finds is in the “public interest,” as long as FERC adds general findings of some reliability improvements or consumer benefits, which can be claimed for every transmission line ever proposed. In the short term, FERC could use the bill’s grant of new authority to order transmission lines built to serve hyperscale data center development, a Trump administration priority. A future FERC could find that the public interest requires trillions of dollars of new transmission to meet “Net Zero” climate goals.

So my first recommended amendment is to remove the “public interest” and other general criteria, and instead require that FERC must find that the proposed line is the optimal solution to meet a proven reliability need at just and reasonable rates, compared to alternative solutions.

My second amendment addresses the critical role of states in cost allocation. FERC Order 1920 guaranteed to states in a multi-state region the right to reach an agreement on cost allocation for regional transmission projects and have that agreement submitted to FERC. The Senate bill should add an explicit guarantee that for regional transmission projects the states in each region have the right to agree on cost allocation and FERC shall presume such state agreement is just and reasonable.

My third amendment concerns one of the most onerous and frankly insulting provisions in the Senate bill. When we at FERC implemented the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act’s backstop siting authority, we gave each affected state one full year to conduct its permitting proceeding before FERC would start its own backstop proceeding. That allowed the state to develop a factual record and issue a decision within a reasonable period of one year.

The Senate bill allows FERC to start its backstop proceeding simultaneously with the state proceeding. That effectively renders the state proceeding meaningless, and states will perceive it exactly that way. Why should a state spend its time and limited resources on its proceeding when FERC is already doing its own? Any evidentiary record developed in the state proceeding may not even be available to FERC before it overrides the state. This provision in the Senate bill is nakedly punitive and overreaching and should be amended to give the states one full year to act before FERC can begin its own backstop proceeding.

Even if you don’t care about respecting state authority, you should care about the bill’s impact on consumers already struggling to pay their power bills. Over the past decade, transmission has been one of the fastest-growing drivers of power bill increases. The Senate bill as drafted could result in literally trillions of dollars of new transmission being ordered by FERC to serve whatever policy goals a president wants. These costs will flow right into consumers’ monthly bills.

Every compromise offers candy and hairballs. With these amendments, the bill can be made a lot sweeter.

#5881. The Power of Schema Markup: A Comprehensive Guide for Real Estate Agents

The digital world has revolutionized how real estate is marketed and sold, placing a premium on strategic online presence and data-driven advertising. Among the myriad online tools available, Schema Markup stands out as an extremely potent weapon for real estate agents. This article offers a comprehensive look at how real estate agents can effectively use Schema Markup to bolster their online presence, increase organic reach, and drive sales.

Before discussing its application, let's first demystify what Schema Markup is. Schema Markup, also known as Structured Data, is a form of microdata. Once added to a webpage, it creates an enhanced description (commonly known as a rich snippet), which appears in search results. Developed by a collaborative team from Google, Yahoo!, Bing, and Yandex, Schema.org was launched in 2011 to create and support a common set of schemas for structured data markup on web pages.

To break it down simply, Schema Markup tells the search engine what your data means, not just what it says. This enhanced understanding allows search engines to present more detailed and accurate snippets of your site to users, increasing click-through rates (CTR), boosting organic search rankings, and potentially improving conversion rates.

Why Real Estate Agents Need Schema Markup

Schema Markup is incredibly beneficial to real estate professionals. The real estate industry is hyper-local, and search engine queries reflect this. Buyers and sellers are often searching with a specific location in mind. A real estate agent using Schema Markup can provide rich, location-based information directly in search results, making their listings more likely to be clicked on. It's an opportunity to outshine competitors and improve visibility in local search results.

Furthermore, Schema Markup helps real estate websites become more comprehensible to search engines. This enhanced understanding translates into more accurate categorization, increased visibility, and a better chance of appearing in relevant search results. In a landscape where digital prominence equates to business success, Schema Markup is no longer a luxury but a necessity for every real estate agent.

Implementing Schema Markup for Real Estate

Now that we understand why Schema Markup is crucial, let's look at how real estate agents can effectively implement it.

Adding Schema Markup to your website can be quite technical, especially for those without a background in web development. Here's a simplified step-by-step process:

The Power of Schema Markup for Real Estate Agents

In conclusion, Schema Markup offers a competitive edge for real estate agents willing to harness its potential. It can significantly enhance your search engine visibility, increase click-through rates, and ultimately contribute to driving more leads and sales. With patience and precision, implementing Schema Markup can provide an appreciable uptick in your digital marketing efforts and become an integral part of your online strategy.

#5879. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5875. EVs Sales Are Rising And Another Gasmobile Bites The Dust

The automotive press is practically aflame with rumors that Ford is replacing its Ford Escape crossover SUV with an electric version, also to be named Escape. That has raised quite a ripple across the esoteric field of car-naming. Normally when a model retires, so does the name. The US auto industry is littered with has-beens, wanna-be’s, never-was, and just plain exhausted cars that retired along with their names. Hmmm….perhaps Ford is sending a not so subtle signal about the future of EV sales in the US…

By now, everyone knows that EV sales cratered last fall, after US President Donald Trump and his Republican majority in Congress engineered the “OBBA” tax bill to eliminate federal tax credits for EVs.

Still, EV sales did not actually stop in their tracks, let alone reverse course. A fresh wave of off-lease, used EVs helped keep the momentum up earlier this year, and some automakers (looking at you, Subaru) stayed on track with plans for new EVs in the US.

Plug-in hybrids also factored in as a sort of gateway drug, with Audi CEO Gernot Döllner among those looking forward to strong EV sales in that category.

EV stakeholders must smell blood in the water as Election Day 2026 approaches. With polls indicating that  Trump will lose his chokehold on federal EV policy, news about EVs has been tumbling through the Intertubes over the past several weeks. Among the examples, Slate Auto opened its first showroom in Chicago, and General Motors unveiled its gen-4 EV platform.

Somewhat bizarrely, the Trump administration also contributed to the news cycle with the launch of the new “BattMan” battery collaboration earlier this week. Under the umbrella of the US Department of Energy, BattMan hooks up federal laboratories with academic and private sector partners to push the envelope on next-gen batteries for transportation applications as well as stationary energy storage.

Of course, Tesla also weighed in with its “flying car” stunt, but that was just a stunt. Over in the world of street legal EVs, Ford has been working the publicity machine to gin up interest in the forthcoming Fathom all-electric pickup truck, which is on track for volume production early next year at an MSRP starting at the affordability benchmark of $28,350 with a built-in inverter that cuts the cost of bidirectional charging, too.

The Fathom illustrates how much blood, sweat, and tears go into naming a new car. It’s a mid-sized pickup like the popular Maverick, but Ford went off in a new direction with the Fathom, considering hundreds of options over a months-long naming journey before settling on a maritime-adjacent reference to deep understanding.

The Fathom will be the first EV to roll off Ford’s new “Universal EV” manufacturing platform, and the automaker has indicated that the next vehicle will be a compact SUV. The consensus has settled on something resembling the Escape crossover SUV, all the more so because Ford has been laying plans to retire the gasmobile…but maybe, not the name.

That’s where the ears of the automotive press have pricked up, drawing attention to the Escape landing page that depicts the rear end of a forlorn, rain-splattered Escape heading off into the dustbin of automotive history along with its dual exhaust system.

And, here’s where things get interesting. The text on the landing page simply notes that the Ford Escape is “tapping the brakes,” which has been interpreted as a pausing message, not a forever message.

However, on the same page Ford provides a good reason why the 2026 Escape will not fly. The car is not for sale in California and several other states.

“The 2026 Ford Escape® is not certified for emissions requirements with the California Air Resources Board,” Ford states in a drop-down link. “Therefore, it is not available for purchase by customers intending to register their vehicle in states that have adopted California’s certification requirements for the 2026 model year, specifically California, Massachusetts, Oregon, New York, Washington, and Vermont.”

Ford goes on to suggest two other gas-powered SUVs that are street legal in California-compliant states, the 2026 Bronco Sport SUV and the 2026 Ford Explorer SUV.

So, is Ford really banking on the Escape brand to boost its EV sales in the US? Maybe! In August, the Detroit Free Press summarized a swirl of rumors around the idea of an electric Escape, citing insider sources including a Ford dealer with access to the company’s program codes.

However, in the same report, Reuters cites an inside source who indicated that in 2029, Ford plans to introduce a new entry-level SUV in hybrid and gasoline powertrains.

“At the Las Vegas meeting, the executives, the dealer said, assured dealers that the automaker is committed to hybrid powertrains going forward,” Reuters emphasized.

Hmmmm…so much for an all-electric escape. Back in May, the Canadian Ford dealer MSAFord also raised some questions about an all-electric replacement for the Escape in a blog post (breaks added for readabilty):

“Production of the Ford Escape and Lincoln Corsair ended at the Louisville Assembly plant last December, leaving a giant hole in The Blue Oval’s lineup as it pertains to small crossovers.”

“This is particularly true of the Escape, which disappeared at a time when new vehicle shoppers are increasingly looking for more affordable options, too.

“However, back in December 2022, a report surfaced from AutoForecast Solutions, claiming that the Ford Escape would live on as an all-electric crossover, though it has thus far remained unclear if that plan is intact ever since”

Readers, what do you think? Is half an EV better than none? Full EV sales have yet to surge, but plug-in hybrids have become more popular on the heels of US President Donald Trump’s witless decision to bomb Iran last February.

EREV (extended range EVs) are another halfway-there option that will factor into an uptick in EV sales. The Scout Motors branch of Volkswagen, for example, has stated that EREVs account for the majority of its reservations so far.

Then there’s the pure EV players, including robotaxi stakeholders alongside Slate and Rivian, which is forging ahead with plans to construct a new factory in Georgia.

As for industry leader Tesla, that’s a whole ‘nother can of worms. CEO Elon Musk has earned a fair share of bad publicity in recent years, as depicted in the new “Musk” documentary. However, the film wrapped sometime last year, leaving Musk’s latest hi-jinks for the sequel.

#5873. Electric Hydrofoils Save Energy, But Do They Earn Their Price?

Hydrofoils have an obvious appeal. Lift a boat’s hull clear of the water, reduce drag and move passengers faster while consuming less energy. I’ve written before about the proposed electric hydrofoil ferry between Vancouver, Bowen Island and Gibsons, where floating logs, coastal conditions and uncertain passenger demand make the economics difficult. Hydrofoils have been commercially available for decades, but their operating niche remains narrower than their technical appeal suggests.

A different opportunity emerged when Antwerp pilotage operator Brabo ordered an Artemis EF-12 electric hydrofoil pilot boat in August 2024. The vessel combines electric propulsion with submerged foils and active controls. Artemis has also demonstrated ship-contact manoeuvres using its EF-12 workboat platform, while the EF-12 Pilot design entered sea trials in 2026. The handling benefits are relevant to pilot transfers even when the boat isn’t operating at full speed.

The financial question is whether a hydrofoil can spend enough of its working life taking advantage of its design. Antwerp pilot boats work around locks, docks, ship movements and changing assignments. Reaching a high maximum speed is of limited value when the vessel spends much of its day manoeuvring or travelling through constrained waterways.

I tested that question using a Python-based operating model with synthetic assignments and three hypothetical sets of port-speed permissions. Depending on how much suitable high-speed water was available, the EF-12 spent zero, approximately 6% or approximately 13% of its underway time foilborne. These are modeled scenarios, not measurements of Brabo’s operations or actual Antwerp speed authorizations.

The resulting financial comparison was unflattering to the hydrofoil. In the central case, the EF-12’s modeled fifteen-year discounted cost was approximately €5.32 million, compared with €3.81 million for a conventional diesel tender, a premium of about 40%. Both figures include purchasing new vessels, rather than comparing a new hydrofoil with the marginal cost of keeping an existing diesel boat.

Electric propulsion still brought substantial energy benefits. The hydrofoil’s problem was that lower purchased energy did not compensate for the assumed capital cost and the limited opportunity to operate in its most efficient regime. Its active controls and potential ride-quality benefits remain relevant, but the available evidence did not justify assigning them a financial value.

An alternative electric approach also deserves consideration. Norway’s Moen Marin and Pascal Technologies have developed an air-cavity electric personnel vessel that reduces hull resistance without lifting most of the vessel above the water. It performed better financially than the hydrofoil in the exploratory comparison, although the existing craft was designed for aquaculture work and would require appropriate adaptation and certification before serving as a pilot tender.

The lesson resembles the one I drew from the Vancouver hydrofoil ferry proposal. Hydrofoils can be excellent machines, especially where sustained high-speed passages and suitable sea conditions allow them to exploit their advantages. In congested harbours, their additional complexity and capital cost need much stronger justification.

Different routes, operating permissions and vessel prices could produce different results. For commercial fleet decisions, the relevant benchmark is the work the vessel actually completes and the total cost of providing that service.
